# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum VIII

## Funding proposal package for FP260

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Enhancing the resilience of Serbian forests to ensure energy security while contributing to the livelihoods and carbon sequestration (FOREST Invest)";

b) Receiving letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or local point(s);

c) Environmental and social report disclosure;

d) Secretariat review;

e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

g) Gender documentation.

## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
* Accredited Entities are required to submit a revised proposal with the details provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all relevant sections of the proposal.
* The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
* The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

## Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed to the public in full upon submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities are advised to limit information on their proposal to what is required for the Board.

# Executive summary

## I. Context and climate vulnerability

The Republic of Serbia is recognized as a global hub of plant diversity, with more than 30% of its territory covered by forests. The country has a population of approximately 7 million people, representing about 40% of its population, as well as supporting rural livelihoods and ecosystem services. Climate change and excessive freshwater use are the main drivers of forest degradation in Serbia. Forest fires have become more frequent and devastating over time. Rising energy prices, due to the current regional conflicts, further worsen the situation by increasing the demand for wood.

## II. Key Challenges

### i. Damage caused by pests, diseases, fires, and climate hazards has led to a 260% increase in the total area of forest damage (1985-2017) compared with the period from 1945-1985.

### ii. The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events are increasing, leading to more frequent droughts and floods.

### iii. Forest fires have become more frequent and devastating over time.

### iv. The energy sector is a major contributor to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions (78% of total in 2015). The Agriculture, Forestry, and Land Use sector (AFOLU) contributed the second highest share with 14%. In the BAU scenario, AFOLU emissions are projected to increase by 4.5% in 2030 and 14.4% in 2050 compared to 2015.

### v. The more forests are damaged, the more total emissions will be released into the atmosphere.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 8

## B: PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### 1. Climate Change

#### 1.1. Context

- **Contact:** Climate Trends and Projections.
- **Serbia's topography** features a wide range of elevations, ranging from 17 meters above sea level (a.s.l.) in the plains of Vojvodina to 2,680 meters a.s.l. in the southern mountain ranges.
- Serbia is located between latitudes 43° and 47° N and longitudes 19° E and 25° E, bordering Hungary, Romania, Bulgaria, Macedonia, Montenegro, Bosnia-Herzegovina, Croatia, and Slovenia.

#### Climate

- The climate in Serbia is characterized as continental with four distinct seasons: spring, summer, autumn, and winter.
- Climate change has been observed since the 1980s. The average temperature increased by +0.4°C per decade (FAO, 2021). Greater increases were observed in altitudes above 1000 m and during the period from January to March. The average annual temperature increase was +0.6°C between 1980 and 2019 with a +0.4°C per decade (FAO, 2021).
- The number of days with temperatures above 35°C has increased by 7 days since the beginning of the century.
- The average duration of heat waves has increased from 6 to 8 days in the period between 1980 and 2019. The frequency of heat waves has also increased, with an average of 27 days per year (FAO, 2021).
- In recent years, there was a general trend towards increased rainfall variability, with +36mm/decade of accumulated precipitation.
- The average standard deviation in daily maximum temperatures is increasing by approximately 0.5°C per decade since the beginning of the century.

#### Projections

- **Future climate change** will affect Serbia even more in the future (Between 2020 and 2050, average temperatures are expected to increase by +0.29°C (RCP4.5) and +0.46°C (RCP8.5) each decade (FAO, 2021; TNC, 2020)).
- The months of June, July, August, September will be the most critical months regarding average temperature increase.
- Warming will be higher in July-August (+0.4°C and +0.7°C for RCP8.5) than in other months (TNC, 2020).
- The number of days with temperatures above 35°C is expected to increase by more than 15 between 2016–2059.
- The duration of heat waves will continue decreasing, especially at higher altitudes (+15 days RCP4.5 and +30 days RCP8.5). (Reportedly (TNC, 2020), the number of days with temperatures above 35°C is expected to increase by more than 15 between 2016–2059).
- The average standard deviation in daily maximum temperatures will continue increasing (+0.4°C per decade under the RCP8 scenario (FAO, 2021)). Similarly, the analysis of the Forest Aridity Index (FAI) confirms that climate change is expected to significantly increase between 2050 and 2099 under RCP8.5 scenarios by +0.42°C/mm per decade (ibid).

## Exposure and Susceptibility: The three national communications to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) focus on identifying agriculture and forestry as the most vulnerable subsectors in Serbia. These sectors are particularly exposed to climate change impacts, including increased temperatures, more frequent heatwaves, reduced rainfall, and changes in precipitation patterns. They also face challenges related to water availability, soil degradation, and biodiversity loss.

### Forests: Forests cover approximately 30% of the country, play a crucial role for the population due to their biodiversity, carbon sequestration capacity, and provision of ecosystem services such as clean air, water purification, and habitat. However, forests are also vulnerable to climate change impacts, including increased temperatures, more frequent heatwaves, reduced rainfall, and changes in precipitation patterns.

## and Productivity (LUPP) in the waste management sectors. The forestry sector contributed to CO2 removals for 8.4% of Serbia's emissions equivalent (GPA, 2019). At the same time, the forest sector decreased by 16% between 2010 and 2020 and is expected to decrease further in the coming years. The total forest area in Serbia has been decreasing since 2005 and is expected to increase by 4.5% by 2030 and by 14.4% by 2050 (compared to 2010) while the more forests will be damaged the more total net emissions will grow.

### B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram

#### Goal Statement: IF knowledge and technology, coupled with climate-sensitive forest investments, are made available to Serbia's private sector, public sector, and civil society actors, then carbon and water ecosystems in Serbia will have an enabling environment to foster cooperation between private and public actors to increase and sustainably manage forests, enhance energy security, and support sustainable development—particularly women and girls—while increasing carbon storage and advancing decarbonization within the agroforestry sector.

#### Assumptions: Forests in the Serbia have a vital role in ensuring energy security and other ecosystem services that are critical for climate change mitigation. The forest sector is one of the most important sectors in terms of water, land use, agriculture, and jobs providing a pivotal role in enhancing both low-carbon development of the economy and private sector investments. However, forests in Serbia are increasingly vulnerable to climate change (Annex 2 para B190). As outlined in Chapter B1 and further explained in Annexes 3-5, there is a need for new forest management strategies, policies that have forest ecosystems services (e.g., livelihoods, non-timber forest product NTFP) and carbon sequestration as the main objectives. These investments will be made possible by the introduction of a permanent national forest monitoring system (NFM) and to fully engage private forest owners and other stakeholders in the design and implementation of these interventions. Decisions have therefore been considered of primary importance and being guiding principles in the design of this GCF proposal.

#### 12. Environmental and climate finance in Serbia has largely focused on water management, energy, biodiversity. While these sectors are of great importance to the country and its adaptation path, there have been limited forest projects in the past that have addressed the role forests play in climate change mitigation. The project will contribute to the forestry-energy-decarbonization nexus. A complete list of ongoing projects and their complementsaries and synergies with this project is provided in Annex 6. These projects are designed to address the specific needs by combining existing local insights, expertise, and resources.

#### 13. The activities of the proposed intervention build on the initiative "Contribution of sustainable forest management to a low emission and resilient development in Serbia", financed by GEF5 and implemented by FAO. This initiative was successful in raising awareness about climate change mitigation through the introduction of new forest management practices, as well as in its implementation of, among others, the 2nd National Forest Inventory. The terminal evaluation of this project highlighted that the most important lessons learned were related to the importance of the introduction of a permanent national forest monitoring system (NFM) and to fully engage private forest owners and other stakeholders in the design and implementation of these interventions. Decisions have therefore been considered of primary importance and being guiding principles in the design of this GCF proposal.

14. Aligning with climate change impacts (section B1 and Annex 24), in the BAU scenario, fuelwood extraction and will remain the main anthropogenic driver of forest degradation, affecting the energy security, livelihoods and decarbonization character of forests. In addition to this, the BAU scenario also assumes that the transition from fossil fuels to renewable and sustainably managed forests constitutes a central and non-exchangeable element for the fulfillment of key adaptation, poverty alleviation, energy security and food security goals. Given the above, the nexus between forest-energy-livelihoods and decarbonization is critical. In this context, there is an urgent need to transition from BAU scenarios towards sustainable and maladaptation-scenario to one that is sustainable, integrated and climate adaptive, to allow the country to fulfill its climate change mitigation goals while ensuring energy security, poverty alleviation, food security and livelihoods for forest-dependent communities and incentives to increase private sector participation in forestry. The adoption of forests and their associated ecosystem services as a key element in national climate change adaptation strategies is critical to achieve these objectives.

15. Barriers: The main costs and barriers to sustainability are a) inadequate, effective and transparent governance of the forest sector; b) lack of capacity development for stakeholders (including private sector actors); c) limited forest stakeholders to guarantee key ecosystem services (energy, protection, livelihoods, among others). (ii) Incomplete legal framework and weak enforcement of laws and regulations; (iii) Lack of adequate financing mechanisms; (iv) Weak community engagement in SFM and decarbonization. Further information on how the project will address these barriers is provided below.

16. Main risks: The combined adverse effects of identified barriers (Table 2) impede the forestry sector from adding significant carbon to the national GHG inventory, while also increasing the overall economic impact that climate change has had on Serbia's economy. In addition, the BAU scenario identifies the following key barriers and risks: a) Lack of adequate adaptation and mitigation commitments. It would also further aggravate the overall economic impact that climate change has had on Serbia's economy. b) The lack of capacity development for stakeholders (including private sector actors); c) Weak governance perspectives; d) no action approach would also prevent rural communities and the poor from being energy secure and it would further increase their vulnerability to climate change impacts, including forest fires, droughts, floods and other natural disasters. In a BAU scenario, the LULUCF on the other hand, is expected to decrease annually on average by 0.3% (2017) to 1.6% (after 2081) with a temperature increase of 3°C [GWP, 2019: 2012]. In a BAU scenario the LULUCF is expected to decrease annually on average by 0.3% (2017) to 1.6% (after 2081) with a temperature increase of 3°C [GWP, 2019: 2012]. In such a scenario, the other economic sectors would have to further increase by 22% the overall GHG reduction efforts, mainly through energy efficiency and renewable energy. The total cost for achieving this level of mitigation in such a scenario will be approximately €3 billion (US$ 4.5 billion) per year. This is equivalent to 10% of Serbia's current carbon development pathways, which already correspond to 6-7 billion euros (US$ 8.5 billion).

The project will address the adaptation deficit of public stakeholders with limited actions focused on capacity development and infrastructure investments, and will not allow the application of CAS to forestry investments. Applying a traditional sectoral approach will have a positive

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 16

## Nexus approach: This approach will integrate all features of the sector approach, expanding its range of activities to focus on ecosystem services, including energy security, livelihoods, protection and decarbonization, applying an inclusive approach that includes private forest owners, local communities, agroforestry sector actors, and other stakeholders in forest management. Particularly, the participation of agroforest sector operators, local communities and private forest owners' participation is crucial for the success of this component. The objective of this component is to support the energy security-decarbonization nexus. The beneficiaries are described in more detail in section D2. Under this option, the project will support (i) households that have been displaced by climate change and/or natural disasters; (ii) smallholder producers (e.g., fuelwood and peat) and their associations; (iii) land owners who have halted farming their lands due to soil salinity or other environmental factors; (iv) private forest owners; (v) enterprises from the agricultural and other sectors in investing in the adaptation and decarbonization process; (vi) public and private financial institutions; and (vii) public institutions not directly involved in forestry (e.g., Water Authority, etc.). The project will support these actors to adapt their activities to climate change and/or natural disasters while allowing them to protect their crops. ## Co-Benefits

The co-benefit proposal will also contribute to generating several co-benefits. The main ones are: (a) Biodiversity conservation by promoting forestry activities following clear protocols aiming at restoring local forests in a climate-adaptive way; (b) Improved livelihoods through increased livelihood and rural job opportunities and enhancing rural energy security; and (c) Gender empowerment, as all activities will promote the participation of women. A detailed description of co-benefits is provided in Section D3.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

## B.3 Programme/project description

### OUTCOME 1 - National-level upscaling of sustainable and climate adaptive silviculture and carbon finance framework

24. Serbia recognizes that forest ecosystems are paramount in its economy. Forest health, richness and cover are prerequisites for the country’s economic development. However, there is a lack of reliable data on the status and dynamics of forests at national levels. There is also a lack of up-to-date information on laws, policies, and governance to ensure transparency and accountability in forest management and monitoring.

25. The project will implement a continuous national forest monitoring system (NFMS) based on the methodology developed by FAO. This will allow for an accurate approach to estimating dynamic variables such as change of forest area and growing stock and drain inundation areas, which are essential for climate change adaptation and mitigation, biodiversity conservation, and disaster risk protection. The project will also deliver regular trainings and support to field teams in data collection to ensure the quality of collected data.

26. Activity 1.1.1: Establishing the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS). Building on the methodology sampling design and preliminary results of the ongoing 2nd National Forest Inventory, the project will establish a continuous national forest monitoring system based on the FAO methodology. The NFMS will allow for an accurate approach to estimating dynamic variables such as change of forest area and growing stock and drain inundation areas, which are essential for climate change adaptation and mitigation, biodiversity conservation, and disaster risk protection. The project will also deliver regular trainings and support to field teams in data collection to ensure the quality of collected data.

27. Activity 1.1.2: Establishing a database on land categories and assessing land use changes on a rolling basis. This activity will be carried out by the Institute of Forestry in Belgrade, with technical assistance from FAO. The project will establish a database on land categories and assess land use changes on a rolling basis.

28. Activity 1.1.3: Establishing a continuous forest monitoring system for climate change adaptation and mitigation. This activity will be carried out by the Institute of Forestry in Belgrade, with technical assistance from FAO. The project will establish a continuous forest monitoring system for climate change adaptation and mitigation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

## Activity 1.2: Develop guidelines for decision-makers on AFGLU to prevent soil degradation.
The project will internationalize political policies to the Serbian context and develop guidelines for decision makers regarding AFGLU and its use in agriculture. The project will also provide training workshops for decision makers, farmers and other stakeholders on the developed guidelines on the related practices that can have significant benefits for climate change mitigation, food security and local economic development. These guidelines will be disseminated to a wider audience through a series of national and local workshops (GCF financing 20,750 USD; Co-financing FAO 105,100 USD).

## Activity 1.1.3: Create national standard for biomass production / handling for energy purposes.
The project will develop a national standard for the production and handling of AFGLU in order to ensure that it is produced with maximum economic value. This issue is mainly attributable to the lack of awareness among the population on how to select the best available fuelwood species, as well as the tendency to use wood or other biomass materials that are not suitable for energy purposes (e.g., sawdust, bark, etc.). The project will develop a national standard for AFGLU production and handling procedures that sellers and distributors of firewood must follow. The implementation will also enable the possibility on how to introduce a system of quality control and labeling of AFGLU in order to ensure its quality and safety. The project will provide the market with firewood with a maximum moisture content of 30%, thus increasing the energy output of purchased wood. This intervention will benefit urban households, which often purchase and utilize raw wood immediately after collection from forests or other areas where it is available. The standard will also ensure that AFGLU sellers are accountable for unethical practices and provide consumers with the necessary information to make informed decisions.

## Activity 1.1.4: Develop the national strategy, action plan and execution guidelines for Short Rotation Plantations (SRPs) in order to promote their use as an alternative to woodlands and plantations.
The project will develop a national strategy, action plan and execution guidelines for SRPs in order to promote their use as an alternative to woodlands and plantations. However, there is growing recognition that planted woody species are an important means for providing energy. Establishing plantations may also serve other purposes, such as land rehabilitation and erosion control, watershed protection, soil conservation, wildlife habitat creation, and the provision of timber products in urban or peri-urban systems where trees are planted in non-forest conditions. The current level of production of wood from plantations is very low (0.1% of total forest area). However, this figure could be increased to 5%, which would provide significant energy in certain parts of the country. The project will, therefore, develop a national strategy, action plan and execution guidelines for SRPs with the aim of increasing their use as an alternative to woodlands and plantations.

## Activity 1.1.6: Establishing a framework for national institutions to engage with carbon finance for AFGLU created.
The EU has committed itself to reducing its GHG emissions, in a signatory to the Paris Agreement, including part of its accession process to the EU. Forestry and agriculture are critical sectors to achieve these mitigation efforts with carbon forestry being one of the most important tools available to the sector. The project will support Serbia’s commitment to this sector and more broadly to its green agenda through national legislation and international treaties. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the Serbian Forest Code, which sets out a target for forest area by 2030 relative to 2010 through SFM and restorative approaches. Further in the agriculture sector, the LCDS includes provisions on the use of agroforestry systems as well as the promotion of afforestation and reforestation activities. The EU has recently adopted plans for the Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) which is imposed on imports from countries that do not have a functioning carbon pricing system. This mechanism, which will be implemented in 2023, will increase the cost of imported goods if they are produced with fossil fuels and will be applied to all sectors including agriculture. The LCDS also includes provisions for the use of fertilizers, but not covered by CBAM, will increase the urgency to decarbonize and introduce domestic carbon pricing.

As emerged from the option analysis (Annex 2 table 19), developing a domestic offset mechanism for forestry, and opening up the market for forest carbon credits in Serbia is an important step towards achieving Article 6 of the Paris Agreement. The project will support the development of a national framework that will enable Serbia to meet its goals in the NDC - and beyond – and Low Carbon Development Strategy. It will help the country to access international climate finance, including through the Green Climate Fund (GCF Financing: 415,414 USD; Co-financing GIZ USD).

eventually integrate with the EU ETS as well as comply better with the impending CBAM. The country currently does not have a well-defined carbon pricing framework overall. Stakeholder interviews reveal a knowledge gap around the LULUCF sector and its role in the national climate change strategy, which is reflected in the draft NDCs and the development of the ETS plan, as it evolves. This will include developing specific policy options for domestic offset mechanism for forest and soil carbon linked to Serbia’s evolving ETS plans. ### 3.2.10.4: Support access to carbon finance through capacity building

The project will provide technical inputs and augment national capacity on activities related to forestry and land restoration projects, including the development of a national forest inventory, which is currently in progress, as well as under the mandate of its National Council on Climate Change. The Council has an advisory role on Article 6, falling under two related areas of the Law on Climate Change: Related to Article 6 implementation of Plans and guidance on measures under Article 8.1.2.3. The project will provide technical inputs for Serbia’s sectoral action plan on carbon markets under Article 8.

Section I will formulate a set of guidelines and action plan for the forestry sector's access to carbon markets under Article 8. Section II will provide technical inputs for Serbia’s sectoral action plan on carbon markets under Article 6, as well as discussions. Third, it will provide technical inputs for Serbia’s Article 6 strategy in this sector that can be considered and included in the NDCs. Fourth, it will provide technical inputs to support the development of a national forest inventory (GCF financing: 100+207 USD; co-financing: 0 USD).

### 3.4.2.12: Access to public funds and policy knowledge sharing at the regional level (Serbia)

In the Western Balkans under the Sofia Declaration go beyond decarbonization and extend to deputation, the circular economy, and carbon markets. The region is a diverse group of countries with different levels of development in their countries in the region on carbon finance governance and mechanisms. Western Balkan countries are at different stages of defining their carbon governance frameworks and have accessed carbon finance to different extents. For example, Montenegro has been active in carbon markets since 2015, while Bosnia and Herzegovina is still in its early stages.

The Balkan countries – Serbia notwithstanding – need to accelerate their efforts to decarbonize through defining their offsetting mechanisms and developing the necessary capacity for carbon finance. The project will provide technical inputs to policymakers tackle similar issues in Serbia (and vice versa), drawing lessons from existing policies and experiences. The project will also support the development of a regional knowledge-sharing platform that can be used by governments, municipalities and private sector operators). This will allow Serbia to learn from neighboring countries and to further expand the scope of its carbon market. It is expected that this platform will be used not only by policymakers but also by a wider audience not only include policymakers from Ministries of Environment and Agriculture but also potential buyers of carbon offsets (e.g., companies, financial institutions) and other stakeholders in the sector. The project will also provide support to disseminate information around policy developments and technical guidelines.

## Key limitations of the project’s activities and the existing ones is that while the project focuses on a specific aspect of carbon markets – the AFCD sector – other projects including those on MRV focus on carbon markets broadly (GCF financing: $4,728,936 USD; co-financing: $0 USD).

### 3.5. Activate 1.2b: Enable in-sourcing as part of company decarbonization strategy support.

The project will reduce barriers such as lack of information and knowledge to facilitate in-sourcing at the level company. First, it will document policy options for companies to enable in-sourcing and provide guidance on how to implement them. Second, it will produce guidelines for companies on good practices for carbon pricing and decarbonization strategies. Third, it will organize a series of workshops with companies, consultants, and other consulting companies in the field of decarbonization on their technical expertise around in-sourcing. The support will target large companies (GCF financing: $14,750 USD; co-financing: $0 USD), using tools and systems for reporting and their cost implications.

### OUTCOME 2 - Improved energy security and livelihood from climate resilient forest ecosystems and GHG emission reduction through afforestation and reforestation

#### 36. Transfer technology and knowledge with on-the-job training to forest stakeholders is a precondition to secure long-term benefits of investments in the forestry sector, including private sector investments. As discussed further in the option analysis (table 20 of Annex B), addressing only a few steps of the process is unlikely to be sufficient for ensuring that the project will have a positive impact on the ability of Serbia to adapt to climate change, the needs of people and the health of ecosystems. Therefore, the project will work with Serbian stakeholders in order to ensure that they are able to make informed decisions about investments in forestry over the greatest extent possible. The project will support forest stakeholders with (i) technology and knowledge transfer to ensure adaptive afforestation investments, and (ii) tailored forest investments through afforestation and forest restoration.

#### OUTCOME 3 - Improved livelihoods from climate resilient agriculture

#### 37. The current production of seedlings in the two national public nurseries and seed centers is fully dedicated to routine natural regeneration activities and there are no resources available for the development of a new generation of seedling and is therefore unlikely to help contribute to the achievement of targets identified by Serbia in relation to forest expansion, resilience and contribution to energy security. While the country possesses a basic technical know-how, skilled staff and technology exist within the public sector, it lacks the capacity to develop new generations of seedlings that are adapted to climate change. The project will support the development of a new generation of seedlings by increasing the number of species with often limited survival rate after planting in the field, including single-species forests, with lower environmental impact and higher resilience.

#### 38. Activity 2.1.1: Upgrade public nurseries (Volodyčinac/Ljutiči). Serbia as the project aims to select species for forestry investments that are adapted to climate change and will be able to provide a wide range of ecosystem services, including seed center and the two state nurseries; as well further develop capacity of the operators. In order to at least 100 hectares of land in each nursery, with the aim of producing 35 million seedlings per year (GCF financing: $24,798 USD; co-financing: $0 USD). The project will also support the development of a new generation of seedlings by increasing the number of species with often limited survival rate after planting in the field, including single-species forests, with lower environmental impact and higher resilience. Serbia has to produce at least 33,000,000 containerized seeds; at least 1,500,000 bare-root seedlings; and at least 500,000 vegetative planting materials (cuttings), mainly poplar and willow cuttings.

#### Activity 2.1.2: Train and support operators of public and private nurseries in the production of diverse and climate-resilient tree species. Serbia has a number of public and private nurseries that produce seedlings for reforestation each day, (two in Kac and two in Požega/Ljutiči) on the production of high-quality plant material (seeds, seedlings and cuttings). The project will support the development of new generations of seedlings by increasing the number of species with often limited survival rate after planting in the field. The project will also support the training of all staff of PES and the Institute of Lowland Forests and Environment (LIFE) for the charge of the nursery works and the use of their equipment. The project will also support the development of new generations of seedlings by increasing the number of species with often limited survival rate after planting in the field, including single-species forests, with lower environmental impact and higher resilience.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 16

are rigid, not a major factor in climate change impacts and increasing forestry investments in an almost monoculture scenario. As a result, the planned forest interventions remain modest and similar across all management classes with one exception: the introduction of climate adaptive silviculture practices (CAS) to mitigate climate change. With CAS, the project will help existing forest communities to diversify their practices through appropriate restoration practices emphasizing spatial heterogeneity and diversity. It will increase the share of species that are adapted to climate change in the future. The project will also support the development of seedlings that are better prepared and hardened in a climate adaptive way, resulting in a low survival rate after planting and a higher exposure to pests and diseases. In addition, the project will provide training for forest managers on how to make informed decisions concerning the individual seed stands, but also ensure that, from seed production to the maintenance of forestry operations, all activities are carried out with climate change adaptation as an explicit objective (Activity 2.1: The project will cover: 1) climate adaptive nursery production including seed selection; 2) soil preparation for planting on extensive sites; 3) establishment and management of plantations on extensive sites).

### Activity 2.2.1: Provide guidelines on climate adaptive silviculture

The activity envisages developing four guidance documents to support forest managers in the smooth and swift transition to climate-adaptive silvicultural approaches. The four guidelines will cover the following:

* **Climate smart nursery production including seed selection**
* **Soil preparation for planting on extensive sites**
* **Establishment and management of plantations on extensive sites**

The development of these guidelines will be based on the application of guidelines developed by relevant institutions in regular forest management planning, transferring knowledge from other countries with experience in climate change adaptation to Serbia, and involving stakeholders involved in the sector, poor individuals and those at risk of poverty involved in the sector, and workers of forestry companies. Additional support for the development of these guidelines will be provided through study tours and training learning tours to countries with successful experiences in Forest and Landscape Restoration (FLR). Countries for the tour will include Ethiopia, Kenya, Tanzania, Uganda, Mozambique, Malawi, Zambia, Zimbabwe, and South Africa.

### Activity 2.2.2: Train public and private stakeholders in climate adaptive silviculture (CAS)

Through the collaboration with the Ministry of Education, Science and Technological Development of Serbia (MESTO), the Forest High School in Belgrade, the Faculty of Forestry at the University of Belgrade, the Faculty of Forestry at the University of Novi Sad, forest workers, and stakeholders in integrating sustainable and climate-adaptive approaches in their work, with a focus on forest management planning, nursery production, planting techniques, silvicultural practices, and other relevant approaches, and silvicultural practices. The activity will include study tours for private forest owners, prioritizing women and young people who are at risk of poverty involved in the sector, and training learning tours to countries with successful experiences (e.g., from NGOs/COs/Academia), and university and vocational schools professors involved in the sector. With the support of relevant institutions, the activity will also provide training for forest workers on climate adaptive silviculture approaches. These areas will ensure long-term sustainability in achieving national climate change adaptation and mitigation goals.

### Activity 2.2.3: Upgrade the National curriculum of the faculty of forestry and vocational schools working on forest management planning

The upgraded curriculum will be based on the guidelines developed by the project, but it will also be transferred to future generations of professional staff working in relevant sectors by reviewing national curricula related to forest management planning (e.g., Faculty of Forestry at the University of Belgrade, Faculty of Forestry at the University of Novi Sad and Sremska Mitrovica), agriculture (28 agricultural high schools) and accounting. The newly introduced practices and techniques will be included in the curriculum for all relevant subjects. Particular attention will be paid during project implementation to involve and empower younger professionals for sustainability. As part of the activity, the project will also provide training on climate adaptive silviculture approaches through the upgrading of the sustainability of the project's results, by involving into the future generation to apply sustainable and climate adaptive forest management planning practices in their work.

### Activity 2.2.4: Facilitate regional knowledge-sharing dialogue on CAS

The activity will support regional practitioners in sharing relevant data and information by bridging experts, institutions, and universities. Further, the

## Output 2.3: Public forest land restored and afforested in a climate adaptive and participatory manner

### 45. Afforestation of degraded forests (GCF funding: €9,682,659; co-financing: MoAFM/WB: €7,500,255)

- **Main aim**: To improve the framework for AFOLU investment in Serbia and the CAS capacities of Serbain stakeholders. The modalities for knowledge sharing will be defined by a communication specialist and will include workshops, seminars, training sessions, and as many as possible to existing coordination mechanisms (GCF funding: €9,682,659; co-financing: MoAF/WB).
- **Activities**:
  - **45.1**: Develop a national afforestation strategy for Serbia.
    - **Objectives**: To define the criteria and methodology for identifying potential areas for planting of seedlings on both state-owned forest land (about 17,400 ha) and undermanaged or not managed privately owned land (about 235,000 ha). The project will also identify areas where afforestation is not possible.
    - **Activities**: Define criteria for potential afforestation areas; define methodology for identification of potential afforestation areas; define criteria for selection of seedlings; and define criteria for use of land or pasture (Annex 16).
      - **Selection criteria**:
        - Clear forest cover
        - No significant human activity
        - Absence of land abandonment
        - Absence of land degradation
    - **Project outputs**: Define the national afforestation strategy; identify potential areas for afforestation; and define criteria for selection of seedlings.
  - **45.2**: Afforestation of degraded forests on public land in Serbia (based on publicly available land cadastre).
    - **Objectives**:
      - To provide a climate adaptive approach to the restoration of degraded forests
      - To ensure local engagement and finalize an inclusive afforestation/restoration plan.
    - **Activities**:
      - Define criteria for selection of seedlings; define methodology for identification of potential areas for planting; and define criteria for use of land or pasture (Annex 16).
      - Develop a national afforestation strategy for Serbia.
      - Ensure the logistics and execution of the needed maintenance and monitoring operations to carry out afforestation activities in accordance with the climate adaptive approach defined in component 45.1.
    - **Project outputs**: Define criteria for selection of seedlings; define methodology for identification of potential areas for planting; and define criteria for use of land or pasture (Annex 16).
      - **Selection criteria**:
        - Clear forest cover
        - No significant human activity
        - Absence of land abandonment
        - Absence of land degradation
    - **Project outputs**: Define the national afforestation strategy; identify potential areas for afforestation; and define criteria for selection of seedlings.
  - **45.3**: Carry out afforestation activities on public land in Serbia (based on publicly available land cadastre).
    - **Objectives**:
      - To address the issue of degraded coppice forests and forest management practices
      - To manage those forests in an efficient manner.
    - **Activities**:
      - Define criteria for selection of seedlings; define methodology for identification of potential areas for planting; and define criteria for use of land or pasture (Annex 16).
      - Develop a national afforestation strategy for Serbia.
      - Ensure the logistics and execution of the needed maintenance and monitoring operations to carry out afforestation activities in accordance with the climate adaptive approach defined in component 45.1.

### **Public (State or Municipal) ownership**
- The land is clearly defined from legal point of view and verified by third parties (e.g., NGOs, Municipalities, Companies). Potentially disputed plots will be excluded.
- Higher potential to enhance ecosystem services (disaster risk reduction/protection, water and erosion control, timber).
- Potential for enhancing carbon sequestration (afforestation/forest restoration and monitoring/monitoring tasks).
- Accessibility of the plot (reaching using access for standard 4x4 vehicles used for transport of seedlings and workers).
- Restricted accessibility (e.g., steep slopes, difficult terrain, etc. – reaching using access for standard 4x4 vehicles).
- Distance from human settlements (avoidance forestation works in the perimeter surrounding of the most peripheral communities).
- Availability of workforce within 25 km radius to ensure that local communities benefit from employment opportunities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

## Priority will be assigned to the possibility of establishing wildlife corridor(s) in newly established forests in Vojvodina

### Activity 2.23: Convert public degraded coppice stands into high forest.

The project will work with the MOAFM and other relevant public enterprises (PES) to pre-identify at least 33,000 ha of forests for public project interventions, the majority of which will be degraded coppice stands for conversion into high forest, but also forest stands damaged by fire or wind. The average age of these degraded coppice stands is around 50–70 years old and lack proper management, resulting in underutilization of their potential for wood production and other ecosystem services.

### OUTCOME 3 - Private sector is enabled to adopt afforestation and deconsolidation investments

The project will support the Serbian's establishment and deconsolidation efforts by enhancing the operationalization of the existing legal framework (e.g. Climate Change Law, 2021) and supporting the private sector engagement in forest management through capacity building and technical assistance. The project will also provide financial incentives tailored to climate action; take-up of these products remains low at the firm level due to low awareness and knowledge on how to access them. The project will support the development of a public-private partnership (PPP) model for co-financiers to provide Serbian companies with technical assistance to invest in best available technologies and approaches that address climate change.

### Output 3.1: Private sector contribution to climate resilience of forests enhanced through adaptive climate and diversified investments and greening of the biomass value chain initiatives

The project will support Serbia's efforts at forest adaptation, by building a new concept characterized by the complexity of holistically addressing the environmental, social and economic challenges of forest restoration in a climate change context. The scale of such interventions is limited in scope; it is necessary for call on an active involvement of non-state actors to reach the national forestry targets set out under Serbia's NDCs. The project will support the development of a public-private partnership (PPP) model on a voluntary basis and all applicable rights are protected.

The project will conduct awareness raising campaigns and establish a digital platform to collect and structure cadaster data and information on private forest owners interested in forestry investments. This will allow to cluster private forest land parcels to reach a minimum scope of five (5) hectares for forest coppice forests.


---
**Page 22**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WINDING-UP PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

Interventions will be carried out jointly with interventions in adjacent state-owned coppice forests. Thanks to the utilization of the digital cadaster, the PEIS will ensure direct contact with private forest owners and ensure that women and single-headed households are not excluded from the process.

The project is supported by the establishment of a technical partnership between the MOAFRM, the University of Belgrade and the University of Forestry. The technology partners will provide training for PEIS staff in terms of support and Annex 2, par.107 for Forest Sharing. The option to participate is outlined in table 21 of Annex C, concluded with the Ministry of Agriculture.

The choice of private forest owners as a partner is justified by the fact that they are the most efficient one in engaging the private sector in sustainable forestry activities and greening the value chain. The chosen approach will be based on the following principles: (i) the rehabilitation of degraded forests; (ii) the reforestation of abandoned land areas (approx 6% of agricultural land); and the rehabilitation of private coppice stands, which has unsuccessfully been attempted in the past.

## Activity 3.1.1: Convert degraded private coppice stands into high forest

The project will work with the MOAFRM to identify and select private forests for conversion from coppice to high forest. The selection criteria are based on interventions, namely to convert degraded coppice forests stands into high forest. Most private-owned coppice forests are primarily managed for timber production and have a low level of biodiversity. This approach is limited in terms of the quantity and quality. This limited approach also reduces the forest's ability to provide important ecosystem services, such as carbon storage.

The activity will initiate, every year from year 2 to year 10, the gradual conversion of an average of 3,500 ha of degraded coppice forests into high forest. The selection criteria are based on interventions, namely to convert degraded coppice forests stands into high forest. Through MOAFRM cofinancing is critical to ensure access to restoration sites and allow sustainable forestry investments (MOAFRM: 24,681,105 USD; co-financing: MOAFRM: 12,234,000 USD; PEIS: 239,400 USD). Selection criteria:

- Proven land ownership (disputed plots excluded)
- High vulnerability to climate change of target regions
- Forest area with at least 5 hectares to ensure efficiency of the investment and guarantee the best conditions for high forest growth
- Total forest area under ownership limited to no more than 100 ha
- Availability of a road network within a 25 km radius, ensuring local communities could benefit from employment opportunities
- Steepness of the slope (sites with slope average over 30 degrees may be excluded due to high cost of operation)
- Proximity to existing forest roads
- Land plots that had forest in the near past with a clear need for active restoration interventions (well-preserved deforested areas where there is no natural regeneration, or degraded areas), e.g. after forest fires, with no signs of natural regeneration
- Ensuring that areas of high biodiversity value remain untouched - confirmed by MEP.
- Land plots that had forest in the near past and are suitable for reforestation purposes such as short rotation plantations, agroforestry or soil rehabilitation purposes (Vojvodina). Regarding to the rehabilitation of unfarmed private lands through forestry investments the project will work with the regional government, responsible for Agricultural Land. The Grant Agreement will be concluded between MOAFRM and the PEIS. The PEIS will ensure direct contact with interested parties to identify at least 50 ha of unfarmed private lands to be cultivated with woody species for energy use.

The project will cluster private land parcels to reach a minimum scope in terms of area of cultivation with woody species. The project aims to establish a network of forested areas (forest corridors) and the rehabilitation of abandoned agricultural areas, together with woody species (including shrubs) (Annex 2, Appendix A). The concerned municipalities will confirm and map potential restoration sites for the purpose of land acquisition and cofinancing: MOAFRM: 2,311,400 USD. Selection criteria:

- Proven land ownership (disputed plots excluded)
- High vulnerability to climate change of target regions
- Forest area with at least 5 hectares to ensure efficiency of the investment and guarantee the best conditions for high forest growth
- Land plots that had forest in the near past with a clear need for active restoration interventions (well-preserved deforested areas where there is no natural regeneration, or degraded areas), e.g. after forest fires, with no signs of natural regeneration)
- Availability of a road network within a 25 km radius, ensuring local communities could benefit from employment opportunities
- Possibility to contribute to wildlife corridor(s) with newly established forests in Vojvodina region.

## Activity 1.3: Establish shelterbelts in agricultural landscapes (Vojvodina)

The output also fosters investments in shelterbelts.

### Output 3.1.3. Establish shelterbelts in agricultural landscapes (Vojvodina)
The project will work with the regional government's secretariat responsible for agricultural land, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, to establish shelterbelts on agricultural land that is at least 835 km² (equivalent to 500 ha) of agricultural lands for the establishment of shelterbelts, predominantly in almost treeless agricultural landscapes of the Vojvodina region with the main aim of soil protection (sediment erosion), controlling wind and water erosion, improving soil fertility, increasing biodiversity, and mitigating routes. (GCF financing: 773,158 USD; co-financing: MOAFMIP: 355,570 USD). Selection criteria:

- High vulnerability to climate change of target region
- Minimum distance between existing shelterbelts at least 5 hectares
- Area potentially protected from wind erosion
- Distance from human settlements and roads in the perimeter surrounding most peripheral houses) of at least 50 m

### Output 3.1.4: Engage private actors in sustainable production chains (Vojvodina region)

The project will engage private sector actors on the value chain for biomass residues from agricultural production, such as straw, stalks, and other by-products from crops like wheat, corn, soybeans, sorghum, or others already in place and can be reprocessed to support biomass production processes. The activity will enable the development of a platform that connects producers of biomass residues with private sector actors on the value chain for energy purposes.

### Output 3.1.5: Support platforms involving stakeholders of the forestry sector for a modern and transparent biomass value chain

The activity will provide technical assistance to the Serbian Biomass Association (SERBIO), which is responsible for developing a platform that connects producers of biomass residues with private sector actors on the value chain for energy purposes.

### Output 3.2: Mobilize private finance for agriculture businesses involved in decarbonization processes

The activities aim to increase agribusiness firms' capacities to assess the potential for decarbonization, to incorporate carbon accounting into their business models, and to identify opportunities for financing and facilitate linkages with financial institutions to meet their eventual funding requirements. The project will prioritize agribusinesses that are already involved in the production of biofuels (e.g., biodiesel, ethanol), biogas (e.g., anaerobic digestion, processing, and sawdust) interests in decarbonizing their processes, including through using wood waste to offset the emissions that cannot be avoided and/or engaging in meeting projects to climate proof their operations and reduce net emissions. The project will also support agribusinesses in assessing opportunities for incorporating carbon-based decarbonization in their activities and processes beyond the project life cycle.

### Output 3.2.1: Mobilize private finance for agriculture businesses involved in decarbonization processes

The activity aims to strengthen the network of stakeholders involved in decarbonization (e.g., ministries, regulatory bodies, financial institutions) and to identify opportunities for financing and facilitate linkages with financial institutions to meet their eventual funding requirements.

### B.4. Implementation arrangement

#### 64. Accredited Entity (AE): FAO has extensive experience in supporting countries in the field of climate change adaptation and mitigation, including through its work with developing countries for over four decades with International Finance Institutions (IFIs) such as the World Bank (WB), the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD), and the Global Environment Facility (GEF). With a portfolio exceeding $10 billion over USD 600 million, FAO has the experience and technical capacity to manage the requested grant and support Serbia in implementing the project. The proposed activities will be implemented across multiple sectors, including in SFM, biodiversity and wildlife protection, sustainable management of natural resources, land degradation, disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation. Activities include the capacity development of national institutions and stakeholders, policy formulation and implementation, technical assistance, financial management (including restoration, forest assessment and afforestation) and policy assessment.

#### 65. Following consultations with the MOFAW, other international NGOs, UN agencies, civil society and private sector representatives, FAO will be responsible for implementing the project in Serbia across all priority areas during the period 2019-2022. Building on FAO's experience in the country and its comparative advantages - and in the context of the EU's Common Agricultural Policy (CAP), environmental protection and standards.

#### 66. FAO will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Project. FAO will be responsible for: overall oversight of the project implementation; ensuring that all activities are implemented according to the approved work plan, including implementation of the Project; ii) Supervision of effective management of funds to achieve the results and objectives; iv) Quality assurance and monitoring of the project's progress through regular reporting on the status of the project as it is executed in compliance with GCF and FAO rules and regulations, policies, and procedures, including relevant requirements for planning, procurement, monitoring and evaluation, environment and social safeguards, and other project performance standards.

#### Table 4: Contribution of the Project to the FAO Country Programme Framework 2013-2025

| **Sector** | **Objectives** | **Main Contributing Activities** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **SFM** | Component 1: Restoration and degraded land and abandoned agricultural lands into forestry investments. | Training and capacity development. |
|  | Component 2: Enhancing the forest-based economy in public lands through SFM practices. | Training and capacity development. |
| Natural and natural resources are managed in a sustainable way | Component 3: Enhancing the forest-based economy in private lands through SFM practices. | Training and capacity development. |
| Economic and social benefits are provided through innovation | Component 4: Support to the decentralization of the agricultural sector. | Training and capacity development. |

#### 67. FAO will have the following responsibilities for the activities listed in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between FAO and the GCF. As an Accredited Entity (AE) of the Project, FAO's supervision role will be carried out through its Office at HQ (Rome), as well as through its Country Office (CO) in Serbia, the FAO Regional Office and Offices and desks at HQ/FAO Italy, such as the FAO Office of Climate, Biodiversity, and Forests (OCBF) and the FAO Division for Europe and Central Asia (ECA). The implementation of the project is also a responsibility of the FADG-FAO Project Task Force (PTT) composed with staff from REU, FAO Office in Serbia and HQ. The delegation of responsibilities within FAO will ensure that the Organization can independently and effectively perform the AE functions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 23

The project management and supervision function will remain independent of the Executing Entity functions. The Project Task Force (PTF) will be established as a management and consultative body with a Formulator/Budget Holder (BH), a Lead Implementing Agency (LIA), and other stakeholders.

## Government Line Agencies
The project will include several GIs/line Ministries. Thus, the MOAFWIM, the MEP, the Ministry of Finance (MoF), the MESTD and its affiliated departments at the Government level will be involved in the implementation of the project activities. The MoF will provide financial support to the project with the facilitation of activities in the provinces. The MEP, together with the MoF will participate in the activities connected with the development of the national energy strategy and the preparation of the National Energy Sector Development Plan (NESP) and facilitate role during project implementation and identify opportunities for building synergies with other projects dealing with renewable energy sources and climate change adaptation. The MESTD will be involved in the areas of Water and Energy (IME), in particular on all activities in support of the biomass energy value chain. The participation and contribution of these line agencies will be essential to ensure a strong country ownership.

## Project Steering Committee
The Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be established for the overall strategic guidance of the project and housed at the MOAFWIM. The MOAFWIM as the NDA or GCF in Serbia will notify the formation of the PSC and chair and convene its meetings. The PSC will consist of representatives from the line agencies, including the Ministry of Finance, Forest Owners Guild, FAO (Observer) and Civil Society (Observer). The role of the PSC will be to: (i) Provide oversight on project implementation and ensure that the project is aligned with national priorities; (ii) Monitor progress and report against its availability and use; (iii) Address project issues as raised by the Project Management Unit (PMU) under the supervision of the NDA; (iv) Review and approve project deliverables, including the annual work plan, project reports, and other documents; (v) Make recommendations for subsequent work plans to build on achievements and address any shortcomings, etc. One representative from the PMU will act as Rapporteur to the PSC that should ensure through its overall leading and central role a strong country ownership.

# B.S. Justification for GCF funding request

74. In 2022, Serbia’s public debt accounted for more than 60% of its GDP (IMF, 2022; latest available date). The government launched several programs to address structural weaknesses, increase public sector efficiency, and eliminate budgetary imbalances. These efforts have been complemented by the European Union’s financial support in Serbia to support post-COVID-19 economic recovery efforts while addressing a shrinking population, labor shortages, and climate change challenges. The country has also received significant international attention for its ambitious climate action plans including for renewable energy, energy efficiency, sustainable water and wastewater management, pollution prevention and control, and waste reduction. Serbia’s National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) was adopted in 2019 and has been oversubscribed three times as of December 2022, and it has helped to fund 34 projects on environmental protection, biodiversity conservation, and climate change adaptation. The country is also a signatory to the Paris Agreement and is committed to its decarbonization process if financial support is provided for key investments. Furthermore, the country estimates the need for additional investment of USD 10 billion over the next decade in order to achieve its NDC targets. Serbia has a strong track record for investing in forestry, agriculture, and agrofood sectors. The performance of the EUR 1 billion green bond shows an overall positive impact on the environment and economy. However, the share of investments in the energy sector (including renewable energy) does not pass 5% of the total allocation. The project is proposing support to investment in the forestry sector sub-objective, therefore, involving both public and private stakeholders. Due to their remoteness and limited access to markets, forest owners are often unable to benefit from market prices for timber and other forest products due to higher concessionality (cf. Annex 3: narrative). Furthermore, energy efficient projects (2% of the total share of processes of green bond) have not been prioritized in Serbia’s NCCS. The project is proposing support to investment in energy efficiency and decarbonization investment in the agriculture and agrofood sector, representing a large untapped potential for overall reduction of greenhouse gas emissions.

75. While the country is currently engaged in forest investments and substantially cofinancing both the adaptation and mitigation components of its NDCs, it does not have sufficient resources to cover additional costs of CC adaptation and support development of its NDCs. The project is proposing support to investment in energy efficiency and decarbonization investment in the agriculture and agrofood sector and cover the added cost needed to guarantee the paradigm shift that will transform Serbian forests in the main adaptation and mitigation engine of the country (the project investment is equivalent to about USD 1.8m, from output 4.1.2). The project is also proposing support for capacity building activities aimed at increasing Serbia’s ability to engage in forestry and decarbonization process. As reported in Annex 2, appendix 12 and supporting letters (Annex 13) the country has not yet developed a national strategy on energy efficiency and decarbonization of agriculture and agrofood sector which misses the capacity to define the investment needed. Therefore, as reported in both its NDCs, to ensure that expected

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 26

results are required to contribute to identified goals, the country requires international technical and financial assistance. As reported in the updated NDC (Section 3), Serbia will reduce its GHG emissions by 37% by 2030 (compared to 1990 levels). The government has set a target of reducing CO2 emissions from energy sector by 45% by 2030, with forests as a priority area in the updated NDC (Section [3]), and the country plans to invest over USD 5 million annually into the sector, including a majority of its budgetary budget over the next 7 years toward the targets of FOREST-FIRE proposal. Therefore, Serbia is seeking financial support for the implementation of the FOREST-FIRE proposal, which includes forest decarbonization related to forests (decarbonization governance), which pulls the overall government investment in forestry sector and contributes to reducing CO2 emissions from energy sector by 45% by 2030. The private sector has committed and committed funding to fill this gap on its own, by injecting initial concessional financing into the forest sector through the Forest Fund (FF) and by providing guarantees for the loans of the FF. This will help Serbia to reduce CO2 emissions from deforestation and degradation in forests and decarbonize its economy. The public sector is the only major financier of Serbia's forests, and while the government has announced a budgetary allocation of USD 5 million annually over the next seven years toward the targets of FOREST-FIRE proposal, it will not be enough to cover the costs of forest decarbonization. Therefore, Serbia needs concessional financing from the public sector (to mobilize the private sector) to improve carbon storage in forests and national capacities to manage them, to contribute reducing emissions from deforestation and degradation in forests, and to trigger investments in the forestry sector through strategic investment.

The concessional financing is not adequate given the magnitude of challenges. For instance, the European Union and International Financial Institutions (IFIs) support Serbia with some financial instruments for Pre-Accession Assistance to support energy efficiency and renewable energy. However, this does not adequately help Serbia achieve its targets.78. The forest sector in Serbia is highly fragmented with limited investment capacity. Out of the over 200 companies involved in forestry (mostly in wood processing), about 61% are microenterprises with limited human capital and access to finance. This makes it difficult for them to invest in forest decarbonization, which is a long-term process with limited scale emanating from their small plots. Beyond the public budget, there are limited resources to address these challenges. The private sector has committed and committed funding to fill this gap on its own, by injecting initial concessional financing into the forest sector through the Forest Fund (FF) and by providing guarantees for the loans of the FF. This will help Serbia to reduce CO2 emissions from deforestation and degradation in forests and decarbonize its economy. The public sector is the only major financier of Serbia's forests, and while the government has announced a budgetary allocation of USD 5 million annually over the next seven years toward the targets of FOREST-FIRE proposal, it will not be enough to cover the costs of forest decarbonization. Therefore, Serbia needs concessional financing from the public sector (to mobilize the private sector) to improve carbon storage in forests and national capacities to manage them, to contribute reducing emissions from deforestation and degradation in forests, and to trigger investments in the forestry sector through strategic investment.

The proposed budget reflects the needs of the state to increase its forest cover while adopting sustainable and climate adaptive management practices. The proposed budget also reflects the need for additional concessional financing to support private sector participation in decarbonizing the economy. Although Serbia is a middle-income country, the magnitude of funding required for this project is significant.

In the short term, concessional financing into the project will help enhance the energy security of the rural poor and set Serbia on a more sustainable forestry-energy-decarbonization path. In the longer term – because Serbia is a forest-rich country with significant potential to sequester carbon in forests – it will also contribute to reducing CO2 emissions from deforestation and degradation in forests.

In the short and medium term, concessional financing in forests is critical to ensure continued energy security for Serbia. Forests account for a significant source of energy (11.86%) for the population. Concessions in rural areas as well as the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WEBSITE PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 27

## Most vulnerable countries are the primary users of firewood as a source of energy, and due to the more limited economic opportunities, they are most affected by fluctuation or spikes in energy costs. Nearly half of the lowest income decile households in Sub-Saharan Africa rely on firewood for cooking and heating, and many of these households are unable to afford it and not able to absorb rising energy prices.1 The present geopolitical crisis makes a strong case for immediate investment in forest and decarbonization efforts more broadly to ensure that rural populations do not get priced out of using firewood as the shock waves from the crisis continue to reverberate through global markets, while also avoiding further deforestation compromising the provision of other paramount ecosystem services (e.g. carbon removals, protection, NPPW and others). Simultaneously, there is a strong case for immediate investment in forest and decarbonization efforts more broadly to support investments in decarbonization through energy savings and optimization.

## The magnitude of financing required is larger than the size of existing and committed financing, making it necessary that Serbia mobilizes additional resources from both domestic and international sources. In addition to the government, providing initial concessional financing to unlock broader investments, and external financiers, including the private sector, are needed to support the project. The Serbian government has already demonstrated its commitment to support energy efficiency and renewable energy. However, these are not enough to help Serbia achieve its targets.

## Concessional financing through this project, in addition to Serbia's positive and stable economic outlook,2 will help attract more international investors to Serbia.3 This is confirmed by the recently agreed increase in co-financing from the state to a level of 50% for projects under the EU’s EBRD-ESF Joint Venture Facility (JVF).4 The Serbian government has also been working with the OECD Credit Reporting System, Serbia's forestry sector only received USD 441,000 in official development assistance (ODA) between 2015 and 2019.5 This is a very small amount of financing compared to the total volume of private sector financing (concessional financing, public funding and ODA) which will de-comprise private investment envisaged in this project.

## The Serbian government and the main stakeholders involved in the project not only confirm their support but also their willingness to provide additional resources.6 In addition, several international financial institutions and development banks in Serbia have also expressed their interest in contributing to the project's activities (see Annex 1). Banks indicate that they are willing to provide concessional financing at a lower cost than market rates, which will reduce the overall cost of financing and technical assistance in reducing information barriers and regulatory uncertainty. This will bring to the project some very important leveraged private finance that is expected to be around USD 50 million.

#### 82. An increased adoption and decarbonization agenda is necessary for Serbia to continue doing business with the EU and other markets where decarbonization policies are increasingly more stringent toward products that are high in terms of carbon emissions. The project will contribute to this by supporting the development of a forest sector strategy where it targets a climate neutral and resilient climate economy. Each project activity is tailored to support national strategies and programs, including the National Forest Strategy, which aims at increasing the share of renewable energy sources, while also potential and export capacity. The project will not add to the already existing or planned processes and infrastructure.

#### 83. Sustainability and replicability of the project has been considered in the design through the following: (i) processes and mechanisms introduced will be self-funded and will optimize actors and national experiences in the forestry sector, including private landowners, municipalities, local administration, and other stakeholders; (ii) the project will progressively intensify and optimizes its investments in forests and decarbonization; (iii) the introduction of new forest management practices will reduce the cost of operations over time by increasing productivity and efficiency while at the same time, decrease the overall cost of operations, supporting the country in ensuring the adaptation of the forestry sector to climate change. The project will also support the development of a national strategy for sustainable forest governance strategies which enhance the role and contribution of the private sector and its associations;7 and (iv) allow for the creation of an enabling environment that supports the implementation of new forest management practices, with a faster response of concerned stakeholders at no additional cost. Further, proposed actions will become part of the national policy framework and will be supported by the development of a legal framework, including legislation, policies, and technologies supporting the development of an offset mechanism will provide further opportunities for a continuous flow of resources to support the project's activities. The project will also contribute to the development of new tools for the private sector and local administrations. ## Adaptation of the agricultural sector, the expansion of forest areas and the reduction/removal of the negative impacts of drivers of forest degradation are preconditions for the adaptation of the most vulnerable people and communities as well as development of a sustainable economy in Serbia. The project will contribute to this by ensuring that forests (including removals, biodiversity, non-wood forest products, protection and fuel biomass) can no longer be delegated to central institutions only (Component 1). It requires new paradigms where central governments, local authorities and the private sector should effectively collaborate on the management of forests. The project will contribute by providing a framework for Serbia’s entry into with new approaches for people to engage and additional financial resources making the sector less dependent on national budgets. In addition, the project will support the development of a National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS) in order to secure the trust of national and international stakeholders and to assure that reported data about forests and emissions are verified. The project will also contribute by supporting the establishment of an enabling environment for private sector investment and transversal engines of sustainable and climate resilient development.

### Potential for scaling up and replication: The established mechanisms and introduced practices / technologies will be scaled up in Serbia, with a focus on the period 2019-2022 and beyond. In addition, the project will contribute to the period 2023-2030 and expand its role in forests and the agriculture lands. Furthermore, the proposed theory of change and intervention strategy are replicable in the entire Balkan where the forestry sector is confronted with similar bottlenecks and faces of deep-rooted problems. The project will contribute to this by ensuring that the lessons learned from the project are shared through FAO offices. In terms of adaptation, the activities of the project will allow stakeholders to explore the adaptation opportunities and challenges in the context of climate change. This will be done through a series of workshops organized under the Chamber of Agriculture, the Serbia Grain Association, The PNS, Association of Financial Institutions, the Forest Owner Guild, the Serbian Forestry Society, the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS) and other stakeholders. In order to ensure immediate scale up with the whole country by embedding in their processes and action knowledge and technologies introduced through the project, the project will contribute by ensuring that the lessons learned from the process supporting the scale up and replication at the national level of the project’s activities. Finally, the FAO is currently working on a similar project in the Republic of Macedonia which will be replicated in Serbia as well as other countries applying a blueprint approach. In addition, the project will ensure companies to access already existing knowledge and technologies through dissemination events. This will create high potential for attracting other companies in the future and contribute to the national offsetting market.

### Potential for knowledge sharing and learning: The project will invest in training and capacity development of both public and private sector stakeholders. In total, 100 people from the public sector and 350 people from the private sector capacities of over 4250 persons, investing over USD 8 million. Additionally, to further ensure long-term sustainability beyond the project period, the project will contribute by ensuring that the NFMS is embedded in the curriculum of national curricula (universities and vocational schools) related to topics covered by the project such as SFM practices. Furthermore, the project will contribute by ensuring that the lessons learned from the process supporting the scale up and dissemination knowledge and retaining implementation best practices related to decarbonization. Finally, the project will share its experience with other countries through dissemination events.
