#### D.3 Sustainable development

**Environmental Co-benefits:** In addition to the presented positive impacts in terms of CCA and CCN, the project will also contribute to a few processes such as: (a) improvement of soil quality and water availability; (b) decrease of erosion and landslides; (c) reduction of soil salinity; (d) increase of agricultural yields; (e) protection of river communities and infrastructures from flash floods, landslides and other natural disasters. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the Water Management Strategy 2017-2034 and paramount to the prevention of water pollution and soil erosion. The project will support the active protection and conservation of biodiversity. These will create corridors and shelter for wild animals, birds and insects. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the Forest Act (Official Gazette No. 58/17) and the Decree on the Ecological Network (Official Gazette No. 63/10). Finally, the project will partner during the energy transition process as WWF Serbia that will support and advice on community participation as well as biodiversity and nature conservation.

**Economic Co-benefits:** Directly monitorable benefits will originate from: (b) the improved efficiency of wood production; (c) increased value added from NFFP markets (e.g. fruits, berries, beekeeping etc.) and eco-tourism thanks to the increased biodiversity and forest quality; (d) increased income for households through the creation of new market opportunities in forestry plantations and from the agricultural lands protected by shelterbelts. With regards to energy, the lower unit cost of energy generated by wood will be a major economic benefit. Households are expected to face a lower unit cost for energy output produced by fuelwood. In addition to the direct benefits of reducing the adverse impacts of deforestation include distinctive social benefits as project’s activities will help reduce poverty by increasing income from NFFP markets and energy generated by wood. The project will also increase the value added from NFFP markets (e.g. fruits, berries, beekeeping etc.) and eco-tourism thanks to the increased biodiversity and forest quality. The project will also contribute to the creation of new market opportunities in forestry plantations and from the agricultural lands protected by shelterbelts. With regards to energy, the lower unit cost of energy generated by wood will be a major economic benefit. Households are expected to face a lower unit cost for energy output produced by fuelwood. Second, the project will enhance transparency around fuelwood availability and quality. Specifically, rural consumers will have greater knowledge on the custom user behavior and actual fuelwood availability in their vicinity. This will help them to make better decisions when purchasing fuelwood. The creation of new market opportunities in the forestry sector (e.g. green biomass) and for private forest owners to engage in sustainable forest management practices will also create economic opportunities in rural areas, directly benefiting the population of these areas.

## D.5 Country market ownership

106. The climate change policy and strategy framework of Serbia is articulated and has at its core: (i) The Climate Law (33/2021), the LCDS (2022), the NDCS (2020 – First update) and the National Adaptation Plan (NAP). Furthermore, the country’s climate change policy framework includes a number of other policies that will contribute to several of Serbia’s efforts to adapt to climate change and maximize mitigation benefits through measurable actions. The project will support Serbia in implementing its NDCs and LCDS by promoting Cas technologies, processes and practices and will support the country in expanding/engaging/catching up the needed policy framework for Cas. The project will also contribute to SDG 3, 6, 8, 9, 10 by promoting matter through increasing efficiency of biomass combustion and the occurrence of disasters through enhanced ecosystem resilience. The project will also contribute to SDG 12 by reducing waste generation and improving recycling in the frame of industrial decarbonization practices (SDG 7) undertake several key investments for combating climate change and its impacts, including the promotion of sustainable forest management and the reduction of deforestation and degradation and biodiversity loss through Cas practices (SDG 15). For more details see Table 23 in Annex 2.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 14

## NDCs, National Communications, National Adaptation Plans (NAP, 2015 and 2024) and national policy framework As demonstrated by the letters of support (Annex I.5.) and by the partnerships that the project will establish once operational During each phase of the project, the NDA will be involved in the preparation of the project document and its annexes In addition, the project will contribute to the implementation of the GCF Country Programme of Serbia, by supporting the Cluster (Energy efficiency and use of renewable energy sources) and Cluster 3 (NEKUS Water Resources – Agriculture - Forestry)

### 107. The NDA has been involved in the preparation of this proposal since the Concept Note phase During each phase of the project, the NDA will be involved in the preparation of the project document and its annexes Stakeholders in the process The project was developed and prepared following a request to FAO for the GCF in 2019 During the project preparation phase, the NDA has been involved in the development of the project document and consulted Occasions occurred with the MOFARM, the MEF, the METRO, the ME as well as local institutions such as municipalities, communities, farmers' associations, and other stakeholders The NDA will be involved during each phase of the project to obtain feedback from stakeholders on all aspects of the project: climate rationale, relevant climate change adaptation, and mitigation targets, the project approach, including the expected paradigm shift, target areas, and site selection Stakeholders engaged in the process include local communities, farmers' associations, municipalities, and other stakeholders

### 108. During project execution, consultations with stakeholders will take place yearly at the time of the preparation of the annual work plan (AWP) for each year of implementation The AWP is prepared by the PMU and reviewed and validated by all stakeholders involved in the project At the end of each year, a final AWP, all activities will be discussed, reviewed, and validated The AWPs will be presented by the PMU and members of the NDA will participate in these consultations to ensure that all stakeholders are informed about the engagement consultations, the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) – including relevant ESMFs for each project component – and the project implementation plan The ESMF is a document that sets out how the project will be implemented and explained A complete description of the stakeholders' engagement plan during project execution is available in Annex 7-10

### D. Efficiency and effectiveness

#### 109. The project will focus on forest areas identified by Serbia in its own NDC (see p. 3.10) Cohesion, Serbia agreed to focus most of its forestry budget over next 7 years to the FP target areas and contribute to FP results The co-financing commitments from the EU for the FP target areas are expected to be made over the next 7 years Co-financing commitments earmarked to project activities are essential to ensure the additionality of GCF projects such as implementing climate-adaptive silviculture, promoting biodiversity in natural forests over monocultures, facilitating knowledge transfer and capacity building

#### 110. The project will promote a more efficient and effective way to address forests, fast wood and carbon removals and contribute to the FP target areas by: (1) increasing the efficiency of forest management and reducing costs as an impact of the project via the outputs and inputs, including leveraged private finance from private banks and forest owners, (2) increasing the financial returns on investments in forestry projects, particularly in the agroforestry sector related to (i) investing in forestry through the project's activities, (2) investing in decarbonization, particularly in the agrofood sector, (3) increasing the value of ecosystem services generated by the project's activities that are not captured in the individual models A detailed analysis of the option is available in Annex 2, p. 2-22 On the financial side, the individual models show that the project exhibits a positive NPV and IRR for all scenarios The results indicate that the project will be financially viable and potentially more profitable for the private sector and the beneficiary households The models show positive financial parameters including an internal rate of return (IRR) of 15% and net present value (NPV) of 20% at a discount rate of 7% used as the financial discount rate of 7% used as a relevant cost of capital for private investment decision The forestry investments demonstrate high net ecosystem services production with a low financial value of economic benefits deriving from

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 15

## Ecosystem Services

The project's efforts on policy and regulatory framework will reduce the incentives for unsustainable practices (e.g., illegal logging) and strengthen the economic opportunities related to the forestry and energy sectors.

### Economic Analysis

#### Aggregate Level and Accounting for All Relevant Economic and Ecosystem Benefits

- **20-year Economic IRR:** 15.0% (higher than the social discount rate), with USD 34 billion in net present value.
- **Sensitivity Analyses:**
  - **6% discount:** Positive returns across all scenarios, even when loan disbursements for decarbonization investments are evaluated.
  - **9% discount:** Positive returns except for the 10-year scenario where aggregated economic outcomes remain positive regardless of variations in carbon prices.

#### Key Findings

- The project maintains a positive stream of aggregate economic returns under different sensitivity analyses, even when loan disbursements for decarbonization investments are evaluated.

E. LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

E.1. Project/Programme Focus
- Reduced emissions (mitigation)
- Increased resilience (adaptation)

E.2. GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential


| Assessment Dimension | Current state (baseline) | Potential target scenario | How the project/programme will contribute (Description) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Scale | Forest management is highly fragmented with limited investment capacities. Instead, an increasing number of small-scale forest users are beginning to adapt to climate change, fuelwood and charcoal demand and lack of management instruments. | Forest cover in Serbia is increased and sustainable and resilient forest management practices are adopted by all stakeholders. | Proposed actions will involve Serbia to reduce the deforestation rate and increase the forest cover while enhancing income and energy security of local households. The project will also contribute to the reduction of direct and indirect carbon emissions from forests, as well as the impact on biodiversity, water resources and soil conservation at the national level. It is expected that the project will also have a positive impact at the regional scale, as well as to the next generation of practitioners. |
| Replicability | Climate change actions are currently limited by project experience due to fragmentation of forest management activities across different sectors and incomplete policy framework. | The project will provide the model and replicable sector with the enabling framework for CAFD and carbon market access. It is expected that the model will be replicated throughout the country and the region. To be sustainable, the project will also contribute to the capacity building of local stakeholders in other countries beyond the project implementation area (e.g., a similar project will also be implemented in Montenegro). | |
| Sustainability | Forest and forest ecosystem services are at the core of the subnational development, decision-making process and public policy. However, they are managed in an incomplete and exclusive forest carbon finance framework that does not reflect their true value or expected contribution to the overall | The introduction of new forest governance and management methods and practices will increase the income from forests for local communities and private sector actors while contributing to the reduction of direct and indirect carbon emissions from forests. At the same time reducing the adverse impact of drivers of deforestation on biodiversity, water resources and soil conservation at the national level. Furthermore, with the creation of a carbon market, it is expected that the project will also have a positive impact in other countries beyond the project implementation area (e.g., a similar project will also be implemented in Montenegro). |

E.7. Monitoring reporting and evaluation arrangements

113. M&E System: A monitoring and evaluation system will be established for the project in keeping with the guidelines of GCF to report on its Integrated Results Management Framework designed to measure the core indicators and outputs of the project. The M&E system will be based on a matrix that will track the progress of the project activities within the oversight of FAO-Serbia Office and technical backstopping by the regional office (REU) which will also provide support in terms of capacity building, training, and other technical assistance to the PMU to keep track of performance and core indicators at the national and provincial level. The M&E officer of the project will work in parallel with the M&E officer of the Forestry Directorate that has local functional support and integrate the monitoring system into its existing framework.

All implementing partners will be required to provide a monthly progress report on their activities, and all implementing partners will specify their responsibility with respect to see-disaggregated data collection and reporting. The PMU will review these reports and prepare an overall project progress report (PPR) for submission to GCF. Regular meetings for monitoring and follow-up will be organized where problems will be discussed and, when needed, corrective actions will be taken.

114. Types of Reports: The PMU at the FAO office in Serbia will formulate an annual work plan and budget based on the annual physical targets based on the implementation plan (Annex 5) which will be approved by FAO-Serbia/FAO/REU. This will include a project progress report (PPR), Annual Project Performance Report (APPR), Annual Financial Reports, and Annual Performance Reports (APRs). These reports will be prepared by the technical staff at the PMU under the supervision of the M&E officer. The PPR will contain an overview of all activities carried out during the year with the M&E Reporting Matrix given together with their timelines and reporting responsibility (Table 14). More details are provided in Annex 5. The APPR will be a summary report on the project’s progress against the annual targets, including any additional project level indicators that have been selected for the project. APRs will also contain a narrative with updates on the project activities and achievements during the reporting period.

The PMU will ensure that all implementing partners are aware of their responsibilities in terms of data collection and reporting as well as the format to be used. The M&E officer at the FAO office in Serbia will specify their reporting responsibilities, the frequency of the reports to be produced and provide them with the formats for the reports. All reports will be submitted on time and all data will be provided in a timely manner.

All data will be disaggregated by sex to enable an assessment of the program inclusion in women in the project.

115. M&S System: An M&IS system will be developed for the project to record key information of all beneficiaries (e.g. GMR, gender, age, etc.) and their participation in the project activities. The M&IS system will also generate a monthly M&IS report for the project on an annual basis. Within the first quarter of the second year, when activities have been implemented and data has been collected, the M&IS system will be operationalized to ensure that all data will be generated by the project and will be monitored through consolidated remote sensing practices or geospatial analysis. The M&IS system will also provide a basis for monitoring and evaluation of the project in terms of understanding the relationship between climate parameters and the pattern of adoption and participation in project activities. The M&IS system will also provide an opportunity to assess the impact of the project on women.

The M&IS system will also record beneficiary phone numbers for feedback from participants. The M&IS system will be set up by the PMU at the FAO office in Serbia.

116. Survey Methods: The project will construct a baseline using primary and secondary data against which subsequent changes and impact will be measured. To measure attributable changes, the evaluations will draw on information collected through surveys (quantitative), focus group discussions (qualitative), key informant interviews, etc.) and quantitative (e.g. site-scale survey). Information on some of the key GCF indicators will be collected using a combination of primary data collection methods such as structured questions on elements. All surveys and assessments will be co-disaggregated and key gender-sensitive indicators will be included in all surveys and assessments to ensure that women’s participation is measured and findings. In addition, external partners will conduct all evaluations to ensure that there is no bias in the findings.

117. Independent Evaluation: To ensure that the project achieves its objectives and in line with the AIN signed with the GCF, two independent project evaluations will be conducted by FAO – interim and final evaluations. In line with the FAO policy on evaluations. They will be carried out at least once every three years to assess progress towards achieving the project’s outputs and outcomes as per GCF requirements as outlined in the GCF Evaluation Policy, Evaluation Standards, and Evaluation Operational Procedures and Guidelines. The project's studies and baseline mid-term and final surveys will constitute important

## G

Inputs for the project and final evaluations. Mid-term and Terminal Evaluation will be arranged in compliance with OED Evaluation policy requirement with support from the REU Regional Evaluation Coordinator.

### Beneficiary Feedback

FAO will establish a mechanism for beneficiary feedback and demonstrate how they are involved in the project. The PMU will undertake periodic visits to the project areas to discuss with communities their views regarding project activities and to confirm that the project is meeting its objectives. The PMU will also organize workshops, seminars, and other events with partners and executing entities about their experience with the project and the way the partners engage with them during the implementation of the project. These events will be organized in a manner that ensures participation from both sex-disaggregated perspectives. The PMU will also establish a GRM which ensures confidentiality. FAO's Guidelines for Compliance Reviews will follow the procedures for Complaints Related to the Organization’s ESS.

## F: RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

### F1: Risk factors and mitigation measures

#### Selected Risk Category 1: Change in political leadership could shift focus on CAS

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Change in political leadership could shift focus to other priority investments and lead to rapid turnover of staff in public offices, potentially affecting the capacity of the project to deliver its investments.

**Mitigation Measures**

The project will not reengage in the creation of additional policy frameworks, but in creating the conditions to execute the existing ones. The project will also ensure that the project is aligned with the policies and strategies of other institutions (e.g., MOM/FPM), with sectoral associations and with communities. Therefore, the project will only engage relevant high-level officials who are committed to the project’s objectives and have a clear understanding of its implementation process. This will ensure that the project can withstand structural changes that may take place in the government during implementation. Furthermore, the project will be designed in such a way as not to create new policy frameworks but rather to support the existing ones.

It is therefore likely that even in the event of changes in political leadership and further staff changes, the overall policy strategies will remain favorable.

#### Selected Risk Category 2: Rising energy prices exacerbate pressures on forest resources

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Given the current conflict in Ukraine and the resulting tension in the region, as well as other factors impacting energy costs. Soils could be exposed to economic and social shocks that may affect the country’s ability to guarantee access to forest resources for its population. In addition, rising energy prices may increase pressure on forest resources for fuel, as they remain the cheapest energy source.

The strategy of the project stands on three main pillars that serve to mitigate these risks: (i) Enhancing monitoring of resource uses. One of the main problems related to fuelwood is that this extraction occurs often in an uncontrolled way. This leads to overexploitation and deforestation, which can have negative impacts on forest resources. To ensure efficient use of wood for energy purposes, the project will provide the rules and regulations that biomass sellers must abide by, giving the consumer more possibilities to choose between different types of fuelwood with respect to their environmental impact and energy value. The project will support the enhancement of the regulatory framework and the strategies of SRPs and create

The project team, through the PMU, will collaborate with line ministries to regularly monitor energy prices and fuelled-based subsidies, as well as to identify sustainable and viable options to guarantee energy to the most vulnerable. Ad hoc PSC meetings will be called as necessary. | Category | Probability | Impact |

Description

Serbia may become the first country in the Balkans to seize such an opportunity. However, the development of strategic policies and policy-making delays, as such, can lead to exchanges with comparable experiences, which may be lacking in this context. Additionally, the evolving policy frameworks within the EU, particularly concerning the implementation of the CBAM, could impede the project’s progress if Serbia does not adopt certain measures.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

FAO is coordinating with national and international finance institutions, private development agencies and CSOs on potential carbon projects. The FAO will also work closely with stakeholders in understanding existing models. Furthermore, the project will establish regional policy dialogues motivating other countries to apply similar approaches as those that have already advanced.

**Selected Risk Factor 4: Resistance to change**

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

Description

Administration, municipalities and private landowners as well as companies and agribusiness will require time and incentives to shift from the BAU scenario to a low emission sustainable development. In the past decades forests have been considered a mining resource and the shift to a new governance will require transparency and clear engagement of all stakeholders.

**Mitigation Measures**

* The project will invest in awareness campaigns, including emphasizing investments and beneficiaries with an evidence-based approach and replicating local and international good practices. Additionally, the project will provide technical assistance.
* As agreed in the national engagement process, consented state actors will be involved in the whole project cycle process including monitoring and evaluation of executed activities.
* The project will use participatory approaches and for involving the private sector at a larger scale in reaching national forestry and biomass targets. Women groups that are already actively participating in forest management should be included as well.
* The project will create the enabling conditions for the private sector to engage in the country’s decarbonization process, including through the development of a business plan.
* Besides working with the State authorities at the national and the local levels, the Project will develop strong and effective partnerships with other stakeholders from civil society.

### Selected Risk Factor 6: Delays in production of seedling material

| Technical and operational description | Probability | Impact |
|---|---|---|
| Medium | High |

A consistent and essential part of the activities is dependent on the production of high-quality seedlings. Any delays in the production of these would have a very high impact on all other project outcomes.

### Selected Risk Factor 7: Lack of private sector engagement in decision-making process

| Technical and operational description | Probability | Impact |
|---|---|---|
| Low | Low |

Currently there is substantial interest by the private sector – including financial institutions and industry associations – to be involved in the project. However, given the current regulatory, statutory and other constraints that are currently available, the private sector may be discouraged or lose interest in investing in the process and prefer to invest in business as usual.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The project will build the capacities of all stakeholders on decarbonization and reduce the financial and technical barriers to entry for private sector engagement. To achieve this objective, the project will emphasize and disseminate the economic advantages of investment. The project will work together with the PKS and its circular economy platform, financial institutions, and other industry associations to establish a common understanding of the project’s objectives and benefits. Through this process, all parties will be charged with identifying potential barriers and financing avenues. Furthermore, as reported in section 8.4, private sector organizations can serve as observers to the project’s progress and provide feedback on how the project can better meet their needs. The private sector also needs to invest in reducing their overall carbon footprint as well as climate adaptation deficit. To this end, the project will work with financial institutions and other service providers to develop a common understanding of the role that they play in providing technical assistance to finance institutions, associations and firms to assess climate risks and to monitor emissions or potential carbon credits generated by projects.

The project will also involve service providers active in the provision of technical and financial services to private sector organizations. The project will provide training for these service providers on how to use the GHG emission reporting templates as reported in Annex 6. The project will supply the banks, companies and service providers with templates based on recognized international standards like Greenhouse Gas Protocol Corporate Standard, ISO 14064 and Emission Reduction Unit (ERU) Methodology. These templates are designed to be used by private sector organizations for GHG emission reporting as well as the importance of accurate and transparent reporting and compliance, including on reputational risks. The project will also provide training for service providers in the use of the GHG emission reporting templates from Start Date to End Term to verify GHG emission mitigation occurred. Additionally, the project will work with partners in strengthening stakeholder networks between GOs, banks, and the PKS to promote additional accountability.

This will establish connections with private sector actors, including financial institutions. Key mitigation measures are embedded in component 3, focusing on raising awareness and training private sector operators on carbon pricing and the associated risks. The project will also provide technical assistance to the Ministry of Letters of Intent (LOI). Rising energy costs further incentivize investment in efficient technologies (Annexes E–FA), and the risk of waning private sector interest in decarbonization is considered minimal. The project’s PMU and new technical unit will be responsible for implementing these measures, including providing training on carbon pricing, accessing updated information, data, and technical assistance on decarbonization and climate risk mitigation. They will also gather feedback from stakeholders to ensure the effectiveness of the measures.

| --- | --- | --- |

Selected Risk Factor 9: Carbon price volatility and decrease in carbon price

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Description |

The volatility in carbon prices during the project implementation period could shape investment, by adding a dimension of uncertainty to returns on investments. Additionally, carbon prices have been going down over time. A potential decrease in carbon price will reduce the financial return from carbon credits.

Mitigation Measure(s)

The timely and accurate information provision and sharing and identifying potential investments with positive financial returns, even after excluding carbon-related benefits, will reduce the effects of price volatility and decreases.

Selected Risk Factor 10: Money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism (ML/TF)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance and compliance | Low | Description |

Risks of money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism

Mitigation Measure(s)

FAO includes in the project agreement signed between FAO and the GOs clauses related to AMIC/CTF, as follows:

a) The Government shall comply; and shall require all persons and entities engaged in its activities under the Project to comply with the obligations set out in Chapter V of the UN CTF Convention. In particular, it shall ensure that it complies with the substantive obligations of the GO’s CTF on Prohibitions Practice.

b) The Government shall also comply; and shall require all persons and entities engaged in its activities under the Project to comply with the obligations set out in Chapter VII of the UN Charter. The Government and FAO are firmly committed to the international fight against terrorism and, in particular, against the financing thereof. In this regard, it is agreed that the parties will not provide any support or assistance directly or indirectly, to provide support to individuals or entities: i) associated with terrorism; as included in the list of terrorist organizations published by the United Nations Security Council (UNSC); ii) who are on the UN List or iii) that are the subject of sanctions or other enforcement measures promulgated by the United Nations Security Council. This provision must be included in all agreements concluded with third parties under the Project.

## E5.4 Forest biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of natural resources

The project will enhance the resilience of its forest ecosystems introducing CAS and SFM practices, it will not engage in poor quality afforestation/rehabilitation activities, nor will it introduce exotic species or genetically modified organisms (GMOs) at restoring forests, increasing biodiversity as means to restore. Forestry investments are designed to enhance biodiversity with specific priority to those areas that will act as corridors among existing forests. Therefore, activities at the sub-project level will be carried out in accordance with the following principles: a) The project will not engage in poor quality afforestation/rehabilitation activities, and environmental and social assessments undertaken at the sub-project level will ensure that no genetically modified organisms (GMOs) are introduced into the country. b) The project will only plant with native or locally adapted species and involving local communities. Activities will be executed according to the recommendations of the Forest Ecosystem Management Plan (FEMP). c) The project will not introduce exotic species in areas or in areas previously covered with forests. Therefore, livestock and aquatic genetic resources will not be impacted.

## E5.4.1 Project work plan

The project will select local species with wide ecological range and higher drought resistance, considering the climate change scenario. In addition to the above-mentioned principles, the project will ensure that planting seedlings from species in the lower limit of their ecological range, planting seedlings from species somewhat outside their ecological range, or planting seedlings from species in the upper limit of their ecological range. The project will select local species with wide ecological range and higher drought resistance, considering the climate change scenario. 129. The project is designed in line with GCF’s Gender Policy and considering that gender equality considerations should be mainstreamed into the entire project cycle to enhance the efficacy of climate change mitigation and adaptation interventions. A gender analysis was conducted to identify potential for promoting gender equity and mitigate potential risks. This was followed by a review of existing gender policies, strategies, guidelines, and studies conducted in the sector, and considering the consultations with the project team with stakeholders in the Government of Serbia. The identification of gaps and opportunities for addressing these gaps through the project design was followed by GAFIP addressing gender gaps and maximizing benefits of women’s engagement (Annex 8).

130. Compared to Euro Member States, the United Kingdom, and other countries in the region, Serbia ranks 24th. Considering that women are more vulnerable than men to climate change impacts, this ranking is even lower when higher values in the sub-domain of segregation and quality of work (62 vs. 61.4) compared to North Macedonia. Women in Serbia have a higher risk of exposure to sexual harassment at work (50% of women in Serbia are exposed to sexual harassment), higher levels of segregation, and quality of work (63 vs. 70.7). Compared to first-ranked Sweden, Serbia has the domain index lower by over 12 points (48.9 vs. 61.4) and is ranked 5th in terms of exposure to sexual harassment at work (50% of women in Serbia are exposed to sexual harassment), higher levels of segregation, and quality of work (63 vs. 70.7). Women with lower resource level and lower adaptation capacities to respond to climate change related natural disasters. According to the World Bank’s Gender Development Index (GDI) for 2019, Serbia ranks 58th out of 149 countries. Considering economic and financial capacities, women are more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change (FAO 2021). Reportedly Serbia has a high prevalence rate of gender-based violence (GBV), with 37% of women reporting that they were victims of GBV in the last year. In 2019, 58% of women aged between 16 and 49 years old reported having experienced sexual harassment at work. According to the latest data from the National Institute for Public Health and/or Sexual Intimate Partner Violence concerned in 2019, 17% of ever-partnered women aged 18-74 years were victims of intimate partner violence (IPV). The law on combating GBV was adopted in 2015. Serbia is a party to the UN Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and ratified the Optional Protocol in 2019.

131. The project will assist with gender mainstreaming of climate change policies and mechanisms to respond to recommendations from the Gender Equality Action Plan (GEAP). It will also contribute to the development of incentives for the improvement of the situation of rural women in terms of advice for access to land ownership, its acquisition, and management. The project will provide technical assistance to the Ministry of Agriculture on gender mainstreaming in the sector. The project will give priority to women owning degraded coppice stands or lands for conversion and will ensure that at least 50% of the beneficiaries are women. The project will also contribute to the development of a gender action plan, consulting available databases related to women’s participation in the different sectors/taxons addressed by the project. The project will provide technical assistance to the Ministry of Agriculture on gender mainstreaming in the sector and contracted and invited, and priority will be guaranteed for raising quality forests and forest production systems with established full value chains and partnerships and making them operable. The opportunity to restore their forests will promote women’s empowerment.

G.3. Financial management and procurement

134. FAO will ensure that financial management and procurement of goods and services using GCF resources adhere to relevant FAD rules and regulations, as well as relevant provisions in AMA signed between FAO and TCF. These include the “Financial Management Rules” (FMR) and the “Procurement Rules” (PR), which are part of FAO’s accreditation process. This includes financial management and procurement performed by FAO-GCF through its project supervision missions, including the GCF-FAO Project Supervision Unit (PSU). The Global Resources Management System (GRMS), which provides all FAO employees in all locations globally with travel, human resources, procurement, and finance functionalities. Using GRMS improves the flow of financial information, streamlining processes for reporting and payment. The GRMS is a web-based system that allows FAO to maintain a Chart of Accounts which is used by the whole Organization and that allows for a separation of income and expenses into different categories. All transactions are recorded in the GRMS and then automatically classified, summarized to facilitate internal management and external reporting requirements.

135. The GCF-FAO PSU will be responsible for the overall financial management and procurement of Goods, Works, and Services. To subcontract the delivery of specific activities using Letters of Agreement, FAO operates in accordance with the “Financial Management Rules” (FMR) and the “Procurement Rules” (PR). The Procurement Service, which provides policy and operational support to ensure that the Organization procures goods, works, and services in line with agreed standards and practices. This will be overseen by the GCF-FAO PSU and the FAO-RESURBENEA Office as Executing Entity will be overseen and supervised by the FAO-GCF project supervision missions.

The GCF-FAO project supervision team will undertake regular supervision missions and recruit an internationally recognized independent auditor to perform annual financial audits. All procurement activities, including those conducted by the PMU and executing entities are conducted in line with agreed standards and practices. This will be governed by the “Financial Management Rules” (FMR) and the “Procurement Rules” (PR).

G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

[2] No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full to the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.

□ With confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full to the GCF. The accredited entity will provide a redacted copy of the funding proposal. According to an accredited entity is providing the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

• full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly; together with an explanation of the nature and extent of such portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity’s disclosure policy; and

• redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

Republic of Serbia
MINISTRY OF AGRICULTURE,
FORESTRY AND WATER
MANAGEMENT

Date: 23rd September 2023
File No.: 12-5

To: The Green Climate Fund (GCF)

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management of the Republic of Serbia titled the project "Enhancing the resilience of Serbian forests to ensure energy security of the most vulnerable while contributing to their livelihoods and carbon sequestration"

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project titled the project “Enhancing the resilience of Serbian forests to ensure energy security of the most vulnerable while contributing to their livelihoods and carbon sequestration” in Serbia as described in the funding proposal submitted by the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management of the Republic of Serbia.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management of the Republic of Serbia.

Pursuant to GCF decision 8/III.16, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objective to the project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objective, it is implied that:

(a) The government of the Republic of Serbia has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;
(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of the Republic of Serbia;
(c) In accordance with environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our no-objective applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme. We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

[Signature]

GCF National Designate Authority / Focal Point

# Secretariat's assessment of FP260

## Proposal name:
Enhancing the resilience of Serbian forests to ensure energy security of the most vulnerable while contributing to their livelihoods and carbon sequestration (FOREST Invest)

## Accredited entity:
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations

## Country:
Serbia

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

#### Strengths
- The project targets two of Serbia's most vulnerable sectors: agriculture and forestry, which are crucial for food security, energy security, food and energy security, and the well-being of both rural communities and wildlife.
- These sectors play a pivotal role in reducing poverty and enhancing livelihoods while also safeguarding biodiversity and ecosystem services.

#### Points of caution
- To accelerate adaptation and mitigation efforts, including through private sector engagement:
  - Introduce innovative climate-adaptive silviculture and carbon finance solutions;
  - Apply financial instruments that maximize adaptation, mitigation and poverty reduction;
  - Strengthen partnerships between national banks, investors, and private sector companies focused on decarbonization and forestry; and
  - Develop digital tools to engage and aggregate private land and forest owners.

The proposal emphasizes financing from the Government of Serbia in in-kind contributions, underscoring the significant co-financing from the government and targeted leveraged financing from the financial sector and private forest.


---
**Page 76**
---

government’s commitment to its national adaptation plan and nationally determined contribution priorities, particularly for the agriculture and forestry sectors. This co-financed project will address climate change impacts in Serbia including the rehabilitation of access roads, productive forest management, increasing climate adaptive, biodiverse seedlings, and monitoring of forest health for pests and diseases. The project is essential for ensuring the additionality of GCF investments by combining increased climate adaptive silviculture, promoting biodiversity in natural forests over monocultures, facilitating knowledge transfer and climate proofing assets.

Furthermore, the project aims to attract USD 50 million or leverage private finance from financial institutions in Serbia as demonstrated by letters of interest. This funding will be used for co-financing loans for decarbonization investments across various sectors in Serbia. The project is expected to be leveraged from private forest landowners to support forestry investments.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE) and, if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in Annex II to document GCF/B.41/02.


## Summary of the Secretary's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

3 Serbia is a country with significant forest cover that plays a critical role in its energy security, ecosystem health and economy. With over 40 per cent of the population, especially rural communities, depending on forests for their livelihoods and energy supply. However, climate change has intensified the degradation of these forest resources through higher temperatures, irregular rainfall and prolonged droughts, threatening biodiversity and timber production. The combination of increased demand for fuelwood further endangers forest sustainability, leading to soil erosion, habitat loss and increased fire risk.

4 The project titled “Enhancing the resilience of Serbian forests to ensure energy security of the most vulnerable while contributing to their livelihoods and carbon sequestration (FORSERB)” is aligned with Serbia’s National Forest Strategy and the Sustainable Development Goals of the United Nations (FAO). aims to address these challenges through a nexus approach that integrates climate adaptation and mitigation. Key interventions include upscaling climate


---
**Page 77**
---

adaptive agriculture, strengthening energy security through climate-resilient forestry and engaging the private sector in de-carbonization investments. The project introduces innovative solutions, such as carbon finance frameworks, digital tools to engage private forest owners, and partnerships with international organizations. These measures aim to improve the energy supply chain through sustainable biomass products, reducing dependence on unsustainable biofuels. Targeted support for private sector investments in biomass facilities will help Serbia meet its renewable energy targets while also addressing Serbia's carbon sink capacity while meeting energy needs.

The project is expected to directly benefit 357 million people (54% of the total population), with direct benefits reaching over 729,000 individuals, including 171,000 women. Over 27 years, the project is expected to sequester approximately 68 million tons of CO₂ and reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 35%. It will also leverage biodiversity conservation, gender empowerment and poverty alleviation, aligning with Serbia's climate action plan.

The total funding required for this project is USD 92.8 million, with a request for USD 25 million from GCF. The Serbian Government will contribute in-kind co-financing amounting to USD 10 million, while the World Bank will provide a loan of USD 37.6 million through the Climate Investment Funds (CIF) and a USD 0.4 million grant from GFo. Additionally, USD 50 million is expected to be leveraged by the Serbian Government, through the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management, along with Serbia's climate action plan.

The project is classified under environmental and social safeguards (ESS) category B, indicating moderate impacts that are site-specific and manageable through mitigation measures. By promoting sustainable land use practices and reducing deforestation, this project will contribute to Serbia’s climate resilience, benefiting vulnerable local communities and fostering long-term environmental and economic sustainability.


### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

#### Outcome 1: National-level upscaling of sustainable and climate-smart agroforestry and carbon finance frameworks (USD 37.6 million; GCF and USD 0.4 million)

To achieve this outcome, the project will enhance forest management practices and establish a carbon finance framework, enabling Serbia to align with international standards and meet its commitments under the Paris Agreement. The project will also provide technical support to achieve this goal.

- **Output 1.1: Forest management and monitoring policy framework for climate adaptive infrastructure enhanced and disseminated.** This output focuses on establishing a continuous National Forest Monitoring system to provide reliable, up-to-date data on forest health, biodiversity, carbon stocks, and ecosystem services. The project will utilize permanent sample plots to assess changes in forest area, carbon storage and ecosystem health. This output builds upon the existing monitoring framework of the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry's ongoing monitoring post-project. The project will also develop guidelines to promote sustainable land use and forest use, reduce soil degradation and align with forest management practices that are consistent with international standards. These guidelines will be shared widely through national workshops and outreach efforts.

- **Output 1.2: National-level upscaling of institutionalizations to engage with carbon finance for agriculture, forestry and other land uses created.** This output will develop Serbia's capacity to leverage carbon finance by establishing frameworks for carbon offsetting and domestic carbon markets. It will also promote the use of carbon credits in agriculture and other land use sectors. Key activities include designing a policy framework for carbon offset


---
**Page 78**
---

# Output 1.3: National standard for biomass production and handling for energy purposes

The project will develop a national standard for biomass production and introduce standards for biomass production that mandate a maximum moisture content for biomass destined to be used in energy applications. A national certification scheme, will be established for biomass suppliers, along with consumer awareness campaigns to promote efficient biomass usage. Pilot programmes in selected municipalities will evaluate the impact of these measures on the development of sustainable bioenergy practices in biomass usage alongside other renewable energy options.

## Output 1.4: Short rotation plantations

Developed. This output focuses on diversifying Serbia's biomass resources by developing sustainable short rotation plantations (SRPs) for soil, rewilding and agroforestry. Activities include the development of a national standard for SRP management, establishing energy plantations and training experts in SRP management practices. This strategy is expected to support the country’s climate resilience goals through climate-resilient forest ecosystems, contributing to the country's carbon reduction and energy diversification goals.

### Outcome 2: Improved energy security and livelihoods from climate-resilient forest ecosystem and agroforestry

The project will develop a national standard for SRP management and create opportunities (total cost USD 40 million; GCF cost USD 14.3 million).

## Output 1.5: Climate-resilient forestry and improved energy security and livelihoods by fostering a climate-resilient forest sector that enhances carbon sinks and decarbonization opportunities

To achieve this, the project will deliver the outputs and activities set out below.

### Outcome 2.1: Knowledge on climate-adaptive silviculture enhanced

The project will develop knowledge and skills in climate-adaptive silviculture through a series of courses on increasing Serbia's capacity for climate-resilient forest management by upgrading public nurseries and seed centers to produce diverse tree species, including fast-growing and native species. The projects will produce over 31 million seedlings and bare-rooted seedlings, targeting 7,000 hectares for afforestation and 5,100 hectares for forest restoration. Activities include selecting native species and developing climate-adaptive silviculture techniques to improve tree survival rates in modern seedling production techniques. This output is designed to enhance biodiversity and improve the resilience of forests against climate change.

### Outcome 2.2: Knowledge on climate-adaptive silviculture enhanced

Under this output, the project will equip forest stakeholders with the knowledge and skills to implement climate-adaptive silviculture practices through a series of courses on increasing Serbia's capacity for implementing climate-adaptive silviculture practices. The project will also develop training materials for over 600 individuals in rural communities, prioritizing women and vulnerable groups. These programs will be designed to improve forest resilience against climate change by promoting agroforestry with successful forest and landscape restoration practices. This output aims to support a transition towards climate-adaptive forestry, reducing risks of forest degradation and productivity, and to facilitate regional knowledge sharing on the implementation of climate-adaptive silviculture.

### Outcome 2.3: Public forest land restored and afforested in a climate-adaptive and participatory manner

The project will restore and afforest public forests on pre-defined public lands, targeting 7,000 hectares. The project will develop detailed planting plans, conduct consultations with local communities and use climate-adaptive seedlings to ensure successful restoration of degraded forest areas. This output aims to transform degraded remnants into high forests, promoting sustainable management practices and actively involving local


---
**Page 79**
---

### Assessment against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The project aims to deliver transformative contributions to Serbia’s climate mitigation and adaptation, aligning with targeted results of the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2024–2027 [5: Ecosystems; 6: Infrastructure; 7: Clean energy; and 10: Micro, small and medium-sized


---
**Page 80**
---

enterprise(s). By addressing critical vulnerabilities in the forestry and energy sectors, the initiative supports Serbia’s transition towards a resilient, low-carbon future.

22. On the mitigation front, the project prioritizes afforestation, forest restoration and degraded coppice management, with a targeted net carbon impact of 8.4 Mt CO₂ eq. Over 27 years, these activities are expected to sequester 105.3 Mt CO₂ eq and reduce net emissions from the private sector by 0.8 Mt CO₂ eq. Key strategies include greening the economy through climate-smart agriculture, energy efficiency measures, and carbon markets to enable Serbian companies to mitigate emissions in production. Several commercial projects are being developed as part of this initiative, demonstrating strong private sector engagement. By mobilizing both public and private financing, the project aligns with Serbia’s net zero aim by 2050.

23. In addition, the project promotes climate-adaptive agroforestry practices to enhance resilience for rural communities that rely heavily on farmland. Climate-adaptive silviculture, coupled with energy efficiency measures, will improve water and soil conservation and ecosystem services. Private and community-led sustainable forestry and agroforestry practices will improve energy security, diversify sustainable energy sources and enhance resilience for the most vulnerable groups in Serbia.

24. The project is expected to reach 729,064 direct beneficiaries and 2.8 billion indirect beneficiaries by 2035. It also aims to reduce rural poverty and increase significant CO₂ reductions. Through its integrated approach to carbon finance, sustainable forest management and adaptive capacity-building, the initiative positions Serbia for a paradigm shift in climate change mitigation and adaptation strategies that align with national and international climate targets, driving a sustainable and inclusive transition.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

The project demonstrates a high potential for paradigm shift by introducing climate-adaptive silviculture practices and continuous forest monitoring, aligning Serbia with international best practices in carbon finance and sustainable forest management that enhance ecosystem services, including carbon removals, energy security and livelihoods, while directly supporting Serbia’s national and international climate targets.

By engaging private sector actors and strengthening their value chains, the project creates a replicable and scalable model for forest adaptation across Serbia and the wider Balkans. The integration of innovative financing mechanisms, such as public-private sector carbon finance mechanisms lay the groundwork for sustainable forest management, encouraging regional adoption.

Key elements such as climate-adaptive silviculture adoption, implementation of biomass standards and increased private sector participation underscore the project’s transformative potential in Serbia’s forestry landscape. At the national level, the project establishes a strong foundation for enhanced climate resilience, adaptation and long-term environmental and economic sustainability.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The project demonstrates high sustainable development potential, rated as high owing to its socioeconomic and environmental contributions. By supporting energy security for rural areas through improved biomass efficiency, the project aims to significantly reduce energy costs and support rural incomes, contributing to poverty alleviation. The improved efficiency of biomass also reduces household energy expenditure, freeing up


---
**Page 81**
---

resources for other needs. This socioeconomic impact aligns with the project’s goal to improve livelihoods, especially in rural areas.

26. Environmental co-benefits are substantial, as the project restores degraded lands, establishes shelterbelts and promotes biodiversity. These measures not only enhance local ecosystems but also reduce soil erosion, mitigate flooding, and prevent desertification of floodplains and landslides. By collaborating with the Serbian Water Directorate, the project ensures that affected communities have access to clean water and improved sanitation facilities, directly benefiting communities vulnerable to climate impacts.

30. Social and gender empowerment co-benefits are core components of the project. By creating new jobs in rural areas through sustainable forest management and modernizing the forestry sector, the project will boost rural employment and support poverty reduction through more efficient fuelwood production. The project also aims for a balanced gender distribution among beneficiaries, with at least 30 per cent of beneficiaries expected to be women, particularly in activities like land restoration and reforestation. This aligns with Serbia’s Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): supporting sustainable energy (SDG 7), ecosystem restoration (SDG 15) and climate action (SDG 13), while also promoting poverty alleviation and food security (SDG 2 and 1).

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient
Scale: Medium to high

31. Serbia is highly reliant on its forestry sector, which faces significant vulnerability due to climate change impacts, especially in rural areas where communities depend on fuelwood, non-timber forest products, and timber for their livelihoods. The project aims to address these vulnerabilities by introducing sustainable forest management practices, developing alternative energy solutions, and promoting the diversification of income sources within the forestry sector. By focusing on critical areas – risk reduction, monitoring and policy reform – the project strengthens Serbia’s capacity to protect forest resources, ensuring long-term energy security and ecosystem health.

The project also addresses key barriers to resilience by establishing a national forest monitoring system that supports sustainable forest management practices and carbon sequestration in sustainable forestry and decarbonization. By reducing technical and financial obstacles, it enables rural households to adopt new technologies and practices. Given the vulnerability of rural communities and the urgent need for climate adaptation, this project’s impact potential is rated medium to high. Key indicators include improved energy access for rural households, funding for community-based forest management projects, and increased participation in sustainable forestry.

### 3.5 Country ownership
Scale: High

35. The project aligns closely with Serbia’s updated climate goals, including its Low Carbon Development Strategy, Climate Law and national adaptation plan. These frameworks emphasize sustainable development as a key element of the country’s strategy for reducing greenhouse gas sources, making forestry a core element in Serbia’s ambition to become carbon neutral by 2050.

This project contributes directly to Serbia’s climate resilience goals by advancing climate adaptive silviculture practices and introducing new technologies that enhance forest productivity. By increasing ecosystem services and expanding carbon sinks, the project directly supports Serbia’s nationally determined contributions and the broader goal of adapting to climate change while achieving energy security.


---
**Page 82**
---

## 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The project demonstrates strong economic and financial efficiency, leveraging co-financing from Serbian Government and private sector investments to achieve its objectives.

Through mechanisms such as offsets, grants and concessional finance, it mobilizes private sector participation in forest carbon projects.

Although it has a high level of kid-contribution as co-financing from the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management, financial models reveal profitable returns with 20-year and 40-year horizons. This is achieved by making investments in forestry, decarbonization and ecosystem services attractive to private stakeholders through long-term contracts, ensuring cost-effectiveness and efficient resource use in achieving sustainable outcomes.

Regarding profitability and viability, the AE provides a detailed analysis of the financial viability of forest carbon projects for both public and private investors. For decarbonization loans, the profitability indicators for agriculture business investing in decarbonization technologies are positive with internal rates of return (IRR) exceeding 10% and payback periods of approximately five years. These results indicate strong financial viability, even in scenarios where interest rates exceed 5%. The analysis also shows that the profitability of forestry investments is constrained by the absence of GCF concessionality, with positive IRRs achieved only in the 40-year horizon at 95 per cent concessionality.

This justifies the need for financing to reduce the risk premium and increase returns on investment.

Economically, the project shows robust long-term viability, with 20-year and 40-year economic benefits exceeding costs by a factor of approximately four. This is based on the social discount rate used in the analysis, and net present values of US$ 78.4 million and US$ 127.5 million. These figures underscore the significant ecosystem and carbon benefits generated by the project.

The results also show that the project remains robust under multiple scenarios, including lower carbon prices (USD 10/ tCO2) which slightly reduce its economic returns.

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Project overview: The project aims to support Serbia in enabling the forestry sector to contribute to the country's low-carbon development by stabilizing and increasing carbon removals, upgrading management capacities of key institutions and communities and incentivizing private sector contributions.

Environmental co-benefits include biodiversity conservation through restoration of local forests and afforestation in a climate-adaptive way; soil quality improvement; increased water availability; decrease in evapotranspiration; protection of communities from floods and landslides; poverty


---
**Page 83**
---

### ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

The project’s ESMP provides a general description of the project areas, potential environmental and social risks and impacts, as well as the measures for avoiding, mitigating measures; implementation arrangements with regard to safeguards implementation; procedural arrangements for monitoring and reporting on safeguard performance; capacity-building needs for safeguards implementation; grievance redress mechanism; information management system; and other relevant aspects. Further details are given in annex 7 to the funding proposal package.

### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions

The project will operate in areas where there is gender inequality and in situations where youth work or unpaid family workers, lack access to decent work opportunities. The project will address these issues by promoting decent working condition as addressed per ILO 4 (Decent work) of the AE. The project will promote recruitment and hiring based on principles of equal opportunity and fair treatment, and there will be no discrimination in terms of age, gender or ethnicity. Workers will have access to training, job assignment, promotion, or termination of employment or retirement. Workers will be registered with the relevant social security institution (the Social Insurance Agency of the Republic of Serbia 204/15, 61/85 and 54/90) and workers will need to abide with the FAO code of conduct for decent work. The project will ensure that workers are within commuting distance from a 25-kilometre radius for each activity, as described in the eligibility criteria. The project will also ensure that no child labour or forced labour is involved in any activities supported by this in the ESMP includes all harmful or exploitative forms of child labour or forced labour.


---
**Page 84**
---

### ESS: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The ESFM recognizes that there will be no land acquisition or involuntary resettlement or displacement resulting from the project implementation. The project will not result in any loss of land ownership by farmers who have free and clear title to their land owned by the State or by farmers with clear ownership free from any dispute. The project will not result in expropriation of lands or planting on land of dubious ownership. The project will not result in involuntary resettlement, displacement, or relocation of households or communities under the project. With regard to road rehabilitation works, will be small scale, limited to existing access roads and minor roads. Works will include clearing of vegetation, removal of shrubs, hoes and rakes, hand tampers and portable pumps. Roads will be cleared from debris to provide safe passage for vehicles and pedestrians. The project will not result in involuntary resettlement or displacement of households or communities. Road users from the communities will be consulted and informed of schedule of works ahead of time.

### ESS: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The ESFM describes Serbia as a global centre for biodiversity in the region characterized by great genetic, species and ecosystem diversity. However, climate change and


---
**Page 85**
---

The rate of forest degradation is causing biodiversity issues such as less diverse species composition and less structured forests, which impacts the habitats of wildlife and wildlife migration, and occurrence of pest and disease outbreaks. The ESF indicates that the project will not only address these issues but also contribute to enhancing the resilience of forest ecosystems through sustainable forest management practices and adoption of climate-adaptive silviculture. The project will restore forests which will act as corridors within existing protected areas and other conservation areas, providing habitat for species involving local communities in the responsible management of planted forests. The non-eligibility of certain activities is due to their potential negative impacts on biodiversity values, including critical habitats and appropriate mitigating measures are adopted. An international expert will be engaged to provide technical assistance where necessary. Mitigating measures will be appropriate and in line with national and GCF ES requirements.

### Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS: Indigenous Peoples
The AE will meet the requirement of ensuring that all meetings of the ESS are held in a manner that at all times meets GCF requirements. The AE has legally committed to ensuring that the funding for the project is consistent with the World Bank Group’s (WBG) Indigenous Peoples Policy. This means that the mechanisms in place to consider sufficient and meaningful participation by Indigenous Peoples and local communities, including their free, prior and informed consent, will be considered when identifying project sites in the target areas. Stakeholder consultations and second-level screening will be held once specific project sites are identified. In line with its roles and functions, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group (IPAG) will provide technical assistance to the AE and other stakeholders share emerging good practices and success stories with the group.

### ESF
The ESF will not have any direct or indirect impacts on cultural heritage sites. However, in the case of any discovery of artifacts, chance finds procedures will be followed. The project will exclude any activity that is considered as illegal under host country laws, regulations, or international conventions and agreements related to cultural heritage.

### Implementation Arrangements
A project management unit (PMU) will be established consisting of an ESS Specialist to implement the ESF for the entire project duration. The ESS Specialist will be responsible for ensuring overall compliance with the ESF, provide capacity-building support to the AE, and ensure that all safeguards are implemented in a timely manner, disseminating and managing the grievance redress mechanisms, and monitoring of safeguards performance. The PMU will report on safeguard performance at least once every six months to both the Gender Specialist and Monitoring and Evaluation Specialist on matters regarding stakeholder engagement and gender equality. The PMU will also report directly to the GEF Sachsia Representative Office, the Project Steering Committee representing the line ministries, and the FAD Regional Office. A monitoring and evaluation system will be established as part of the project, which includes collection and consolidation of safeguards performance. The implementing partners will provide regular updates on safeguard performance in their respective reports as required by GCF. Such monitoring reports will serve as a basis for any corrective actions needed


---
**Page 86**
---

during implementation. Capacity-building activities on safeguards implementation (including training and awareness-raising) will be held at different management levels both at the national level.

### Stakeholder engagement. A series of engagements with direct and indirect stakeholders, including representatives from government offices, non-governmental organizations, community-based organizations, universities and research institutes, conservation groups, local communities, indigenous peoples, and service providers, etc., were undertaken at the project design and formulation stage. Key concerns raised with regard to the project’s potential environmental and social risks (ESR), the project approach, including the expected project shelf, as well as target area and site selection. Stakeholders agreed on needs to be addressed, targets, implementation arrangements and modalities for addressing these needs. The engagement process was documented in detail during the project design. The inclusion of civil society and private sector organizations in the Project Steering Committee (PSC) will ensure that their views are taken into account throughout and well documented. Stakeholder consultations will continue during implementation and will be guided by the stakeholder engagement plan.

### Grievance redress mechanism. A project-level grievance redress mechanism will be implemented at the field level to address project-related grievances during implementation, as per the FAO’s Gender Policy. The AE has appointed a Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Specialist, who will be acting as the grievance redress mechanism focal point, together with the Project Coordinator and the Country Director. The AE will ensure that all staff are aware of international standards. Any aggrieved party may also refer to the FAO Serbia Representative Office, the FAO Regional Office for Europe and Central Asia (Rome), or other relevant national and international standards. Any aggrieved party may also refer to the FAO Serbia Representative Office, the FAO Regional Office for Europe and Central Asia (Rome), or other relevant national and international standards. The grievance redress mechanism will not impede access to the country’s judicial or administrative remedies. The AE has provided a gender analysis and action plan as well as a communication during implementation, as described in the stakeholder engagement plan.

### Gender equality and social inclusion (SEAI) safeguarding measures. The International Social Policy adopted by decision BIII-2021/18 requires safeguarding from sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) in GCF-financed activities. The AE provided SEAH safeguards throughout the project design phase to address the potential SEAH risks of this specific project, identified measures for preventing SEAH and provided guidance on how to implement these measures. To ensure that all stakeholders are aware of SEAH safeguarding measures throughout this project, the AE developed a comprehensive checklist for all staff members in the field offices and at headquarters as well as a risk management perspective. These are outlined at different levels, including stakeholders engaged from 1970 onwards. The AE has also provided guidance on how to address gender-based risks. The AE has put in place various SEAH-related mitigation measures (included in the gender action plan). The project will also leverage existing national and international institutions in the country, and capacity-building activities for these institutions will be done during implementation. The Global Fund, United Nations Population Fund, the United Nations Entity for Gender Equality and the Empowerment of Women, and the World Bank are among those institutions that have been identified as other community stakeholders. The SEAH grievance redress mechanism will be survivor-centred and gender-responsive in line with the FAO’s Gender Policy. The AE has SEAH protection and gender-based violence capacity to facilitate the SEAH measures put in place for the project.

#### Gender policy
The AE has provided a gender analysis and action plan and therefore complies with the GCF Gender Policy. The gender assessment highlights significant barriers to gender equality in


---
**Page 87**
---

Serbia’s rural areas and forestry sector: Key challenges include entrenched patriarchal norms and stereotypes limiting women’s roles in decision-making, economic participation and resource ownership. Rural women face restricted access to education, technology and training, leading to a gender gap in skills and knowledge. Women are also underrepresented or overrepresented in unpaid or informal work and underrepresented in forestry education and technical roles. Additionally, institutional weaknesses, such as insufficient gender mainstreaming and lack of gender-disaggregated data, can exacerbate gender gaps in rural and forestry-focused interventions.

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) for Serbia addresses these challenges by integrating gender equality into forestry governance and rural development. It defines activities, indicators and targets for women’s participation and benefit with corresponding time lines, budget and gender equality action plan. The GAP also includes a monitoring framework to track progress, promoting their involvement in decision-making processes, and fostering access to education, training and employment opportunities.
