### Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

#### Co-financing:
The Secretariat acknowledges the co-financing totaling USD 584.6 million from the European Bank for Reconstruction and Development (EBRD). The co-financing does not materialize, this may impede project implementation. As such, the incorporation of a contingency reserve in the financial plan to cover any status in the term/funded activity agreement are considered adequate mitigation measures.

#### Serbia:
The Secretariat notes that Serbia has been upgraded from Bb to BBB- by Moody’s and Baa3 by Fitch. This upgrade is expected to have positive implications for Serbia, including greater domestic product growth and foreign direct investment. The government debt dropped to a moderate 87 per cent of gross domestic product, providing additional room for fiscal spending.

### Compliance risk (medium risk)

The inherent risk for the proposed project primarily arises from its diverse stakeholder landscape, which includes both government and selected private sector involvement in forest and biomass energy projects. These stakeholders may have varying degrees of experience with AML/CFT compliance, according to the FAO risk assessment, the inclusion of private sector partners introduces potential risks related to their ability to effectively address AML/CFT compliance and adherence with AML/CFT standards.

### Mitigation measures

To address these risks, FAO has instituted measures to mitigate AML/CFT risks, which are embedded within its existing internal controls and reinforced by project-specific strategies. Key controls include:

- **Internal Controls**: The Secretariat monitors compliance with AML/CFT policies and relevant third parties, mandating adherence to FAO policies on prohibited practices and AML/CFT standards.
- **Project-Specific Strategies**: These include a semi-annual review of the risk, establishing proactive mechanisms to detect and respond to potential non-compliance. The Secretariat also monitors compliance with these controls, ensuring alignment with international AML/CFT standards.

Additionally, the integration of FAO into the United Nations Security Council sanctions compliance framework enhances the organization’s capacity to prevent any indirect support to entities associated with terrorism. This multi-tiered control framework is complemented by regional workshops and training sessions organized at the FAO regional offices, ensuring compliance with AML/CFT policies during the entire project lifecycle.

Considering the extensive controls and mitigating strategies in place, the residual risk level for this project is considered low. The Secretariat will continue to engage multiple stakeholders to uphold stringent AML/CFT policies across both public and private sectors. This approach to fraud, regular audits, and inclusion of AML/CFT classes in all agreements with third parties significantly reduces the probability of non-compliance. With the FAO established

## Legal assessment

72 The accreditation master agreement was signed with the AE on 8 June 2018 and it became effective on 4 October 2018. The five-year accreditation term of the AL expired on 3 October 2019, but the Board approved the re-accreditation of the AL for a further period of three years from the date of lapse of the five-year accreditation term or the date at which a revised accreditation framework is adopted by the Board. The Board approved the re-accreditation of the AE on 8 June 2018, and the continuation of the amended and restated accreditation master agreement is ongoing.

73 The AL has provided evidence to demonstrate that it has obtained all necessary approvals, and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

74 The proposed project will be implemented in Serbia, a country in which GCF is not provided with appropriate privileges and immunities. As such, the GCF may not be protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed by the Board. It should also be noted that the legal framework for investments in such countries may be hindered due to the absence of privileges and immunities for relevant GCF personnel.

75 Therefore, it is recommended that the Board consider whether disbursements of GCF proceeds should only be made after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation or that adequate insurance has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities for GCF and its personnel.

76 To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board include the following conditions:

(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form of substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP260

## Proposal name: Enhancing the resilience of Serbian forests to ensure energy security of the most vulnerable who consume it in their livelihoods (Forest Investment (FIREST Invest))

### Countries
Serbia

### Accredited entity:
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations

### Project/programme size:
Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. **Forests play a crucial role in Serbia's economy and energy security**: Biomass accounts for nearly 12 percent of the country's energy consumption. Firewood is used by over 40 per cent of the households, most of them in rural areas. However, both climate change and economic development are leading to deforestation and degradation of forests, negatively affecting the contribution of forests to reduced net greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions.

2. **The proposed project, "FIREST Invest," aims to make renewable biomass a sustainable source of energy**: The proposed project - FOREST Invest - seeks to improve climate resilience, livelihoods and energy security in Serbia.

3. **The programme comprises three components which combine outputs/outcomes into three broader outcomes, respectively**:

   (a) **Outcome 1 involves national-scale upscaling of the sustainable and climate-adaptive silviculture framework**: This includes outcome 1.1 (Forest management and monitoring policy framework for climate-adaptive silviculture enhanced and disseminated), output 1.2 (Knowledge on climate-adaptive silviculture shared with carbon finance for the agriculture, forestry and other land use (AFOLU) sector created); and

   (b) **Outcome 2 involves national-scale upscaling of energy security and livelihoods from climate-resilient forest ecosystems and GHG emissions reductions from increased carbon sinks and decreased deforestation**: This includes outcome 2.1 (Forest management and monitoring policy framework for climate-adaptive seedlings enhanced), output 2.2 (Knowledge on climate-adaptive silviculture of key stakeholders enhanced) and output 2.3 (Public land forest restored and afforested in a climate-resilient and participatory manner).

   (c) **Outcome 3 involves private sector engagement in climate-adaptive silviculture and decentralized energy production**: This includes outcome 3.1 (Private sector engagement in the climate resilience of forests enhanced through climate-adaptive and diversified investments and greening of the biomass value chain initiated), output 3.2 (Mobilized private sector investment in climate-resilient forest management and renewable energy), and output 3.3 (Financial institutions, consultancy service provider, and academia capacitated on climate-resilient forest management).

4. **The programme will be implemented through a set of executing entities**: the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management (MAB/PWM) and two public forest management enterprises [Serbia Shume

## 1.1 Impact potential

* **Score: High**

This cross-cutting project contributes to both mitigation and adaptation results, with most of the funding (two-thirds of the GCF contribution and nearly three-quarters of the co-financing) aimed at promoting adaptation results. The most important result areas in terms of budget allocation are climate change adaptation (50 per cent), followed by forest management (32 per cent of the co-financiers' contribution), and AR4A (Ecosystems and ecosystem services), using 50 per cent of the GCF contribution.

According to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN), Serbia has relatively low vulnerability to climate change and relatively high readiness, suggesting that adaptation is not a priority for Serbia. However, the project specifically targets sectors currently pointed out in Serbia's national communications to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) as most vulnerable to climate change impacts, including agriculture in Serbia. These sectors are also vital for the country’s economy. Hence, whereas vulnerability may not be high in relative terms, the project specifically targets the most climate-vulnerable sectors in Serbia.

Over a million people are expected to benefit from the stakeholder involvement and dissemination of adaptation results (output 1.2). More than one million people will benefit directly (600,000 people are expected to benefit from the enhanced production of seedlings (output 2.1), and the land restoration activities (output 3.1) will also benefit more than a million people. Another 650,000 people are expected to benefit from the private sector's contribution to green and climate-adaptive investment (output 3.3), and nearly 500,000 people stand to benefit from the forest management activities (output 4.1) in terms of increased income opportunities, especially for forest management and policy framework (output 1.1).

Among the direct beneficiaries are approximately 873,000 people (6.7 million direct beneficiaries and 2.85 million indirect beneficiaries), which is close to half of the population of the country. The beneficiaries include forest owners, wood consumers, smallholders, and other stakeholders in the forestry sector who have shown interest or are interested in establishing windbreaks. Assumptions include directly benefiting the poor and those at risk. Indirect benefits are expected for approximately 10 million people (873,000 direct beneficiaries). Box 25 specifies the assumptions and the calculation of the direct and indirect beneficiaries at the output level.

*Among the 187 countries ranked in the ND-GAIN index, Serbia is the 97th most vulnerable country to climate change and ranked 60th in readiness (https://gain.educ/our-work/country-index/rankings/).

13. The FOREST Invest project also seeks to sequester around 84 million tonnes of carbon dioxide (CO2) over its 27-year lifespan. This is to be achieved by greening the biomass value chain, decarbonizing arboriculture, enabling carbon finance mechanisms for Serbian companies to mitigate emissions in their production processes, as well as leveraging sector financing and public-private partnerships. The project’s overall objective is that this initiative seeks to mobilize both public and private sectors in working towards Serbia's climate goals, particularly in the energy and forestry sectors.

15. The mitigation impact potential was prepared using the Nationally Determined Contribution Expert Tool (NDCX), which was recently launched and fits well with the specific context for Serbia. This tool provides a consistent methodology for calculating the carbon footprint of forests, the methodology for determining the baseline, the data used for carbon accounting, and the calculation of mitigation impact potential. The project also includes a monitoring framework that specifies how the monitoring information and data will be gathered and analyzed in a way that facilitates the understanding of the project’s impact on climate change as well as links to the national measurements, reporting and verification system of Serbia.

16. The project stands to effectively work with the most climate-vulnerable parts of the Serbian economy. The impact potential is assessed to be high.


### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Medium to high

The theory of change statement reads:

"If knowledge and technology, coupled with Climate-sensitive forestry investments, are made available in a coordinated way, inclusive governance mechanisms for forests and carbon are established, THEN the country will have an enabling environment to foster cooperation between private and public actors to increase and accelerate forest adaptation and mitigation actions, and achieve its strategic adaptation and mitigation targets. Because these investments will strengthen forest resilience and provide greater benefits to people—particularly women and girls—while increasing carbon storage and advancing decarbonization within the agroforest sector" (revised funding proposal, section B2 (a), para. 12).

The statement is rather complex and multilayered, but emphasizes knowledge and technology as key enablers for change. It suggests that these investments will lead to a shift in mindsets and forms of changing behaviours and generating change beyond a one-off investment. In this sense, a paradigm shift stands to be the main outcome, to the extent that the ambition to decarbonize the wood sector is achieved. The project also aims at supporting the transition towards a more forestry sector, he realising:

18. The project also engages with private/individual land and forest owners, for example through digital tools, to support the scaling up of techniques, practices and investments to the national scale and enable the private sector to transition into a change agent towards decarbonizing the wood sector.

22. The cross-cutting approach of the project involves ensuring that investments in adaptation also yield mitigation as well as poverty alleviation results. FOREST Invest seeks to contribute to sustainable development by promoting a transition towards more resilient states that wider benefits of the project includes contributions towards poverty reduction (SDG 1), food security and nutrition (SDG 2), improved health outcomes (SDG 3) and reduced air pollution resulting from improved biomass combustion efficiency (SDG 4), de-carbonization through increased use of renewable energy sources (SDG 7), climate change adaptation action (SDG 13), as well as the sustainable use of terrestrial ecosystems through climate-adaptive silviculture (SDG 15).

The project also seeks to support income diversification, for example by using forest land for beekeeping or developing ecotourism activities. Forest agricultural lands should be converted to bioenergy plantations stand also to generate income.

Modernization and the greater transparency of the biomass supply chain will also contribute to increased income generation. Farmers should also acquire greater knowledge about fuelwood types and heating appliances to support better utilization of woodfuels.

# Needs of the recipient

## Scale: Medium

Serbia is an upper-middle-income country with a growing economy: annual growth in gross domestic product (GDP) per capita was 8.3 per cent in 2021 (see page 5 of annexes to the funding proposal). Serbia has made significant progress on climate change mitigation and adaptation action. The main justification for the GCF funding request is that in 2022, Serbia’s public debt accounted for more than 70 per cent of GDP. At the same time, the written question and answer exchange between ITAP and FAO, it was also suggested that the GCF grant will address: a complex blend of market failures by delivering public goods, such as by enabling access to markets for smallholders, promoting agroforestry and silviculture techniques not adopted previously due to informational barriers, and enhancing the wood value chain as a source of renewable energy for the country.


---
**Page 96**
---

10. The funding proposal further suggests that concessional financing will help enhance the energy security of the rural poor and set Serbia on a more sustainable future-energy-decarbonization path. In the longer term, however, the idea is that concessional finance should underpin sustainability.
20. Serbia’s revised nationally determined contribution (NDC), submitted in August 2022, commits to “Increasing its ambition to the GHG emission reduction by 13.2 per cent compared to the 2019 level” and to “increasing energy efficiency of all sectors of economy”. The NDC also highlights the importance of hydropower generation for the Serbian economy; the household sector is identified as a priority area in terms of energy efficiency, with electrification being an important element regardless of which fuel is used, and the low level of energy efficiency.
30. The updated NDC refers to the mitigation potential in the sectors of agriculture, forestry and water management. In addition to the 2019 baseline for the forest sector (see para. 75), “plans to invest over USD 9 million annually into the sector, including a majority of its forestry component” (para. 83). The NDC also refers to the potential of carbon sequestration in forests and national capacities to manage them, and to reduce inefficiencies in the wood value chain.
40. As explained above (section 1.1, para. 9), Serbia, being non-RD-GAIN, has relatively low vulnerability to climate change and is well positioned to address existing climate risk. In this context, the NDC highlights the importance of increasing the resilience of Serbia’s forestry sector and enhancing resilience and energy security of households, while increasing total CO2 removals from forestry and biodiversity.
50. Altogether, the needs of the recipient are assessed as medium.


### 1.5 Country ownership

The proposed project was developed and prepared following a request to FAO by the Government of Serbia (GoS) for technical assistance in preparing a project proposal with the forestry, energy and agriculture sectors in Serbia were consulted. The Summary of Consultations and Stakeholder Engagement Plan (see appendix 3 to annex 7 of the funding proposal) outlines the process used to engage stakeholders during the preparation phase. The stakeholder engagement plan foresees continuous national as well as community-level consultations with key stakeholders, including representatives from government agencies, civil society organizations and other relevant actors. It also coordinates with other externally funded initiatives, including the Global Environment Facility (GEF). The anticipated project will be implemented in close collaboration with the Government of Serbia through the Forest Sector Investment Programme (FSIP) and the Green Climate Fund’s Low Emission and Resilient Development in Serbia initiative, financed by the GEF Trust Fund. The specific needs and priorities for forest sector investments that are in need of urgent climate-proofing have been identified through an analysis performed by the Forestry Institute of Serbia.

The main project stakeholders include ministries, municipalities, industry associations and the chamber of commerce. MiSMAF is responsible for forestry, agriculture and water management policies in the country. The project will work mainly with the Forest

### Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: High

The coherent structure of the funding proposal and the logical way that actions feed into projects to provide outcomes and benefits, speaks for a high likelihood of the project achieving its objectives. This contributes to project efficiency and effectiveness.

Moreover, the project sets out to promote a more efficient and effective way to address forest degradation and deforestation in Serbia. The proposed approach is innovative and will be incentivized and mobilized via the offers and inserts, including leveraged private finance from private sector partners.

Forestry is one of the priority areas identified in Serbia's updated NDC, and the country has agreed to focus most of its forest budget over next seven years on the target areas of the proposed project. The total amount of funding for REDD+ (REDD+) in Serbia is important and amounts to nearly two-thirds of the total project budget.

The economic and financial benefits have been estimated using four types of models, as explained above. These models show that the proposed interventions demonstrate high net ecosystem services production with a low financial value of economic benefits deriving from ecosystem services. The proposed approach will not lead to any significant increase in deforestation or unsustainable practices, such as illegal logging. In all, the analysis suggests financial efficiency in the achievement of its mitigation targets.


---
**Page 98**
---

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Overall, the funding proposal is well-crafted and efficiently addresses problems of forest degradation and deforestation in Serbia, particularly in the Vojvodina region, which are leading to increasing climate vulnerability and GHG emissions. The cross-cutting endeavour, to be implemented by FAO, BMOWM and the two public forestry management enterprises PES and PEV, is likely to achieve its objectives and generate significant adaptation and mitigation outcomes.

The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# Introduction

Compared to the reference period used in the two available national communications to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) [1985-1990], climate variables in Serbia have changed. Temperatures – both minimum and maximum – are increasing steadily while rainfall patterns appear more erratic and generally concentrated in autumn and spring. Projections to 2050 indicate that the average temperature will increase by about 3°C, with a significant increase of up to 6°C over the winter months. These changes have already had an impact on agriculture, which is one of Serbia’s main economic sectors. Climate change has become an issue to address especially in those sectors, such agriculture, that are still largely dependent on climate. The Serbian government has therefore included climate change adaptation and mitigation into consideration, especially those related to implementation of low carbon development strategies.

Serbia's low carbon development strategy promotes a transition towards a climate-neutral and resilient economy where decarbonization will largely increase CO2 absorption by forests (137% 2030). The forest sector is therefore expected to be affected in the future by changes in land use across stakeholders. Forests are facing unprecedented threats due to climate change, and CO2 removals from the atmosphere are projected to decrease between 2015 and 2050 and, in a business-as-usual scenario (BAU) might reach -26% in 2050 compared to 2010.

According to National Forest Inventory (NFI) 2008 results, forests in Serbia cover around 30% of the country’s total area (Central Serbia 37 % forest cover; Vojvodina 4% of forest cover). Even though the forest cover has increased by about 15% since 1990, it is still below the target set for achieving habitat loss and fragmentation, is one of the key environmental problems faced by Serbia at present. Forest degradation and deforestation are also a major concern in Serbia as they affect both ecosystem goods and services, and has substantially reduced the potential of Serbian forests to act as carbon sinks. Between the years 2001 and 2018, 63,280 ha of forest were lost in the national territory of Serbia (Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management of Serbia).

Following the government request to Food and agriculture organization (FAO) and the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) targets of Serbia, as well as the GCF Country Programme, the project is under preparation. The project aims at increasing the resilience of Serbian forests through early engagement in decarbonization, as envisaged by the Republic of Serbia. It is titled: Enhancing the resilience of Serbian forests to ensure energy security of the most vulnerable while contributing to their livelihoods and carbon sequestration.

The objective of the project is to support the Republic of Serbia in enabling the forest sector to contribute to the country’s climate change adaptation and low carbon strategy by:

- Reducing the vulnerability of vulnerable communities by increasing their energy services
- Stabilizing and increasing carbon removals,
- Upgrading the capacity of key institutions and private sector operators to manage the decarbonization process.
- Incentivizing private sector companies to engage in climate de-risking and decarbonisation process.

The project will address bottlenecks to climate change adaptation and mitigation through:

- **Component 1 - Enhancing forest management and forest governance while reducing drivers of degradation:**
- **Component 2 - Enhancing LULUCF contribution to climate change mitigation and DRR:**
- **Component 3 - Supporting private sector engagement in climate densifying and decarbonization.**

The co-benefits of the project will include:
1. The increased participation of the private sector in sustainable and climate adaptive forest management
2. The promotion of the wood-based biomass value chains

The combined impact of these actions will enable Serbia to reduce the vulnerability of its forestry sector and communities while increasing total CO2 removals.

Given the distribution of forests in Serbia, the project will work at the national level in order to benefit the entire population of the Republic of Serbia. The project will ensure the participation of key sectoral actors from national and local institutions as well as from civil society organizations and academia.

The success of the project is dependent on the integration of gender equality mainstreaming into forest governance and climate finance. The value of its contribution will be increased, as the overall implementation plan and actions foreseen are sensitive to the diverse needs of all stakeholders involved in forest management. The project will strive to promote gender balance from the basics - stakeholder participation in meetings and other platforms - through capacity building for women and men, ensuring that they have equal opportunities in developing gender-responsive forest governance and in-depth technical knowledge extension, budget planning and actions conducted.

To enable project to have a gender-sensitive approach and provide benefit to the wider community of the Republic of Serbia as the standard requirement of the GCF, this gender analysis provides recommendations and action plan for the funding application.

# Aims and targets of the proposed project

By enhancing the resilience of Serbian forests and rural communities while enhancing the carbon storage potential of the country to support and boost the decarbonization process through adaptation and mitigation measures, Serbia is moving towards areas of mitigation and adaptation.

Transition towards a climate-neutral and resilient economy in Serbia is seen through a low carbon development strategy which promotes primarily decarbonization through increasing the CO2 absorption capacity of forests. This requires an adaptive approach to ensure ecosystem services to rural and vulnerable communities.

Therefore, the project aims at:
- Increasing the resilience of 730,064 individuals (371,823 women) (7.6 MC02 277)
- Reducing CO2 net emissions by increasing carbon removals from the forestry sector

Unfortunately, Serbian forests are already affected by climate change themselves and being degraded by firewood extraction. Forests are degrading, making Serbien's capacity to deal with required CO2 absorption increasingly uncertain. The forest is also more vulnerable to pests and diseases exposing them more often to forest fires. Rural livelihoods have become increasingly uncertain, pushing people into poverty and increasing the risk of migration.

For that reason, project targets are seen as possible to reach only by supporting the forestry sector for:
(a) Increasing forest cover by 7,000 ha;
(b) Enhancing about 42,000 ha of public and private degraded coppice stands into high forest;
(c) Enhancing forest management and promoting afforestation on 500,000 ha of land;
(d) Creating an effective and efficient mechanism that will allow the private sector to dispose of CO2 credits to complete their decarbonization.
(e) Engaging with private sector companies in climate de-risking and decarbonization processes.

These actions fall under the 3 main categories of adaptation measures of the Serbian Low Carbon Development Strategy:

1. Afforestation using location mapping and tree species adapted to climate change;
2. Introducing "Climate-Smart Forestry" approaches; and
3. Climate-smart forest management practices following a Close-to-Nature Forest Management/Close-to-Nature Forestry approach

Those will require full engagement of various local rural development stakeholders and governmental institutions, civil society, and academia. Also, complete knowledge/human and natural capital of local projects is required to ensure the success of the project. The project will also use cutting-edge technologies, to conduct long-term action for the purpose of supporting development of sustainable communities and healthy ecosystems capable to interact for the benefit of each other.

Gender-related international frames of the proposed project

The Republic of Serbia is investing a lot of energy to come along with the contemporary trends of sustainable development, which also includes the maximization of the available human resources for the benefit of its citizens. Serbia's gender equality policies are guided by international commitments that the state has undertaken for the ratification of key international conventions, such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women and the Council of Europe Convention on preventing and combating violence against women and domestic violence (Istanbul Convention). In addition to these international instruments, Serbia is also guided by regional commitments, such as the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, and UN resolutions, including the United Nations Security Council Resolution 1325 on Women, Peace and Security. Serbia regularly submits its reports under these conventions.

Serbia's development policies have also been aligned with the Agenda for Sustainable Development until 2030* and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Therefore, Agenda 2030 represents a framework that is in line with Serbia’s national development strategy and the country’s interest protection. Gender equality is addressed under the SDG 5, but it is in practice a cross-cutting issue for all of the 17 SDGs. Regarding the sustainable forest conservation and management part of these goals, important issues are:

- Sustainable production and consumption (SDG 12), which targets also employment of the local populations, including women, to provide rational natural resources exploitation and their long-term use.
- Climate action (SDG 13) which considers differences in women and local communities, and other groups affected by climate change, as much as the knowledge and experiences they possess, that can help them adapt to the changes.
- Conserving forests and biodiversity (SDG 15), which is talking about the limiting of industrialization and urbanization processes in order to protect forests, biodiversity and preserve local communities’ ways of subsistence.

Recommendations of international mechanisms indicate the direction that reforms in Serbia should take to improve gender equality in different areas. The Concluding observations and recommendations of CEDAW Committee (Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women) are considered as a set of recommendations for Serbia, which include: the improvement of the situation of rural women in terms of access to land, education, health care, housing, employment, social security and retirement schemes, life-long training opportunities, ownership and use of land on an equal basis with men, and that they should be involved in decision-making. The CEDAW Committee considers that gender equality has not been achieved sufficiently in the case of women in rural areas of Serbia (a) Serbia missed to adopt measures, including temporary special measures, to ensure that rural women have access to education, healthcare, housing, formal employment, social security and retirement schemes, life-long training opportunities, ownership and use of land on an equal basis with men, and that they should be involved in decision-making. (b) Serbia doesn’t ensure the equal participation of rural women in decision-making, including in relation to agricultural holdings, and involve them in the design, development,

*Official Gazette of the Republic of Serbia, No. 10/2019; **Official Gazette of the Republic of Serbia for EU accession, No. 47/2018


---
**Page 108**
---

Implementation, monitoring and evaluation of all relevant plans and strategies, such as those relating to health, education, employment, retirement and social security.

(S) Serbia missed to strengthen data collection on rural women, disaggregated by age, gender and geographical area, to assess their situation in the context of the SDGs.

The NGO "Environmental Ambassadors for Sustainable Development" in their 2019 "shadow report" emphasized that Serbia is committed to all SDGs but has not adopted specific national Sustainable Development Strategy (SDSS) or National Action Plan on Gender Equality. The shadow report was presented at the UN Commission on the Status of Women in March 2019 (Government of the Republic of Serbia). The report has emphasized that SDGs are important for reaching gender equality, human rights and environmental protection which is obviously not fully understood by the Serbian government.

Another important international framework followed is provided through the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), which Serbia ratified in 2002. The four strategic objectives of the CBD Gender Policy are:

i. Integration of gender perspectives
ii. Promoting gender equality
iii. Developing capacity for integrating gender
iv. Increase the effectiveness of implementation efforts

Actions based on these objectives are related to policy, organizational, delivery, and constituency. Some of the actions have been implemented in a participatory way, but not integrated by gender as required in almost every project, yet usually with no relevant follow-ups. Project activities of the kind are mostly related to training or events, while recording of participating women is the only evidence of providing equal opportunities for men and women in terms of access to resources and services.

GCC guidelines for the promotion of gender equality

At the Climate Change Conference in Marrakech (COP21), Parties to the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) reiterated their commitment to mainstreaming gender in climate action and the UNFCCC process, providing substantial instructions in a stand-alone decision on gender. Parties agreed that the Gender Action Plan for the UNFCCC should be integrated into the Organizational Mechanism, to enhance reporting on how gender considerations are integrated in all aspects of activities.

Gender is increasingly central to the SDG's objectives and guiding principles, including through engaging women and men of all ages as stakeholders in the design, development and implementation of strategies and activities to be financed. The GCC Governing Instrument states that: "The Fund will strive to ensure that its investments contribute to poverty reduction, sustainable economic, environmental, social, economic and development co-benefits and taking a gender-sensitive approach."13

Thus, gender equality considerations should be mainstreamed into the entire project cycle to enhance the efficacy of climate change mitigation and adaptation interventions and ensure that gender co-benefits are obtained. The GCC applies to all projects, not just those funded from the budget to combat


---
**Page 109**
---

on women or to have a gender focus. Gender mainstreaming is fundamental to any project intervention and does not necessarily signify additional costs; in fact, mainstreaming gender makes climate interventions more effective and efficient.11

According to the GCF’s Gender Policy, projects submitted to the Fund are required to be aligned with national policies and priorities on gender and fit with the Fund's gender policy”. This includes a gender assessment as part of the project preparation phase, which is intended to inform project responses to the needs of women and men in view of the addressed problematic.12

### Methodology

Gender analysis was conducted during the project preparation phase to identify potentials for promoting gender equity, advancing women’s participation in project activities and to assess and mitigate gender-related risks.

In order to ensure relevance, quality and proper targeting, the gender analysis has been elaborated using a combination of a desk review of relevant national policies, legal and regulatory frameworks and studies conducted in the sector (secondary data) and direct consultations with relevant stakeholders. The latter were carried out through focus group discussions and interviews with representatives of NGOs and women from rural communities involved in forestry-related activities.

The gender assessment was followed by the development of a concrete gender action plan (GAP), intended to ensure that the project is gender-sensitive, inclusive and responsive to the needs of women and men. The GAP aims at promoting gender equality through the implementation of measures with boosting the decarbonization process, through enhancing the resilience of Serbian forests and the carbon storage potential of the country. To ensure ownership as well as proper identification of issues and priorities, a participatory approach was adopted in the development of the concrete action plan. Development of the GAP was conducted through regular consultations between the project team and various stakeholders. This project is implemented by the Forests for the Future (FFF) project, which works with rural communities in Banat (Bikinac) and Eastern Serbia (Dimbrijevac) as typical regions for Voivodina and Serbia. The FFF project has been working closely with local communities since 2015.

Questionnaires were used to assess positions and needs of the private forest women and men holders. Further in-depth analysis is planned during the inception phase using the same approach, with the intention to identify gender-related risks and opportunities for the implementation of appropriate measures. The Gender expert, with support from the FAO PAFU will oversee this engagement.

### Stakeholders consulted during the project identification and formulation:

During the preparation of this assessment and the subsequent gender action plan (GAP), the team engaged with a diverse set of stakeholders and their focal points for issues related to the social and gender dimensions of climate change. The following groups were involved: Women’s Groups, Civil Society Organizations and IVI Academia.

The feedback received was integrated into the actions and indicators included in the GAP, such as, for example, ensuring a gendered approach to updating syllabi, as well as the provision of data, contacts and documents.

# Gender Analysis

The following gender analysis should help understand relationships between men and women, their access to resources, their activities, and the constraints they face in Republic of Serbia. It is intended to provide a number of entry points, policies and identify opportunities for enhancing gender equality in respect to enhancing the resilience of forests and the carbon storage potential of Serbia.

Gender equality is not only about eliminating discrimination against women and girls, but also about building on the diverse knowledge and capacities that both men and women bring. This analysis will help identify and build on these assets so they can be used to make them more resilient to climate related shocks and risk while boosting the decarbonization process.


## Gender Equality in Serbia

Serbia is a landlocked country in Southeast Europe (West Balkans), with a socialist heritage, which is relevant for the forms of gender inequalities and position of women. There is formal equality in rights (inquiry of the Constitution, Law on Equal Treatment, Law on Women's Rights, Law on Family, Law of Serbia, and there are also Gender Equality Law and the Law on the Prohibition of Discrimination).

At first glance in the public sphere, men and women are formally equal. With more than 30% of women in the national parliament and women in the position of Prime Minister, Serbia has recently scored a high score (85) in the World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Report 2019. Women are one of the key emancipation strategies for women and for 2021 (i.e., more female than male graduated students). However, inequalities are visible in many aspects:

- The private sphere, family, parenthood, or unpaid work, which spills over into the field of paid work.
- Segregation in the education system and the labour market, where dominance female jobs are paid and less remunerated. Women dominate the informal labour market, mostly due to unpaid motherhood. Women are in general less active in the labour market and less employed, regardless of age.
- Prevalence of gender stereotypes visible in the private and public sphere, at the discursive and practical level, and individual attitudes.

The value of the Gender Equality Index for Serbia in the sphere of time is 48.7 out of 100 (European Union average 65). The Country Gender Assessment conducted by FAO during 2020-21, indicate significant gender inequalities in terms of access to land and other productive resources, economic participation, roles in and gains from agricultural production, the exercise of a range of welfare rights, political participation, access to social services, lifestyles and resilience to climate change or adaptation. Gender equality is also important for improving the well-being of women on the rural population and the position of women in rural areas, also creating opportunities for innovative solutions that can contribute to improve the economic activity of rural women in the future, and consequently their overall wellbeing.

Serbia is confronting several demographic challenges, such as emigration, rural depopulation, and demographic ageing. The current demographic trend in rural areas is within this most recent research estimated as unfavourable, characterized by continuing population decline, a rise in the average age of the population, and an increase in the proportion of elderly people.

The gender gap in terms of access to land and other productive resources is significant. Women’s share of agricultural holdings was 20% (in 1981) and 35% (in 2017). The gender inequalities were approximated more pronounced from the EBRD average (53.6, in 46.2), but those that have been made (compared to 2016) show a very significant improvement of women’s share of agricultural holdings by the main driver for gender inequalities which is education and continues later in the labour market.

# 1. Common beliefs, perceptions, and stereotypes related to gender

According to the data from the 2011 census, traditional values and norms, widespread gender stereotypes and prejudices are present in Serbia. Anti-gender discourse and misogyny are insufficiently effectively suppressed and represent some of the key challenges in Serbian society.

### Volume I: Gender equality in public life

Gender inequality is rooted in patriarchal norms that still highly prevail among Serbia's population in the following spheres: labour and employment, education, political and social participation, financial status, and income. It is also ingrained in the sphere of private life, manifested in the unequal distribution of domestic work between men and women.

### Volume II: Gender equality in public life

Gender inequality is rooted in patriarchal norms that still highly prevail among Serbia's population in the following spheres: labour and employment, education, political and social participation, financial status, and income. It is also ingrained in the sphere of private life, manifested in the unequal distribution of domestic work between men and women.

Due to these reasons, the gender equality method is still considered in the role and responsibility of the woman, which is the basic reason leading towards various forms of discrimination in Serbia. These are mostly women who leave jobs, take half-time jobs or are unemployed in order to be able to take care of their children. According to the 2011 census, 95% of those who work outside households (‘breadwinners’) are women, whereas 98% of those who work in households only are women. Also, 79% of single-parent families are of “mother and kids” type. Women own 23.7% of real estate in Serbia.

Rural women, single female headed households, women with disabilities are particularly vulnerable and disadvantaged groups due to gender stereotypes and other factors of vulnerability and social exclusion in Serbia. However, in both urban and rural areas, men demonstrate a significantly higher level of patriarchal attitudes than women1. It should be noted that, in the private sphere, both men and women tend to adhere more strongly to traditional gender roles and patriarchal values compared to those associated with the public sphere; rural women are oriented toward non-patriarchal values (e.g., equality between men and women), while urban women are oriented toward patriarchal values. Older inhabitants are more likely to adopt patriarchal values in terms of both public and private spheres, whereas younger generations tend to be more egalitarian. This attitude is more widespread, compared to the public one2. Women express a more critical attitude towards gender roles in the public sphere (education, political, and labour) than in the private one3.

### 3.2. Status of women in Serbia - legislation, institutions, and policies

The Constitution of the Republic of Serbia (1990), as amended by the Law on Amendments to the Constitution of the Republic of Serbia No. 54/2015, among other things, recognizes equality of women and men and obliges the state to pursue a policy of equal opportunities, prohibits discrimination based on sex, and guarantees the right to education for all children. The law ensures equality of spouses, equates the status of extramarital union and marriage, guarantees the freedom to decide on childbirth as well as special protection (legal, social and health) of mothers, pregnant women, and young children.

Laws and bylaws that regulate areas important for gender equality in the Republic of Serbia are numerous. The Law on Gender Equality No. 10/2004 is a comprehensive law that directly relates to gender equality and regulates the competence of institutions in this area at all levels of government. It also sets out the principles, goals, and measures necessary for achieving gender equality and women's empowerment. The achievement of gender equality is governed by an umbrella law - the Gender Equality Law, while the prohibition of discrimination on any grounds, including sex, gender identity, or sexual orientation, is regulated in other laws. The concept of gender equality as defined in the Law Prohibiting Discrimination. Gender responsive budgeting is obliged as part of the budget in accordance with the Law on Public Finance No. 10/2004.

The legal framework and public policies in the field of gender equality have been adopted, and there is a gender mechanism at all levels, including local self-governments. The new Law on Gender Equality, adopted in 2021 as Article 42, regulates the implementation of equal opportunities policies and measures to ensure that they integrate a gender perspective in the field of environment and environmental protection.

There is also progress in capacity development and gender mainstreaming processes in public policies, including the adoption of gender equality strategies. Yet, implementation programs harmonizing with the international and European standards of good practice are still lacking, leaving a legal, political, and institutional framework of gender equality, Serbia lags behind regarding de facto gender equality. Many sectoral policies are still gender blind, perpetuating inequalities between women and men.

According to Strategy for Gender Equality, the gender perspective is not embedded in the laws and regulations governing support for innovation. Women's innovative entrepreneurship support programs have been sporadic, with smaller individual allocations. Men’s majority-owned firms


---
**Page 114**
---

There is still a lack of sex disaggregated data and gender statistics in majority of sectors relevant for this project. The evidence shows that women are underrepresented in politics, economy, and entrepreneurship in general. In very few cases, some evidence exists about achievements in implementation of gender equality policies. For example, the 2019 report on the economic situation of the Republic of Serbia shows, that male-owned companies received 9 times bigger amounts of grants than female owned businesses. Yet, in some other sectors, progress is obvious; although monitoring is still lagging behind. The most important sector for women's empowerment and gender equality is agriculture. In fact, that gender sensitivity exists. For instance, law on Agriculture and Rural Development regulates the allocation of land to women farmers. This law was adopted as a part of the National Strategy for the implementation and control of the Instrument for Pre-Accession Assistance for Rural Development (IPARD) in 2013. The strategy is divided into three main axes. In particular, the second axis in the Axis I is devoted to rural economy diversification, special attention is provided to women need to upgrade primary based rural economy through introduction of tourism and processing etc. Through additional support for women farmers, they are encouraged to participate in agricultural cooperatives and are provided with the better chance to obtain financial support for their initiatives. The share of women recipients among all beneficiaries of IPARD projects has increased from 10 percent in 2013 to 15 percent among farm holders and managers (19 percent and 15 percent, respectively). Although the specific mention of gender equality is not present in the law on Agriculture and Rural Development, which has been nationalized (IPARD) it still missing. The existence of gender-sensitive rural development incentives makes timing for the project very good.

Serbian strategic plans related to gender are finally completed during 2021. The last in a series of state documents regarding gender equality is the National Strategy on Gender Equality in Serbia adopted on 4th of October 2021, the Gender Equality Strategy for the period from 2021 to 2030. The core aims overcoming of the general gender gap and achieving gender equality as a prerequisite for sustainable development and the improvement of daily lives of women and men, girls and boys in which women and men, girls and boys, as well persons of different gender identities have equal rights and opportunities for personal development and their contribution to the sustainable development of society and take equal responsibility for the future.

The general objectives of the National Strategy on Gender Equality in Serbia are:

1. Combatting all forms and promotes culture of gender equality.
2. Increased equality of women and men in various areas through the implementation of equal opportunity measures.
3. Gender mainstreaming applied in the development, implementation, and monitoring of public policies.

Competent authorities and other entities involved are obliged to consistently consider the protection of human rights, gender equality and especially the rights of the poor, elderly, children, persons with disabilities, refugees, displaced persons and other vulnerable groups.

Despite the efforts invested in promoting gender-responsive budgeting, the national budget is not yet fully gender mainstreamed. The advances are even smaller at the local level, where the process of transitioning to the new budget system is slow. The Action Plan for the period 2021-2023 is in the drafting process. On 20 May 2021, the country's parliament adopted two key laws paving the way for real gender equality in the country: The Law on Gender Equality and the changes to Law on elimination of discrimination.

Multi-year policy and programming documents take into consideration gender components and the need for a more balanced representation of women in leadership. The promotion of women’s entrepreneurship is set as a specific operational objective under the priority action). An important mechanism for gender mainstreaming is provided in addition in the National Rural Development Strategy (2018-2023) by setting a target to increase the number of women holding agricultural holdings and the empowerment of women in rural areas (especially through designing support measures). Decrease on using family labour in agriculture and increasing the share of women in agricultural activities. The strategy also sets out that associations could get incentives for projects focusing simultaneously on the purchase of raw materials from women’s cooperatives and the sale of their products to these same cooperatives. Very few incentives were given to women and 12 women's associations.

Furthermore, in 2010, the Decree on the conditions and manner of the use of incentives to support rural development through investment in agricultural holdings for the production of vegetables, industrial crops, fruits and flowers was adopted. The decree stated that women who held an agricultural holding would get 10 points more each, and 117 applications with women holders of agricultural holdings were submitted out of which 80 cases the holders of agricultural holdings were women i.e., women’s associations. A total of 70 women were awarded funds, out of which 59 holders of agricultural holdings were women or women's associations (p=12).

The Decree on the conditions and manner of the use of incentives to support rural development through investment in agricultural holdings for the production of vegetables, industrial crops, fruits and flowers was adopted. The decree stated that women who held an agricultural holding would get additional five points, so three agricultural holdings with women holders were awarded funds. Finally, the reply also stated that the government invested efforts in employing land- to-employers relations in rural areas. In 2013, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry allocated for the period 2011-2020; that the 2012 Work Plan of the Development Fund of the Republic of Serbia allocated funds for women’s associations and cooperatives to purchase agricultural machinery. The decree awarded funds to women working in tourism in rural areas. Unfortunately, the measures selected so far were not directly related to the forestry sector itself but have provided opportunity for women owners to diversify their forest economy (processing of forest products/handicrafts, agroforestry, silvopastoral activities, tourism).

### 1.3. Institutional setup and gender equality mechanisms

**Page 116**

Gender equality mechanisms have been established in the legislative and executive branches of power, at national, provincial, and local levels, and within the independent bodies, such as the Ombudsman and the Commissioner for the Protection of Equality.[1]

Within the Government of Serbia, the mandate for agriculture and rural development is assigned to the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management and following the establishment of a new Government in 2020, the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management is responsible for public administration affairs including organizational structure, human resources management, and budgeting within the sector of equality and forestry. The Ministry for Rural Welfare has a mandate to monitor welfare in rural areas, and it proposes measures and policies for improving the life and work situation of the rural population and preserving the cultural heritage of Serbia.

The highest level of the gender mechanism relevant for the project exists within the National Assembly of the Republic of Serbia (NARS). Two of its committees are of importance for gender issues in rural areas: the Committee on Human Rights, Women's Rights and Gender Equality and the Committee for Human and Minority Rights and Gender Equality. Which are both functioning as regular NARS working bodies. These committees check expenditures and monitor the implementation of Government decisions, including those of laws, and other general acts under the responsibility of ministries, state agencies and bodies).

The Coordination Body for Gender Equality (CBGE) provides horizontal and vertical coordination of the mechanisms at all levels of government. The CBGE is composed of a number of focal points that are assigned duties to advise about gender equality and mainstream gender in the work of each ministry. Vertical coordination includes local gender equality mechanisms in towns and municipalities.

The Government recognized in 2020, has also established the Ministry for Human and Minority Rights and Social Inclusion, which is responsible for public administration around human and minority rights, anti-discrimination, and gender equality.

At the level of the Autonomous Province (AP) of Vojvodina, a Committee for Agriculture and a Committee for Gender Equality are active in the AP Vojvodina Assembly, while in the provincial executive body, the Government of the AP of Vojvodina, the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management, and the mandate for gender equality to the Secretary of State for Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management. The Secretary of State has a Deputy for gender equality. The independent institution of Protector of Citizens, or Ombudsman, and of the Commissioner for the Protection of Equality, are also vital for gender equality and protection against discrimination and an essential condition for sustainable development.

According to the Gender Equality Law, local governments are eligible in principle local gender equality mechanisms. Thus, the Local Gender Equality Network (LGEN) consists of representatives of local gender equality mechanisms which role is to provide support to local governments to improve gender equality (SCTM, 2020). "A key factor recognized for the success of the gender mechanism" is that it should be a continuous process and not an isolated event. The LGEN is a network and all relevant actors are fully involved in these processes. Public institutions lack a systematic approach to gender equality and the implementation of gender equality mechanisms, so it is essential to secure consistent budget funding for institutions and organizations, rather than relying


---
**Page 117**
---

on ad-hoc project-based financing. This project will create an opportunity to expand discussions on gender equality in forestry beyond the existing network to include the business sector, focusing on reducing gender gaps in the field and making it more appealing to young women and men in rural areas.

The project will work to provide interactive and long-lasting links between the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management, PE "Serbia Shume" and "Vojvodina Shume" and gender focal points, as well as the focal points in local administrations (officers-in-charge of women's and gender issues) at national level. The project will also provide a platform for continuous further provision of gender data to support further evidence-based inclusion policies upgrade on the national and local levels.

March 2021 marked a beginning of the second phase of a three-year EU funded project "Support to Priority Action Plan on Gender Equality, phase II" (EU project) that supports the Government of the Republic of Serbia in effective implementation of the EU Gender Equality Acquis, adoption and implementation of gender equality policies and measures. The project will support the development of key mechanisms on national and local levels, and gender mainstreaming in policies and EU funds programming, implementation and monitoring. Furthermore, the project will advance the position of women in decision-making processes at all levels of government and civil society organizations, including environmental and forestry sectors. The UN Women Office in Serbia implements this project, in close cooperation with the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management, the Ministry of European Integration, the EU Delegation in Serbia, and other partner institutions, including non-governmental organizations.

Following the new Action Plan on Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment in External Action 2015-2020 (UNFPA) adopted by the EU at the end of 2020 to provide a systemic basis for gender-responsive EU support to Serbia, Gender Country Profile has been developed. The document is providing an overview of the current situation and challenges related to women's empowerment in the GAP III, including promoting economic and social rights and empowering girls and women; promoting equal participation and leadership; and Climate change and environment and digitalization.

UN Women supported the Statistical Office of the Republic of Serbia to finalize, design and print the publication "Women and Man in Serbia" offering data for the following areas: Population, Health, Social protection, Education, Employment, Income, Housing, Wage and Pension, Property, Land use, Judiciary, Decision-making, and International indices. In addition, a draft Gender Equality Index for Serbia has been developed jointly with the Statistical Office, the Social Inclusion and Poverty Reduction Strategy Unit and the Ministry of Agriculture.

Consultations during preparation of the GAP showed that partner institutions on the local level e.g., local administration (municipalities) have limited capacities to deal with gender issues. Typically, these have experience with one or more small scale ad hoc projects which offered information and inclusion of women in decision-making processes. The project will support the development of active CSO sector implemented. Most of these activities were considering economic empowerment of women in rural areas. The project will also aim at raising awareness among local administration and raising awareness of the public about the local champions of small businesses such as tourism, processing and marketing.


---
**Page 118**
---

Academic institutions through their individual researchers were involved with few projects doing research about women and their engagement in forestry, tourism, agriculture, handicrafts, rural development etc. As institutions they respond to legal obligations to possess documents (plans) related to gender issues, but no specific activities are recorded as a follow-up.

Institutions on the national level fulfill legal requirements to create gender-related policy, but no actions are taken to ensure that women’s voices and interests are heard in the sector they are responsible for. The Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management have rural development measures in their budget, but there is no specific funding allocated to these measures. There are no mechanisms which apply for financial support, yet no further actions are taken to promote these opportunities in the field. This was the task of the Rural development network, which is not operative in the sense of providing information and promoting gender equality.

Majority of CSOs working in related areas such as forestry, rural development, environment, hunting, nature protection has no experience with gender issues if not engaged with projects financed from international funds. The inclusion of a gender dimension or gender norms was obligatory at least through provision of women participation in trainings or measures on impact of conducted activities on women.

### 1.4. Gender equality among the target group

The project will bring benefits to the whole of Serbia, although its activities besides work with the administration is targeting primarily rural areas[2] in which the majority of its interventions will occur. Stakeholders involved are local communities and their representatives, as well as public institutions and areas dealing with agriculture and forestry which represent the fundamental of local economies, while agriculture and forestry lands are the main natural resource local communities base their livelihoods on. Therefore, the project is targeting women who work in these sectors or whose husbands or managers, the main target group of this project. Rural areas of Serbia are not the space that can boast of gender equality. Women’s participation in decision-making processes at all levels is very low and held in the hierarchy for the businesses, or resources management - leadership or simple representation of women in the board of directors of companies, which are active in agriculture sector in Serbia, but also the feature of the overall community from which they are spilled over or reflected in the economic sector. Role of women and men in the rural communities of Serbia differ, creating a need for further analysis of the gender equality which follows.

### Education

Out of 127,463 literates over the age of 10 in Serbia (98% of the total population after the 2011 Census), 58,632 are women (46.1%). In 2012, the total number of women and men were equal. There are gender inequalities in education, evidenced in: higher literacy rates among rural women compared with both rural men and urban women; lower levels of educational attainment for rural women than their male counterparts; and gender segregation by field of study, reflected in the higher concentration of women in social sciences and humanities, as well as in health and care service careers (where employment opportunities are often limited to part-time or temporary work), while men dominate in fields of study such as engineering, technology, a and information and communication technology.[3] Boys are

[3] Ibid

more numerous in the fields of education: electrical engineering, mechanical engineering and mechatronics, geodetics and construction, transport, forestry and woodworking, and geology and mining1.

In the general population, 97.7% of boys and girls attended high school. 88.9% of the total available girls were in secondary education. Child labour in rural areas jeopardizes children’s educational attainment. The proportion of girls who complete secondary education is lower than that for boys due to traditional attitudes that men should manage farms and boys’ early engagement in work is considered a natural part of growing up2. In 2016, the proportion of women completing high school was 58% compared with 73% of men (42%). Among those who complete general secondary education (high school), there are more girls (58%) than boys (42%). The proportion of boys to girls graduating from three-year secondary vocational schools is lower in the field of services.

Girls enrol and graduate colleges and universities more often than boys (57% of students and 59% of graduates). Among those who had a doctorate there were 57% of girls compared to 43% of boys. Among all university graduates, women are overrepresented in the fields of education, health sciences, and Informatics and Communication Technologies (ICT) and engineering, manufacturing, and civil engineering (63%). In the field of services, the shares of women and men are equal (50%). In 2016, more women than men graduated from higher education institutions with a degree in humanities or academic fields; however, men are more represented in engineering, manufacturing and construction and, in services (68%).

Men are overrepresented among members of the Serbian Academy of Science and Arts (SANU). In 2016, over 90% of all members were men. There is not a single woman in the Department of Social Sciences.

Among those employed in the field of R&D, 48%, women are dominant with a 57% share. Almost the same proportion of men and women work as teachers (32%). Women dominate in social sciences, almost 60%, and their share is smallest in engineering and technology, about 37%. In the majority of fields, there are more women than men among university professors; however, there are more men among computer users. The majority of internet users are also women1.

The educational structure of the rural population is less favourable than the urban population (a higher share of persons with low qualifications and a lower share of persons with tertiary education), and the gender gap in education is larger for rural women than for rural men or urban women, yet smaller among men. Rural women have the highest rate of illiteracy found among rural women compared with both rural men and urban women. The gender gap in education is largest between older generations. There are significant gender differences with respect to secondary school and higher education completion rates. Women who complete secondary education are more likely than men to complete tertiary education – formal and non-formal is extremely low, except when working as teachers or employed in a state institution, education, or administration where additional education and on-the-job training are obligatory. The gender gap in education completion is largest for women with lower levels of education listed above. The Project should be able to provide trainings in locations accessible to majority of stakeholders and/or a transport for all interested stakeholders with needs.

Education and training in the field of forestry There are 4 career opportunities in forestry education in Serbia: (a) higher education for foresters at BSc level; (b) higher education for forest engineers at BSc level; (c) higher education for forest engineering at MSc level; or (d) a higher education for a doctorate in biological sciences – PhD. level. # Serbia Gender Equality Strategy for the period 2011-2030

## Strategy for the development of education and upbring in the Republic of Serbia until 2030 in its general and specific goals does not deal with gender issues related to the number of boys and girls, and later their participation in the labor market. The situation is the same with the Action Plan for the period 2011-2020 of the Strategy for the development of education and upbring in the Republic of Serbia until 2030.

Gender imbalance is obviously highly expressed in the information and computer technology sector, which represents the best prospects for progress both economically and in terms of participation in creating jobs. Women make up only about one-third of employees in this sector, while men dominate it. In the ICT sector, which makes up two thirds of the employees in the entire sector, in the narrower field of information and communication technology (ICT), women's share is even lower: 18%. The proportion of women in ICT is 36%. It is estimated that less than 20% of women are in managerial positions, while less than 10% of women are in the leadership position of a company director.

### Economic participation, employment

Per FAO's National gender profile of agriculture and rural households Serbia (2001) aging index (the ratio of the number of people aged 65 years or more to the youngest age group aged 0-4 years old) is 3.82:3, with significant differences between women and men (3.60:2.12, respectively). The unemployment and inactivity rates are considerably higher among "non-urban" women (17.9%), and 45.4%, compared to the corresponding male rates of 10% and 28%. Women's share of employment is 43.6%, while men's share is 52.8%. Unemployment is most prominent among the subgroups per gender and age among younger women aged 15 to 24: the unemployment rate is 39.5%. While men


---
**Page 121**
---

of the same age group have a slightly better indicators, they are in no better position, for men between 15 to 24 is 32.2%. There are twice as many self-employed among men than women (in this age group 15 to 64, 28% of men and 13% of women are self-employed). The share of informally employed women and men is larger among self-employed persons than those employed in all age groups. The largest number of informally employed are aged 25 to 29 (87.4%) women and 30-34 years old (86.1%). In the age group 55 to 64, this share is lower for both men (38.2%) and women (38.2%). The largest gender gap considering inactivity occurs in the age group 55 to 64, where the inactivity rate of women is more than twice as high compared with that of men (19% vs. 7.8%). The share of registered employees among both sexes increased, with the growth of registered employment being more pronounced among women—about 93 thousand or 10.3% compared to about 70 thousand or 4.5% in 2016.

The gender gap in employment is more pronounced in rural areas than in urban areas. There are limited employment opportunities outside agriculture for women living in rural areas and agricultural work accounts for a large share of their total working time. The majority of women engaged in paid work usually takes place in family holdings, where women are seldom registered as holder or manager, and more often in the role of unpaid family worker. Rural women are less likely than urban women to find employment in medium- and high-skilled occupations, such as professionals, technicians, and elementary occupations. In comparison with men living in rural areas, women are less likely to be employed in firms and in manual jobs, and more likely to be employed in unskilled and elementary occupations.

The activity rate of women in the labour market was 46.5% and for 15-15.5 percentage points lower than the male activity rate (62%) in 2020 in the working age population older than 15 years. The female employment rate was 38%, which is 9.7 percentage points higher compared with the male employment rate (28.3%). The female unemployment rate was 5.4% and by 0.7 percentage points, was higher than the male unemployment rate of 4.7%.

The gender gap for gas in 2016 was 8.7%, which means that women were paid 8.7% less than men. Serbia is among countries with the lowest gender pay gap in Europe. If earnings are observed according to educational attainment or occupation, the difference in earnings between women and men is even more pronounced. The gender pay gap for university graduates was 14%. The proportion of women with high wages in the total number of employed women (24.8%) is higher than the share of men with low wages among all male employees (30%). This is due to the fact that women, on average, live longer than men; it is noticeable that in all categories of old-age pension beneficiaries, women live, on average, shorter than men.

Women work daily 4 hours less than men and unpaid and spend on average 42 minutes less time on paid work than men. Women spend more time (50%) on household chores—18 hours and 18 minutes more time on unpaid household work than men. Their total work hours are longer: 55% of women and 77% of men participate daily in unpaid jobs. There are differences between urban and rural areas, with


---
**Page 122**
---

rural women spending more time on unpaid household work than any other category? Taking into consideration the average time spent on unpaid care activities: UN Women in Serbia 2020 have estimated the value of unpaid care activities at 65.1% of GDP while 34.6% of GDP is unpaid work of women and 4.7% of men.

There are significant regional disparities in the indicators on women farm holders/managers and women working in farming (higher share of women was recorded in medium and large-sized holdings in the Vojvodina region, where women are younger, better educated and, as a result, more likely to own their farms). The highest share of women farmers is found in the Eastern Serbia regions due to its higher share of women across all indicators, which is likely to be related to negative demographic trends and women’s longer lifespan. (Life expectancy in Serbia for the year 2020 was 71.4 years for men and 80.3 years for women). The highest share of women farm holders/managers in the Serbian region are underrepresented among farm holders and managers, probably due to the higher share of small farms.

The COVID-19 pandemic has had a significant impact on rural women. The most severely affected sectors were wholesale and retail trade, accommodation, transport, food and beverages, service activities, forestry and logging, and crop and animal production. In these sectors, over 700,000 workers are employed in Serbia, of which more than half (54%) are women. Women working in these sectors include informal employment, short-term contracts, and working in micro-enterprises which are particularly vulnerable to the pandemic. The most significant changes that have occurred during the COVID-19 pandemic were related to the closure of schools and kindergartens. Women living in non-agricultural sectors have been significantly affected by this crisis. Women living in rural areas and women with children under 6 years old were more likely than men to be unemployed, as well as women from urban areas and men from both types of settlements. In addition, many rural women and rural men have had to leave their jobs and stay at home to take care of children.

During the state of emergency, rural women have also faced many challenges concerning maintaining value chain activities in agriculture and food production (e.g., lack of access to agricultural workforce, and selling their products). Some innovative solutions were used, such as marketing online, but these were not always successful. Women who had access to land and could get new loans or other investments required to get more rural households on board could do so, obtaining direct financial support and loans offered were insufficient - rural women need additional support to fully recover from this crisis.

Division of labour among young men and women

Young women are limited to households and farms. They also often sell agricultural products on local markets. Sometimes within agriculture products one can also find wild fruits or edible greens, while mushrooms and medicinal plants in the form of herbal mixtures are more often offered by men. Women who have access to land and livestock are more likely to be involved in animal husbandry (rarely another woman; it is more typical for women returnees from urban areas). Although many rural women drive vehicles, they rarely use their family cars to go alone into the forest, or elsewhere. Of course, older women can also be found driving vehicles and going out on their own, but in groups which are side by side, however, thanks to the history of their inhabitants, can differ in habits. instance, villages from which families worked abroad, have more emancipated women population which use vehicles and agriculture machinery regularly, while a neighbouring traditional village will have even women going to locally organised training for women producer groups on milk quality accompanied by men.
