In the period 2015-2019 the share of informal employment of women of working age was 15.6%, (the share of informal employment of men was 15.8%), which is a result of higher growth of formal employment of women (19.4%) in that period than the growth of formal employment men (11.3%). The structure of informal employment is also different from that of formal employment. Informally employed women work as assisting household members, and informally employed men are predominantly self-employed. The majority of these people are economically vulnerable, as they not only do not exercise their formal employment rights (as do other informally employed) but are not paid for their work.

Contributing household members or unpaid family workers are a specific category in the workforce. They are not considered as formal form of business work, and without a labour contract, salary or social benefits based on labour status. Rural women working on family farms form a significant proportion of this group. These women do not receive any remuneration for their work but may make social contributions themselves (for example, pension, and disability insurance). However, if the farm is not registered as an enterprise, then these women are not legally required to pay social contributions. Even on registered farms, informal workers often do not get any salary or remuneration and therefore do not exercise their welfare rights based on employment.

Longitudinal data from the Labour Force Survey indicate a steady increase in the number of unpaid family workers. In 2015 there were 93,800 women and 47,600 men working as unpaid family members in this employment status, while in 2019 there were 95,200 women and 37,900 man. The majority of unpaid family workers are located in rural areas, working on family farms - 91,400 women and 32,600 men in 2019. Their share is the total number of employed persons is 7 percent for women and 2 percent for men.


---
**Page 124**
---

During the first half of the 1990s, the economic position of farmers improved relatively, compared to the lower strata of manual workers (unskilled and semi-skilled), as a result of less elastic demand for agricultural products. The fact that farmers are no longer at the very bottom of the material position hierarchy has persisted to this day (with a further significant reduction in their numbers and an increase in their income). Women made up the majority of employees in administrative services (60%), among professionals and artists (59%), in service and trade occupations (57%), among engineers, professional associates and technicians (54%), and in elementary occupations (53%). Men were in the majority among artisans (83%), machine operators (78%), executives (directors), officials and legislators (67%) and farmers (58%).

There are differences between rural and urban women in terms of the type of occupation they perform. Urban women are less likely than employed women in urban areas to work as professionals, artists, engineers, technicians, associate professionals, and clerical support workers, and more likely to be employed as administrative services employees, service and sales workers, and unskilled workers in elementary occupations. Relative to rural men, rural women are more likely to be employed as professionals, engineers, clerical support workers, service and sales workers, and in elementary occupations.

In the process of implementation and monitoring of measures, activities and interventions in employment policy, a gender-sensitive approach will be applied and strive to achieve gender-balanced results. Based on the principle that women are not only victims but also agents of gender responsive budgeting, which implies equal inclusion of unemployed women and men in measures including gender-balanced opportunities in the employment process, will continue to be applied.

Political participation: decision-making and leadership

Serbia has made the greatest progress in the subdomain of political power, in comparison with its progress in other domains and when compared with other countries that are members of the EU. In 2013, the ratio of women holding positions of legal representation was 30 percent among women (and since 2011, 40 percent) for women in Serbian National Assembly and AP Vojvodina Assembly. Women hold 57 seats out of a total of 250 members of the Serbian National Assembly and 89 seats out of a total of 163 members of the AP Vojvodina Assembly. The current National Assembly of the Republic of Serbia consists of 98 women (39.2%) and 152 men (60.8%). The current AP Vojvodina Assembly consists of 47 women (29.1%) and 106 men (70.9%). The Serbian National Assembly has 20 committees headed by 5 women (25%) and 15 men (75%). Among the deputy chairmen of the board are ten women and nine men, and among the secretaries of the board are 16 (88%) women. On the board of the AP Vojvodina Assembly there are five women and four men; in other words, one out of every two members of the board is a woman. Of the Five Deputy Prime Ministers, two are women (40%). Out of a total of 23 ministries, 18 have female ministers and 5 male ministers. The number of women in political parties reflects their engagement with political parties and their associated bodies. Membership in political parties is, however, not gendered. Women can be members of any party regardless of the fact that their bodies are not disaggregated by gender; they are often out of date or unavailable on the parties’ websites. According to the social stratification survey conducted by the Institute for Sociological


---
**Page 125**
---

Research (ISR in 2018): The share of rural women’s party affiliation is lower than that of women living in urban areas (6.5 percent and 10.5 percent, respectively).

Both rural and urban men are more politically engaged — 11.8 percent of men in urban areas and 13.7 percent of men in rural areas are affiliated to political parties, out of which 6 percent are active members. Looking at specific forms of political participation over the last three years (for instance, petitions or demonstrations), we can see that in the urban population, both women and men are more likely to engage in these forms of political participation than their rural counterparts. Only 10.5 percent of rural women have signed some form of appeal or petition which is significantly lower than in urban Serbia. Only 1.5 percent of rural women engaged in direct protests in public (boulevards less than urban), while rural men were more likely to engage in this type of political participation.

Looking at the share of women elected as presidents of municipalities or mayors, there is a substantial gender gap; only 6.6 percent of women hold these positions. The share of women as municipal presidents/equivalent holds 6.81%, and 31.2% of councillors in local government bodies are women.

Environmental authorities provide equal opportunities for women and men to participate in the natural resources management, but women are underrepresented in decision-making roles within the environment. The Ministry in charge of environmental protection implements gender mainstreaming during the planning, management and implementation of plans, projects, and policies for environmental protection.


| Leadership of women in local governance/political systems and formal/informal institutions |
| --- |

Gender Equality at the institutional level was shown by the Research of the Commissioner for the Protection of Equality from 2019. In 150 self-government units in Serbia, there are 12 women (8.4%) among the 143 local government unit presidents and mayors. This shows a substantial gender gap. The 13.2 percent of women are in the position of the president of the Municipal City Assembly. The data from this research shows that there are only 12.7 percent of women on local community boards, and 8.4% of women on the executive board. Women still have a long way to go to reach managerial positions; research data show that men make up 83.7 percent of directors of public companies in Serbia. The results from the Research of the Commissioner for the Protection of Equality still think that managerial positions are intended for men and that the best role for a woman is to be a housewife, while only 10% of towns and municipalities, 33 percent of town and municipalities have not enforced the statutory quota.

Considering the small share of women in local community bodies, rural women experience far fewer opportunities to improve their lives in accordance with the needs of the communities in which they live. According to the research, the patriarchal values typical of rural communities represent a significant barrier to more active participation by women in public life. The main reasons for this lack of involvement by women included: the absence of family and community support; the ingrained opinion in rural areas that a woman’s place is at home; a disregard for women’s opinions and proposals, and the heavy combined burden of work on the farm, household-work and caring for children or other relatives.


---
**Page 126**
---

There is a considerable opportunity to promote women leadership. Achieving the results of various projects supporting entrepreneurial activities of rural women, especially of the existing cooperatives and associations women initiatives in the last two decades. During 2015, GIZ regional campaign “Empowered women, empowered economy” (EM) it was noticed that rural women united in women entrepreneurship cooperatives and associations were more successful than those who worked individually. Their work finished then average. However, these initiatives failed to expand on local and regional communities. The reason for this is the lack of support from the government and other institutions. Their achievements meaning that leaders of these initiatives no matter of achieved results or increased capacities are considered local community leaders.

An example of good practice concerning the leadership of women in politics is the establishment of the Women’s Parliamentary Network in the National Assembly of the Republic of Serbia. It was founded in 2013 by the decision of all women deputies, and still exists today. The Women’s Parliamentary Network has been working on the adoption of laws that are important for women and managed to negotiate their adoption with the Government of Serbia. A good social example is also the Women’s Parliament in Kosovo. It was established in 2013 by a group of women who wanted to include women voices through dialogue, within the women's movement and with decision-makers. It seeks to include and reinforce the voices of women from vulnerable groups primarily through cooperation and dialogue with other organizations and institutions. The first Women’s Parliament in Kosovo which gathered women from Serbia and the region for the first time in 2000 to talk about what kind of future they want - what public policies, what institutions, what services, what everyday life.

Women's and men's participation in household and community level decision:

Rural women in Serbia are much more oriented to the local community compared with their mobility is lower. They are often engaged on work that is related to agriculture, animal husbandry and their primary role is taking care for children and household, food processing. They are often engaged on work in stables or orchards. Women who have a higher level of education (especially those who have graduated) usually leave high school and many young. If they manage to educate further, they often leave their villages and move to town, except if there are suburban or close to some industrial centre when they combine working with the household work. The women who stay in rural areas are mostly engaged as they being their primary farm production on lands in vicinity of their households, contributing to their dependency from the land. Women’s participation is also very low in decision-making at the community level (particularly in case of milk and pig production in stables which is often combined with off-farm employment). The women who are engaged in forest activities have a higher level of autonomy on land (arable and grasslands) as regular, which is leading to their economic degradation (transformation in coppices). Forest exploitation is also oriented towards closest stands and those ones easily accessible due to existing infrastructure.

The main use of private forests in rural areas (and as a consequence degradation faster) is the use of wood in the industry and for the heating. This activity is based on mechanization and physical strength and that is why it is mostly done by men. Women are more engaged in forest activities related with the formal owners of the land. Because of the role in the household and gender roles, they have lower levels of the autonomy and influence in decision-making at the household/and community level.

In some projects in Serbia is already noticed that women with knowledge (technologists), information (available for them) and skills (to use these technologies and information) are more successful than men. Women who are engaged on activities related to animal husbandry, horticulture, viticulture, or other forms of adding value to primary products have much more favourable position in


---
**Page 127**
---

# Making Decisions

Also, men informed about the importance of the role of women in value chains (for instance, quality of food products, food safety, promotion, and marketing skills) start reducing their resistance to include women members of their families in decision-making[6]. In the public sector women with more skills and higher education do not necessarily benefit in getting more attention and opportunities to participate in decision-making. The reason is that women are underrepresented. Therefore, the introduced rule of having more than 30% of women taking decision-making positions in the Government does not necessarily respond to women's needs for equal access to such a role.

# Women's Decision-Making

Women's decisions and actions are often influenced by cultural traditions as well as reflected in their participation in church-affiliated and other religious organizations and associations, in events related to handicrafts, and in humanitarian actions and similar forms of civic activity. Women do express a readiness for decision-making but they are not always given the opportunity to make such decisions usually made by rural women are linked to the daily routine in households. If large investments are made in rural areas, it is important that women's voices be heard because women more than men hold the reins of household and community life; rarely women are asked for the opinion. Community decisions are under the control of men who have a greater say in decision-making processes. Consequently restricting women's active participation in household and community level decisions rather culture then economic or political (patriarchal culture as described above).

In local self-governments, during the planning, management and implementation of plans, projects and environmental awareness campaigns, women are underrepresented. This is the result of much stronger environmental awareness and involvement in environmental organization of women than men. Recent years have seen a significant increase in the number of women involved in environmental work increased synergy in communities under threat, mobilizing both men and women. Yet men were those who were traditionally expected to defend physically their properties and interests; so, activism is in vulnerable position. Women's involvement in environmental organizations has a more peaceful character than recent events. The project should go further in empowering women and all other members of the community to participate actively in decision-making processes related to natural resources management, forestry, and rural development policies. For this purpose, ecological movement and women's councils could be used as a driving force for the project activities and a vehicle for getting on-board rural communities.

# The Context of the Project

## Situation of Women and Men in Rural Areas and the Forestry Sector

According to the Gender Equity Strategy for the period 2013-2024, the economic position of women in the Serbian countryside (rural area) is unfavorable due to lack of chances for sustainable employment and less personal ownership of resources (land, arable land, and real estate). In addition, there is limited access to information on rights and opportunities. Women are also underrepresented in the labor market (care and nursing services for children, the sick and the elderly), public transport, information on rights and opportunities. The lack of women's participation in decision-making processes would create opportunities for sustainable employment, self-employment, cooperatives, and social entrepreneurship. Current support for women in agriculture and rural development is insufficient and needs to be improved. Women are also underrepresented in the forestry sector which affects the quality of everyday life in rural areas. All measures proposed to reduce the gender gap in the economy should especially include women from all age groups in the countryside.

The share of women farm holders in Serbia in general is comparable to that found in countries with similar cultural, economic, and social heritage. According to the Farm Structure Survey (FSS) in Serbia, in 2018, women farm holders accounted for 19 percent of total farm holders and women farm managers for 35 percent of the overall total. There has been a slight increase in the share of women farm holders since 2012, but this is not significant enough to be considered an increasing trend. It should also be noted that this increase is primarily related to the depopulation of villages and traditional inheritance patterns in which the land often passed to the eldest female member. There is no data on female ownership over forests in Serbia.

According to the study conducted by the project FEMFOREST in 2021, in the forestry sector (generally defined as covering forestry, sawmilling, wood processing and furniture manufacture, as well as pulp and paper production), women are underrepresented. The vast majority of employees work for forest administrations and state-owned forest companies. There are no exact data on share of female workers in these organizations. However, the results presented by the project FEMFOREST show that the forestry sector seems to be more attractive to women. Data presented in this study give an indication of the number of women employed in the forestry sector in Serbia and meet, with an insight into the type of jobs and the qualifications of employees. This analysis will also inform the integration of relevant modules in the syllabus prepared within the project framework.

### Women in forest administration

The share of female employees in administrations is 36% out of 48 female employees in the forest administration are trained foresters, and it seems common that women hold leadership positions only at lower levels. The vast majority of women (90%) work for the Forest Directorate in the Forest Directorate have professional forestry education. Almost all of them (14 out of 16) are engineering graduates and 2 have master's degree. Around 1/3 (24%) of employees in the section of forest administration are women. All of them are foresters.

### Women in forest decision-making

Women are heads of the Department for forestry policy and implementation of measures for forestry improvement, as well as heads of the sections for forest protection, forest management, and forest plantations. Apart from these positions, there were no other management positions where women had been in the previous 10 years. In addition to management positions, women (with professional forestry education) are also heads of the section for forest inspection, head of the section for research and analytical data in the field of forestry; monitoring, the implementation of forestry policy measures, as well as heads of sections for forest protection activities in the field of forest reproductive material, republic forestry, and hunting inspection.

### Women in the public forest user sector

In State Forest companies only 20% of women (trained foresters) work, while in both public and private companies they represent a smaller share in the total number of regular employees in Public Enterprise (PE) Serbia Shume and PE Vojvodina Shume is 3.15%, and 4.67%, of which women are 699 and 239 respectively. In the State Forest Company, women represent only 0.8% of all employees, of whom 10 hold PhD degree, while MS degree, has 6 and 10 employees and BSc/graduate forest engineer degree 127 and 22. Forest technicians were 50 and 19 in PE Serbia Shume, 22% of the total number of regular employees, and 27% who have a professional education in forestry (37% are graduate foresters).

Currently, women with professional forestry education are heads of department, head of grade of forest administration, head of production department, head of department, head of foreign trade and marketing department, head of forest management and forest planning management

Nevertheless, the share of women managers is higher than average in relation to holdings specialized in certain types of primary production, such as poultry, sheep, goats, cereals, oleaginous and protein crops, root crops, arable crops, flowers and decorative plants, mushrooms, grapes, nuts, and fruits; and this is in line with traditional forms of gender segregation in performing economic activities on family farms. Women in nature conservation and forests-related services, research, and education

Trained female foresters are more likely than men to be employed as teachers in forestry schools, pedagogues, and nature conservation. This fact might imply that women cope with the lack of job opportunities in traditional forestry by shifting to other subsectors. The women’s participation in forest pedagogy (employed as teachers) is 50% higher compared to men (data from the Faculty of Forestry of the University of Belgrade; share of women with doctorates in forest sciences is also considerable). Given the high number of female students, it can be assumed that the proportion of female graduates will remain female. The forest sector in the Balkan countries is seen as significantly potent in terms of self-employment and small-scale entrepreneurship, which are more attractive for women than traditional jobs in rural areas. Research in Serbia shows that 12,300 self-employed workers in rural areas were engaged in firewood production in private forests. This is important, as these workers are not reflected in national statistics on employment.

For female working in nature conservation is more attractive than in timber production. The share of women in a total number of employees in National Parks (NP) PE "NP Kopaonik”42 / 130, NP “NP Bereg”57/98, NP “NP Đerdap”60/100, NP “NP Šumadija”10/100. In the total number of employees are women, and 14% of them have a professional education in forestry (50% are graduate forest engineers). Currently, 1 woman with [professional forestry education] holds a management position (head of the section for improvement and protection) at NP “NP Kopaonik”. In PE "NP Bereg", 28% of the total number of employees are women. In the total number of employees in PE "NP Bereg" is 1 woman with professional forestry education who holds a management position (head of the section for improvement and protection). In the last 10 years, women with [professional forestry education] did not hold any management position.

In Public Enterprise “National Park Kopaonik”, 13% of the total number of employees are women. However, none of them have a professional forestry education in the last 50 years; a woman (with professional forestry education) has held a management position, working as head of the section for improvement and protection at NP “NP Kopaonik”.[21] In PE "NP Bereg", 38% of women hold positions. Currently, 4 women with [professional forestry education] hold management positions (head of the section for improvement and protection), and 27% of them have a professional education in forestry (70% are graduate forest engineers). Currently, 1 woman holds a position as assistant director for planning, protection and development and head of private forests section. In the last 10 years, 4 women with [professional forestry education] held management positions (assistant director for planning, protection and development and head of private forests section), 27% are nursery manager, and head of landscape planning section.[22]

Available data show that women in public enterprises and municipalities in forestry sectors play greater roles in Environmental NGOs and civil society groups and may lead entrepreneurial associations. However, there are no official statistics on private forest organizations (POIGAs) in Serbia, which are also registered as NGOs. Thus, the number of members, the share of women, forest area, etc., remain unknown.

2.2. Gender and climate change in Serbia

Men’s and women’s vulnerability and capacity to adapt to climate change

Women have lower resilience than men due to their limited adaptive capacities to respond to climate change and related extreme events such as floods, droughts, or other extreme weather events. Due to their lower preparedness to act in emergency situations, a lack of relevant resource, and weaker decision-making power, women are more vulnerable to the negative impacts of climate change and disaster events. At the same time, measures to decrease greenhouse gas emissions and reduce carbon footprints often have a disproportionate impact on women because they spend more time outdoors and they more frequently belong to groups that are particularly vulnerable to such processes. For example, women are more often the holders of smaller farms or businesses; on average, as farm holders, they are older than men (50 years compared with 42) and have a lower level of education. Women also face greater climate-related risks due to their work in open fields. Recent policy initiatives at a national level have started to integrate gender perspectives into the development of adaptation strategies.

One of the facts supporting the conclusion that women will express even more vulnerability to any change including climate change-related events, is the fact that rural depopulation in Serbia is not solely related to low birth rates but also to migration trends which women are more susceptible to. Namely, the share of female population among the total population aged 15–64 years is higher than for men, and the same trend is observable in the younger population (20 to 35 years) compared with the population older than 65 years. Women also have a lower level of education and are less represented in the working age population in their reproductive years in rural Serbia and have further aggravated depopulation trends (Cimmarusti for Refugees and Migrants, 2014).

Gender inequalities that may be exacerbated by climate change impacts

Because of women’s limited access to land, credit, education, and other forms of climate change adaptation resources, women in general, and rural women in particular, are more vulnerable to the negative impacts of climate change. Women also have less access to information on climate change and resources, as is the case with agriculture. In addition, women are mostly excluded from the decision-making process, which means that their needs and priorities are not adequately considered when policies designed to combat climate change increase the population’s resilience to climate change.

In the last twenty years, Serbia has been hit by five major floods and five major droughts that have occurred alternately. Both floods and droughts occurred during the main agricultural season, significantly affecting crop production and livestock breeding. The 2014 flood was particularly severe, which caused great economic losses to many farms and agriculture as a whole. In addition, the floods caused great damage, destroying infrastructure such as roads, bridges, and buildings in rural areas; and flash floods were recorded. The biggest direct negative impact of these natural disasters has been on plant production. Livestock production was most often indirectly negatively affected—multiplied by the lack of feed for animals and the destruction of livestock breeding facilities—and livestock production had a negative impact on food production, resulting in irregularities in the raw materials supply.

All of this has affected the socio-economic status of the population, whose livelihood depends on natural resources and agriculture. Although production and farm holders are prevalently men, women own about a quarter of small farms with a standard output of up to €2,000, who are particularly vulnerable due to their limited access to land, credit, education, and other forms of climate change adaptation resources and natural resources. Seasonal workers in the agriculture sector are also among the most vulnerable

# Gender inequalities in real estate ownership are pronounced: women are much less likely than men to own land or houses. In rural areas, where households are more dispersed and geographically isolated, women face additional challenges.

## Various socio-economic factors contribute to increasing the vulnerability of the population and national economy to climate change.
- **Vulnerability** is influenced by various factors such as age, gender, income level, education, health status, and access to resources. These factors interact with each other in complex ways, leading to different levels of vulnerability among individuals.

## The global process of transformation into a more climatic and climate-adapted society, as well as all other overviews on the subject, are changing almost every day.
- **The most vulnerable** groups include very young, very old, women with serious medical problems, poorly educated, without the possibility of retraining and/or migration, and those who depend heavily on natural resources for their livelihoods. These groups face significant challenges in adapting to climate change.

## The impact of climate change on rural areas is primarily reflected in lower income due to a drop in agricultural production.
- **Water availability** has decreased significantly, leading to increased health risks among the most vulnerable populations, who are often dependent on agriculture for their livelihoods. These groups include elderly people with chronic illnesses and those living in remote or infrastructure-poor areas.

## Job losses occur primarily in sectors linked to fossil fuels, and in small and medium-sized enterprises.
- **Forestry** and other natural resource-based industries are expected to be impacted by climate change.

## Floods and heat waves can have particularly negative effects on so-called socially isolated population groups.
- These groups include the elderly, patients with chronic illnesses, and those living in remote or infrastructure-poor areas. Climate change exacerbates existing inequalities and achieves results. For instance, floods in 2014 in Serbia and other disasters in the last 5–10 years (including COVID-19 pandemic) showed significant gender-based differences in impact on women and men.

## Gender issues have been identified as a challenge for disaster risk reduction.
- The response between men and women in affected communities has become a significant issue. Since floods in 2014, DDR policy and strategic frameworks have been changed to include measures for gender-responsive disaster risk management.

2.3. Role women and men are anticipated to play in the context of the project

The project is designed to follow relevant Serbian national legal and policy frames, as well as international good practices in achieving gender equality in the execution of all planned activities. However, it also proposes to implement recommendations from the United Nations Convention on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)123, which have expressed their concern that rural women are often excluded from decision-making processes and lack equal access to land ownership on an equal basis with men, while their participation in decision-making is limited.

Gender inequality in the rural economy was also concluded earlier in the Strategy for Agriculture and Rural Development of the Republic of Serbia 2014-2024. Among women there is less participation of active persons in agriculture and forestry, as well as in other economic activities. From the perspective of regional differences, it is noticed that in Vojvodina the participation of unemployed persons in agricultural production is higher than in other regions. Women’s social and economic position is better, since less than men are employed in the non-agricultural sector, less engaged in agriculture and forestry, and more involved in household activities. The situation for women in rural areas is much more unfavourable according to all indicators in South and East Serbia, where gender differences are particularly strong in all segments of the labour market.

Engagement of women in forestry in Serbia remains limited by the nature of households, particularly in rural settlements, where women’s participation in economic activities has not been developed. The responsibility carried by rural women in various forest value chains is limited to processing part of NWFP, while their role in harvesting and cutting wood is minimal. Women are more involved in collecting mushrooms; it is considered that women should avoid going alone into forests due to risks of attack by humans, while animal attacks are not identified as relevant. Hunting and cutting of wood are also almost completely reserved for men. The same applies to the collection of honey, which is considered a male activity. It is considered that women should avoid going alone into forests due to risks of attack by humans, while animal attacks are not identified as relevant. Hunting and cutting of wood are also almost completely reserved for men. The same applies to the collection of honey, which is considered a male activity.

In the context of an aging population and gender disaggregated labor division, rural women, particularly those living alone or with dependent children, are more exposed to energy poverty due to their poor access to equipment to self-procure fuelwood from forests and therefore are more vulnerable in terms of health. As forests in the proximity of rural settlements become overexploited, fuelwood becomes more expensive, and women are more and more dependent on men to acquire such vital resources.

The project assessed that within rural communities, families, especially those headed by single female-headed households, assuring access to high quality fuelwood is an important way to contribute

2.4. Resources that women and men have access to

Women and men have equal legal access to all resources: economic, financial, physical, natural, and other assets. How much of this access is in practice, as well as who manages or controls access to these resources, varies significantly between women and men.

The gender gap in the economy of the Republic of Serbia is more visible through the unequal position of women and men in the labour market, differences in earnings, pensions and income in general, differences in access to credit and other financial services, differences in social protection, self-employment, participation in the informal economy, differences in ownership over real estate and land as well as participation in decision-making in the economy. In each of these observed elements,

women are still in a far more unfavorable position, while in the group of women there are particularly vulnerable groups of women whose economic situation is even more unfavorable. [10]

Declaratively, prevailing gender gap in education and in the labour market, the knowledge and empowerment of women in the field of green technologies and innovations is priority for gender equality that are not addressed in Serbia. Most activities in the field of encouraging women's entrepreneurship and employment are carried out by non-governmental organizations (NGOs) with support measures (national and PARD). Women's organizations and women's informal groups do fund raising addressing, traditionally primarily with the violence against women. Work on economic empowerment of women is also carried out through various projects and programs implemented in rural areas in limited in time and budgets, although often appear in various forms of calls for grants in diverse thematic areas. The majority of these calls are not open to all applicants; their offer is not consistent, neither long term, nor that beneficiaries can plan their actions in a long term.

Yet, the legislative framework is formulated in such a way as to enable equitable access for all beneficiaries, with low eligibility thresholds and requirements, especially when it comes to accessing local and regional funds. The most significant measure is the possibility of registering agricultural holdings registered to women specifically through the following measures: boosting economic activities on farms; providing financial support for the purchase of farm equipment and machinery in rural areas; and providing loan support schemes and incentives for the preparation and implementation of projects aimed at improving the competitiveness of small and medium-sized enterprises. This is reflected in the higher number of points awarded to women applicants when ranking potential recipients of subsidies, as well as in a lower interest rate on loans. Nevertheless, the available data provide no evidence that these measures are effective in facilitating the economic empowerment of women, particularly given that the share of women among agricultural holdings registered to women is 45% and their share in total funding for rural development is 32.3%, which is above their average share among farm holders and managers (19% and 15 % respectively).

This indicates that women are actively engaged in achieving compliance with the eligibility criteria for financial support, but it does not mean that the average amount of incentives per holding is higher for holdings registered to women than for holdings registered to men. The advantages (extra points) given to women applicants when ranking potential recipients of subsidies do not necessarily lead them to register their holdings; however, there is no evidence to support this assumption. Measures aimed at boosting economic activities on farms and providing financial support for the purchase of farm equipment and machinery are intended to boost the development of the tourism sector in rural areas as well as to review one of its subsectors – arts and crafts, i.e., cottage industries. Although these activities can also affect women's entrepreneurship, there are no data to illustrate this effect, while direct support for supporting forestry does not exist.

Annual reports on the state of small and medium enterprises, entrepreneurship, and competitiveness for the period from 2015 to 2020 do not show data on women's entrepreneurship, so there is no data on how these measures are implemented in practice. The report also provides no information about progress report on implementation of the Strategy for Small and Medium Enterprises and its pillar number 6: "Promotion of Women's Entrepreneurship". There are still no systematic gender statistics on women's entrepreneurship.[14]


---
**Page 136**
---

Some progress has been made as a result of the gender responsive budgeting process in the Republic Statistical Office, with the introduction of new gender indicators in business statistics. Progress has also been made with the Public Procurement Office, which since 2020 has started monitoring the participation of women entrepreneurs and small and medium enterprises (SMEs) in public framework agreements through public procurement procedures on an annual basis. The Public Procurement Office’s Performance Report for 2020 states that this percentage was 23%, which is 8% more than the estimate.[157]

Although most of the difficulties faced by entrepreneurs are common to both sexes, there is a particular impact on women entrepreneurs due to sector choice, gender discrimination and stereotyping, lack of access to finance, limited business skills and experience, lack of networking opportunities or work responsibilities, or differences in attitudes of women and men towards entrepreneurship. Women face more challenges than men when it comes to starting their own businesses because they have less access to resources such as land ownership, financial services, and networks.

2.5. Specific needs of women and response strategies to their vulnerabilities

Women have lower resilience and often lower adaptation capacities to the impacts of climate change and related disasters compared with men due to their greater exposure to these events. Due to their lower preparedness to act in emergency situations, a lack of information, a lack of relevant resources, and weaker social networks, women are more vulnerable than men when it comes to adapting to climate change and disaster events. At the same time, measures to decrease greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and reduce environmental pollution can have a more significant impact on rural women than men because they tend to be more involved in activities that contribute to GHG emissions. For example, if they run farms in fertile land, rural women are often the holders of smaller farms. The same applies when it comes to adaptation measures such as water management and irrigation. We tend to be at greater risk of poverty; and we are also more exposed to weather-related risks due to their work in primary production.

Recent policy initiatives at the national level have started to integrate gender equality into climate change policies and strategies. The project will try to assist with gender mainstreaming of related policies to respond to recommendations of the CEDAW Committee in 2019 and will provide incentives for the implementation of gender-sensitive measures, such as land tenure reform, access to land ownership, its sustainable use, employment in forestry sector and models for obtaining economic security. Recognizing women’s role in agriculture is crucial in order to increase their share in rural development projects. The project will also support the inclusion of women for provision of knowledge and information for starting entrepreneurial initiatives on the available land resources by starting primary production, adding value activities or developing new products.

In the course of the preparation of this assessment, both men and women were involved in expressing their needs. Interested stakeholders, private and civil society stakeholders which were engaged primarily focused on pairing finance with the technical support, which is fitting with the experience of the experts who have worked in the field for many years. Numerous development projects were usually dissolved—either capacity building or investment support was provided, generating limited results with no follow-up after the projects were finished.

In all training and investments, when possible, the project will give higher priority to women[17] owning degraded coppice stands or lands for conversion to forest or bioenergy plantation and will ensure that at least 30% of beneficiaries are women; yet, the project will not make specific window for women, since experience shows that women can be easily excluded from such projects. The project will also encourage strategic action and support of whole local community. If just concentrated on the economic benefits of women, it could lead to a situation where women would be seen as objects of development rather than actors in their own right. It is important to note that novelties in resources management should not be brought by women, or to be more precise innovations in forest and bioenergy plantations should not be introduced in such a way that they become a threat by patriarchal society. This is even emphasized with the fact that large percent of women responding positively to initiatives as those which the project will promote, are those which are either single or widowed and live alone in their communities being heir of land and properties in families which had no male offspring.

Engagement with women and women-focused organizations is a key factor for understanding the factors of the Project. The Project will identify challenges and barriers that they face. At project inception, women and women-focused organizations will be included in consultations to ensure that perspectives, needs and challenges are considered by the Project and in adaptation management. The project will also provide training on gender equality and empowerment of women and women’s rights for women organizations as those which have no gender earners, considering that in situation in which women organization is not represented at all levels of decision making, it is difficult to achieve social, economic and environmental sector (even stigmatized for being established to make a mess in social sphere).

2.6. Opportunities to challenge gender stereotypes and increase positive gender relations

Recent discussion over the implementation of Nature Based Solutions, by the ADAPT project of the IUCN, come out with the notion that one of the key gender issues in environmental protection is that benefits from NBS are not equally shared between men and women. The costs and risks are shared to the whole community. This is coming from the fact that a very limited number of ecosystem services are utilized and reduced to quantifiable amounts of products.

In the forestry sector this is driving directly to timber and further to hard wood work only man is capable of doing, while women are left with the responsibility for the management of trees, in which the majority of even this work I mechanised, so men do actually use machinery for this work and not their hands. The same applies to bioenergy plantations where women are left with the task with mechanisation and heavy machines which with modernisation do not anymore require physical strength. In addition, women are responsible for the management of forests in rural areas while male work is harder and therefore more important. If provided with the training how to use machines and financial support to purchase them, this could not just improve women’s economic activities and increase their income but also allow them to manage their resources without being exposed to enticement of money, which will decrease benefits (it’s common that women are offered money for selling their forest products). The same applies in the case when they also safety issues engage man outside of their families to manage their forests, which is often resulting in floods; if they are married in majority of cases their husbands make all decisions and manage forests alone.

The project should provide inputs for final including forestry missions in Rural Development programme of Serbia, while also providing set of rules to help women get advantage in obtaining


---
**Page 138**
---

financial support for improving their economic position and participation in sectors of green and circular economy. Namely, the National Agriculture and Rural Development Strategy of Serbia for the 2014-2024 defines two strategic goals very important for forestry:

* Sustainable resources management
* Environmental protection and efficient public policy management and institutional framework improvement for agricultural and rural areas development.

The present institutional capacity for supporting rural development is so modest that specific forestry measures are not included in the National Agriculture and Rural Development measures which are active, supporting improvement of competitiveness also have space for forestry if property upgraded, as much as those of environmental kind, rural economy diversification, and upgrading of life quality in rural areas. The strategy foresees support to forest owners and implementation of special conditions for providing advantage to women initiatives as already provided in some measures for women farmers dealing with agriculture (see page 40).

Rural development support to forestry might have two kinds of windows:

| Direct support to forestry through forest resources: |
| --- |
| investments in afforestation |
| investments in machinery and equipment for primary forest production |
| investments in forest infrastructure |
| investments in biomass-based energy material production (processing of wooden materials into paper, wood pellets) |
| investments in strengthening other wood processing |
| investments in wild game farming (at the moment it's financed form the Fund for Hunting Economy) |
| etc. |

Indirect support to forestry supporting local community benefits through investments in strengthening food production competitiveness through investments in storing and processing of NAPFs:

* diversification of rural economy through introduction of tourism related also to forest ecosystems and supporting non-consumptive activities (tourist accommodation, catering, infrastructure)
* diversification of rural economy through introduction or strengthening food supply chains to tourism (investments in processing equipment for NWFP or gastronomy)


---
**Page 139**
---

- support to HNVF and agroforestry systems for sharing benefits between: age-diversity conservation, nature restoration in nature protected areas dispensable of pastoral activities, maintaining forests, and providing quality inputs for tourist activities & food production.
etc.

Women's land owners will be invited, and incentives will be provided for raising quality forests and forest production systems with establishing full value chains and partnerships – horizontal and vertical, and making them operative. Opportunity to restore their forests including conversion of degraded agricultural lands into bioenergy plantations, will increase value of the lands owned by women, making them less vulnerable socially and economically. Consequently, their confidence and welfare will be increased. Women who are able to access markets for their products, and who have acquired additional knowledge and skills provided, they can establish or strengthen value chains for products they produce. The project will provide training in marketing and business management. Financial incentives are provided for adding value to these products; women will be able to overcome the problem of lack of capital. Women will also be supported with access to loans. There are more options for income generation in their own environment, without even moving from their farms.

Opportunity for planning and investing in decentralization of private sector companies, in particular through investing (by investing in the forest ecosystems) women may use to provide assured supply of raw materials for production of bioenergy products. This will also contribute to the opportunities surrounding their value chain which on the end will provide double benefit - building a strong reputation of a responsible entrepreneur and (environmental) leader of their communities.

To mitigate risk of not benefiting fully from these anticipated outcomes of the project for rural women, it is important any potential barriers to access information, lack of financial intermediation and preventing lack of information. The project will build on experience gained from the implementation of previous projects in Serbia. It will provide training in business management and co-financing agricultural production and access to favourable credit lines (subsidized loans), women and persons under the age of 40 years are on Serbia granted preferential treatment for obtaining loans and lower interest rates. The project will also provide financial support for women entrepreneurs, people after the printed version of Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management (MOAFWM) publication "Women in agriculture" and the publication "Women in rural development", published by the Rural Development Offices Network (used to have continuous campaigns in all municipalities of Serbia).

An opportunity for rural communities to improve their access to information are represented by diverse communication channels. The project will build on experience gained from women's forest owner's organisations and other branch organisations such as beekeeping, organic producers, etc. which are members of the Rural Development Network of Serbia is not anymore handy, support to the new networking of the kind within the framework of the project. This will contribute to better access to information and quality deriving from isolation. The synergy with interest of the MOAFWM in agriculture and rural development with the forestry sector, could be expanded to ministry in charge of environment, tourism, etc., which are also interested because agriculture extension work poor and other ministries do not even have such an extension.

Lack of access to transportation also disempowers rural women; they are less likely to have a driving license or access to a car. The project will provide training for women on how to use public transport is unsatisfactory due to an insufficiently developed network of bus routes (limited frequency of buses, etc.).

# Inconsistent schedules, poor connectivity to the nearest cities, and long distances to bus stations (40)

This is the shortage which prevents women employment off-farm as much as access to information, financial and legal aids, etc. Creating more jobs through diversification of on-farm and off-farm local jobs through agriculture and adjacent services establishment in rural communities is one of the top contributions of the project. The project will also contribute towards the economic empowerment of rural women and vulnerable communities from remote areas (mountain and other marginal) or with the limited financial resources to access formal credit, as well as those living in disaster-prone areas exposed to various threats including those deriving from climate change such as natural disasters etc.).

Policy and programming documents pertaining to the development of Serbia’s agricultural sector and rural areas take into consideration the gender composition and the need for the economic empowerment of women. The project is aligned with the Government of Serbia’s (GoS) 2015-2023 priority action plan pertaining to the Improvement of the Social Structure and Strengthening Social Care System in Rural Areas. However, despite the fact that the GoS has allocated funds invested in promoting gender responsive budgeting, the national budget is not yet fully gender mainstreamed. The project will contribute towards the implementation of the new budget system as well as to the new budgeting system is slow.[1] In this respect, the project might contribute to a large extent to the gender mainstreaming of the existing budget intended for cooperatives from the portfolio of Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MoARD) in Serbia. The law on Budget System, national and local policies is gender-responsive budgeting is stipulated by the Law on Budget System.

## SERVICES AND TECHNOLOGIES PROVIDED BY THE PROJECT

It is expected that the transferred technologies and interventions conducted will have impact on mitigation of risks of extreme climate-related disasters and climate change in the whole Serbia, which will have a positive effect on the economic empowerment of women. The project will contribute to security of their household to decrease the risks they face due with climate change and microclimatic conditions. It is expected that the technologies and interventions and services provided by the project will be available and accessible to both women and men. The project will benefit from the existing gender mainstreaming in the national budget system, which provides funding vulnerable communities depending on fuelwood for energy, individual forest and agricultural landowners (farmers) and private companies interested in derelicting their business and in decreasing the risks of climate change and extreme weather conditions. The project will give higher priority to women[2] owning degraded coppice stands or lands for conversion to forest or biomass production. This is because the majority of these women are from rural areas, whose income originates from the average participation of women in the various sectors addressed by the project.[3]

The project will provide knowledge through introduction of the up-to-date technologies related to afforestation, sustainable forest management and forest’s ecosystem services (e.g. biomass for energy) and support investments into climate adaptive biodiversity practices[4] and increasing the


---
**Page 141**
---

forest cover, including by establishing shelterbelts/windbreaks[10] in agricultural lands and nature-based solutions for disaster risk reduction and soil quality improvements (agroforestry and agrotechnology technologies).

Specifically, the following services will be provided on the national level to the MOAFWM and other responsible ministries without specific concern about the gender, since the administration have reached consensus that women are not only the main users of forest resources but also the beneficiaries. Women will be involved in all stages of the process including public meetings and consultations. Their participation will be ensured through a dedicated mechanism through permanent communication with the team preparing these meetings and consultations.

All documents as much as materials following capacity building actions conducted in administration to be developed will be available on the internet, but since access to digital means of communication is not equally distributed among all rural communities, and especially among women who are not members often do not have opportunity to use them, project will provide regular bulletins and info sheet which will be disseminated through a wide range of channels including radio stations, community partners, chambers, agriculture extension services, public and private enterprises and municipal authorities. This action will be preceded by encouraging MOAFWM to re-establish support to the Serbian Forest Service (SFS) in order to ensure more efficient and timely distribution of information about the support measures available for development in rural areas.

Stakeholders will be regularly informed about the service project has provided within the strengthened forest management system, including through a National Monitoring, Reporting and Verification System. National strategy and action plans will be developed with the extensive participation of representation of all relevant stakeholder groups through a national steering committee. The information on the progress in implementation of the project as well documents will be published and shared in wider population. Addresses for posting will be selected to ensure that they are accessible by women, especially those living in rural areas (agricultural land) and cadastre (forest land), namely, bulletin will be mailed to women landowners as much as possible. A similar effort will need to expand the address book during the span of the project to include more women.

The programming of the rural development in MOAFWM should finally foresee forestry which will require establishment of the working-group capable of translating interest of the sector into suitable measures and projects. The importance of forest products for food security, income generation and strengthening forest products value chains should be introduced with all suitable measures of all areas. This is especially important because women are the main users of forest resources and of immense importance for shaping new conditions which will provide a positive discrimination for the future project applications deriving from women, youth, and disabled. Revisions will be needed in uses how to improve the use of forest products (e.g., wood fuel, charcoal, firewood, etc.) in agroforestry and silvopastoral systems, maintenance and revitalisation of traditional farming and introduction of new species for food security and income generation. This is especially important when dealing with the processing of non-wood forest products to grow mushroom, medicinal plants, wild fruits and greens, game etc.).

The positive impact of this project for energy security and biodiversity will include consultations with women on the selection of species for afforestation and reforestation of forest ecosystems in general (climate adaptive forestry seedlings which also produce fruits which could be locally processed


---
**Page 142**
---

or used through women entrepreneurship). Consultation with women should be organized for identifying main problems with land degradation and land use in villages and their surroundings and options within the project to address them by selecting economically most suitable solutions and modeling actions which will mostly tackle land that is regularly used and maintained by women for production purposes. Women who are involved in such activities have benefit of the well-maintained countryside (fruits and vegetable production and tourism).

The national standard for biomass production / handling and use will be based on available international standards and adapted to local conditions. The project will also contribute to the context using expertise of women and men working in the business sector and civil society in Serbia.

Design of Execution guidelines for wood energy plantations and Guidelines for decision makers on LULUCF to prevent soil degradation will reflect needs and specific problems in different rural regions of Serbia. Both documents will be developed by a team consisting of representatives from the Serbian Forest Investment and from the work of Soil department in the MOFMN. Execution guidelines for wood energy plantations will be presented at workshops organized with participation of interested persons under 40 using local registries. New national curriculum will be promoted in a Bulletin, and schools through a campaign for scholars which are entering high schools and faculties through media and lectures. The project will also contribute to the context by providing training on how to use acquired knowledge: for CAS will be shared through the same system with the addition of the network of Chambers of commerce and business.

The important question is what should be used for fire-breaks planned activities should occur not just with the business sector, but to include women in households despite do they possess or not forests or agricultural land. Getting them on board should be done through a wide national campaign in both rural and urban areas. The campaign will be organized by the project team and will be presented in a way to promote territory specific and relevant options instead of general solutions which are not applicable for all regions. For example, it is possible to use wood from plantations established in plains of Vojvodina, use of biogas produced from domestic animals encrustments in zones with developed livestock production (e.g., cattle, sheep, pigs, poultry and other livestock zones etc.). Also, solutions for different scales of use should be presented (households, industries).

Services provided by the project should reach diaspora and land and forest owners in urban areas, which are presently not informed about possibilities to properly manage their resources. Since the phenomenon of abandonment is very common among these groups, specific services and mechanisms are needed to be created to activate their potentials as much as to protect further degradation of forests they own (legal frames and incentives).

The project will transfer to Serbia the knowledge and technology and CA3 practices needed to reduce the climate change impact and increase the profit of public and private forest stakeholders, as well as to enable the forest sector to become more resilient to climate change and contribute effectively and efficiently to the national adaptation strategy. The project will also support the development of a network of private forests will be under CJS4/FSM. Project will support all women initiatives, since priority for support are women and single women households. Selected civil society organizations will be engaged to provide technical advice to private landowners willing to do afforestation or other interventions planned within the project.

Enabling and strengthening Rural development can be considered as a key factor for mobilizing private sector in forest management. Institutional capacity building of such a network will in future represent a challenge and innovative approach to the mobilization of private actors in getting involved in decarbonization processes, which might provide more certainty for synergistic activities in

#### 4.1 Assessment of risks related to SEAP

No such risks were identified during preparation of the GAP. Civil society and public enterprises representatives also did not

# 4.2 Mitigating risks and preventing SEAH

The possibility for SEAH to appear is not specific to the project but might appear generally in any other human-wildlife conflict situation. Addressing its threat would be useful for all stakeholders and local communities involved in project to prevent SEAH. Therefore, the project will work to strengthen all involved parties to be able to deal with Protection from Sexual Exploitation and Abuse (PSEA).

The stakeholders engaged on SEAH safeguards within the project will be the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management, PE "Serbia Shumë" and "Vojvodina Shumë" gender focal points and the local administration. The project will also engage with other relevant institutions such as the project PMU, which will have a gender and social experts. Both will work with relevant institutions (e.g., UNFPA, UNWOMEN, UNEVOC, OCHS) to develop specific PSEA and SEAH trainings for extending messaging on prevention of SEAH. The project will also engage with the ESS specialist point in PMU in contact with focal points in local administrations.

The constant coordination will be kept between the project gender and social experts, the National Gender Committee (NGC), and the Social Coordinator in FAO. The social expert will be in charge of keeping work with relevant gender-based violence organizations or networks while gender focal points in local administration will keep working with relevant women's organizations MOA/HRM having institutional stakeholders that are to project stakeholders and communities.

Preventing SEAH is envisaged through continuous work with women through the duration of the project – more specifically, stakeholder consultations prior and during project implementation will include awareness raising on SEAH related risks and mitigation measures. The project will ensure regular visits to communities and local institutions. The Gender and Social expert will be in charge of keeping work with relevant gender-based violence members on SEAH safeguards. The Gender and Social expert of the project will support local officials in campaigns on prevention of SEAH. Changes will be identified to, where applicable, act as allies on SEAH issues. A list of key stakeholders and contacts for each community will be developed including SEAH GRM established by the project will be provided to project stakeholders and communities.

Stakeholder consultations prior and during project implementation will include awareness raising and stakeholder-differentiated understanding of SEAH related risks and mitigation measures. The Grievance Redress Mechanism provides an accessible and inclusive survivor-centred and gender-responsive approach for reporting incidents of sexual exploitation, abuse, harassment, and other forms of reporting with safe and ethical documentation of such cases, which indicate when and where to report incidents, and the process for handling them.

The Project will ensure that all concerns and/or incidents will be reported to the ESS specialist and the FAO Office of the Inspector General, as appropriate. The Project PMU will also have a gender specialist with PSEA expertise.


| 44 |

The Project Coordinator of the PMU will be responsible for documenting, monitoring and reporting on any grievances received and how they were addressed. AE is going to continuously monitor and report on SEAR cases to the responsible Governmental body in the MoSA/PMU and if needed provide required information to the relevant authorities. All this should be done in a transparent manner while keeping the confidentiality as required by the relevant policies and regulations in Republic of Serbia.

# Part II: Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Project-specific GAP provided below should ensure gender mainstreaming in this GCF project.

It mirrors the logical framework of the project and is an integral part of project/program design. It provides guidelines on priority actions within the logical framework, as well as those to be taken into account during implementation, monitoring and evaluation phases of the project.

The project aims to upgrade Senegal's male-dominated agro-value chain and ensure diversification business by fostering inclusive and gender-responsive approaches that ensure no one is left behind. By promoting women’s participation in developing innovation business models, the project will help create sustainable and equitable opportunities for all, contributing to both climate resilience and gender equality.

Despite any laws, what is the prevalence of child marriage in the country?

National statistics

1% of girls in Serbia are married before their 18th birthday and 8% after their 18th birthday. Statistics show a higher percentage in rural areas in both cases: Urban (0.7%) and 4.6%, Rural 2.3% and 14%.

What is the income level of the country?

World Bank ranking (M, L, M-L)

Serbia is an upper-middle-income country

Where does the country rank on global gender indices?

World Bank Other

Compared to EU Member States, the United Kingdom, and other countries in the region, Serbia ranks 21st, between Croatia and North Macedonia. Compared to Croatia, Serbia has lower values on the index in the sub-domain of representation and participation (58.3 vs. 60.7). Compared to North Macedonia, it has a higher value for the sub-domain of aggregation and quality of work (82.3 versus 61.4). Compared to other countries in the region, Serbia has lower values on the sub-domain of representation and participation (57.9 vs. 68.2), but significantly lower in the sub-domain of representation and participation (17.0 vs. 68.2).

Is there a national action plan on GBV and/or harassment?

National government

Yes, Serbia is currently executing the National Strategy for the Prevention and Combating of Gender-Based Violence against Women and Domestic Violence 2013-2025.

Does the country have specific services for survivors of GBV (at both the national and local level including women's)

Local gov / NGO

Yes, there are at least 10 organizations financed by both the state and international donors that are active in providing services for survivors of GBV [at both the national and local level] including psycho-social support, legal aid, and medical facilities and facilities which provide psycho-social support.

Are women concentrated in lower paid roles and mostly line-managed or supervised by men?
AE Yes. In Serbia, women's wages are lower than men's even in professions where women make up the majority of the workforce, such as in the health care and welfare system. The wage gap between women and men is 8-9%.

AE No

Are worksites or project employment based in remote locations?  
Will project workers live in their community or in worker housing in worker housing, in remote areas?
Will workers be required to work long and potentially under challenging conditions at times of day when transport options are limited?
Will the project operate in highly pressurized work environments, with high seasonal deadlines?
Is the project located within a male-dominated sector?  
Have communities, especially income/vulnerable
### AE
Worksites will be in remote forest and rural areas of the country. Nonetheless, as workers will be selected from communities within a radius of 25 km from the worksite, these will not require the establishment of camps or other temporary accommodation structures.
As workers will be selected from communities within a radius of 25 km from the worksite, these will not require the establishment of camps or other temporary accommodation structures.
Workers will be selected from communities within a radius of 25 km from the worksite. Based on the criteria identified in the PEF, the work must be accessible by road. Furthermore, transport from collection points in such areas to markets will be guaranteed by the project through its partners and service providers.
The project will work with tight seasonal deadlines, but it will not be in highly pressurized work environments.
Forestry is a sector where women employment does not go above 14%. Nonetheless, employment will be open and accessible to all without any gender restriction.
Participation in the project is voluntary and involves both state owned forests and forest owned by municipalities and private operators.

# ANNEX X - PSEA RISK MITIGATION MATRIX

## Description of (Potential) Risks | Likelihood (L/M/H) | Potential Impact (L/M/H) | Risk Mitigation Measures

| National Level Risks |
|---|
| Lack of a legal system to enforce laws |
| Low levels of prosecution of SEAH incidents |

### Contextual Risks
- **Ensure presence in the PMU of a gender and social expert with extensive experience in PSEA.**
- **Ensure constant coordination between the project gender and social expert, the relevant focal points at the national level (e.g., UNDP, FAO).**
- **Work with relevant gender/civilian affairs/airline government ministries and departments, other anti-gender-based violence organizations or networks.**
- **Bring enforcement of the Alt SEAH (and/or its equivalent) law.**
- **Adaptation of the Alt SEAH (and/or its equivalent) to local context.**
- **Leverage institutional stakeholders with providers of SEAH training (e.g., UNFPA, IOM, etc.) and other relevant actors in the country.**

### Societal Risks
- **Sociocultural norms that do not challenge SEAH:**
  - Low levels of awareness on rights, stigma, etc.
  - Limited services for SEAH survivors

#### Project Risks
| Low rates of access to justice |
|---|
| High rates of femicide or sexual violence (e.g., used as leverage in conflict situations) |
| Women fear that participation or employment in the project/program may exacerbate ongoing forms of SEAH. |

### Project Risks
- **Limited SEAH protection services in project locations:**
  - Implement a gender and social expert Gender and Social expert of the project, support local officials in campaigns on prevention of SEAH.
  - Leverage institutional stakeholders, identify champions / supporters / changemakers within the government (specifically on SEAH).
  - Conduct SEAH awareness raising and sensitization campaigns within the community.
  - Inform the community the community on SEAH risks, explain how to report them and the services available including SEAH DRM established by the project.
